## Introduction {#ocallaghan.wtd.introduction}

We live in a world that is full of words. They pour out endlessly from the minds and hearts and mouths of nearly ten billion human beings inhabiting the planet, and, for all we know, from those of many other living beings as well. They come from the past, they resound in the present, they reach out toward the future. Some words are banal and everyday, some are repetitive and boring, some are descriptive and pragmatic, some are powerful and world-changing. They inspire us, they instruct us, they console us, they convince us, they challenge us, they effect change in our lives; they are performative.

Yet a fundamental question should be asked: Are the words pronounced and affirmations made in such abundance actually _true?_ Do they correspond to reality? Some statements made may be false or tendentious, some may be partial or superficial, or trite, or banal, or imaginative, or platitudinous, or commonplace, or entertaining. As we listen to people, as we read, as we observe, we inevitably ask whether or not what is being said is actually true. If it is not, then it does not challenge us, it does not really concern us, we do not pay much attention to it, we let it pass us by. But if it _is_ true, then it hits home, it is meaningful, it obliges us, it changes us, all of us, it is hard, it is undeniable. Some years ago, the _New York Times_ made a point of insisting on the importance of truth in journalism, saying that truth is hard, is hidden, must be pursued, is hard to hear, is rarely simple, is not so obvious, is necessary, cannot be glossed over, has no agenda, cannot be manufactured, doesn’t take sides, isn’t red or blue, is hard to accept, pulls no punches, is powerful, is under attack, is worth defending, requires taking a stand, is more important now than ever before, etc. For some reason, according to the title of a recent work on communications, “Truth is more fascinating than fiction.”[^intro-1]

But a further question arises: Could it not happen that a particular emphasis on or search for truth, especially in a religious context, might end up as a form of destructive fundamentalism? Might truth not end up crushing people, forcing them to act against their will, or against natural law? If truth does in fact exist (as the author of this book holds it does), would it not be better to leave aside

[^intro-1]: I.H. Smith, _Well Documented: The Essential Documentaries that Prove the Truth is more Fascinating than Fiction_ (London: White Lion, 2022).

[p. 2]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p2}

our search for it, _pro bono pacis_, for the sake of peace, of tolerance, of respect, of charity, letting each and every one search for their “own” truth? Or perhaps it might be possible to find a path to truth which not only respects the life and individuality of humans, but one that actually contributes to providing them with their ultimate identity and happiness, forming the basis of trust, solidarity, and credibility in relationships, institutions, and societies.

This book deals with one of the ways in which we establish the truthfulness of human affirmations: _witnessing_, testimony. Many, if not most, affirmations can be tangibly documented and experienced, and we can verify their truthfulness easily and _directly_ on an empirical basis: by counting, measuring, calibrating, checking, confronting. We may denominate this _scientific truth._ However, the truth value of other affirmations may be harder to ascertain, because our knowledge of the reality in question is more indirect. Ofen we have to work off the affirmations of people whom we simply trust. Perhaps the affirmations in question are generic and approximative, not easy to check. This might be called _anthropological truth_, and is common in humanistic disciplines such as philosophy, psychology, sociology, etc.

But where access to truth statements becomes most arduous is to be found in the area of religious discourse, that is, _theological truth_. By definition divinities are other-worldly, they are intangible, they are not under our control. The statements we make regarding the divine and their possible revelations may be plausible, inspiring, interesting, beautiful, and appealing. . . . History shows us that people, on the whole, pay attention to religious statements and lifestyles, recognizing in them valid manifestations of the sacred; all civilizations have done so. In principle, it is taken for granted that they correspond to reality, that they are true and valid, that they should be respected. Were they not, they should be rejected out of hand. But are they? How can we ascertain their truthfulness? We know, to an important degree, what religious systems _say_, what the _hold_ as truth. But the ultimate question is not what they say or affirm, but rather whether or not they are _true_ and are in a position to bring about our conversion and commitment and identity. In the words of Avery Dulles, “words can be used to convey information, but mere information does not convert.”[^intro-2]

Augustine in the year 390 ad wrote an important work entitled _De vera religione_, “On the true religion.” He did not deal with religion in general but rather asked the question if religion, in this case Christianity, is true. Which would mean of course that other religions might well be limited or even false. Early Christians openly opposed the religious veneration of human authorities, for example, the

[^intro-2]: A. Dulles, “Fundamental Theology and the Dynamics of Conversion,” _Te Tomist_ 45, no. 2 (1981): 175–93, 186.

[p. 3]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p3}

_Introduction_

Roman Emperor; neither did they reverence pagan divinities as their compatriots did. And it was not just a question of taste. The reason, to their mind, was simple: the Roman Emperor is no more a divinity than we humans are, and therefore on no account should he be venerated as such; besides, the pagan gods simply do not exist, for there is only one God, and all the rest, including angels, are creatures. Believers were convinced this was true, and many of them in the early years of Christianity paid the price for it with their lives. They were called _martyrs_. The Greek term _martiria_ is usually translated as “witness.” Christians communicated to those who believed the conviction that they were “in the truth” (Jn 17:17) in respect of the divinity _by putting their lives on the line_. Obviously, they explained revealed truth, they thought it out in the context of the philosophy of the time (principally Platonism and Stoicism), they expressed it in their lives and virtues, especially in their charitable dealings with others, in detachment from material goods and living a chaste lifestyle, they celebrated it with all the beauty they were capable of in their Eucharistic liturgies. Yet where they really made a point was in their _witness_ to truth, not simply in their words and explanations, but in their silent, unequivocal proclamation of the truth. Witnessing, truth, and evangelization went hand in hand for early Christians, who put their lives on the line, and the same applies to our own times. Thus the subtitle of this work.

Of course the truths of faith, deriving ultimately from divine revelation and received in faith, developed and were expressed over a long period of time. And this happened not only on the basis of shared rational argument but also on the basis of the witnessing process. Revelation takes place, Vatican II tells us, _gestis verbisque,_ “through gestures (events) and words.”[^intro-3] And so does the transmission of revelation to humanity: the communication of faith requires something more than doctrines being developed and taught, but involves the life and gestures of the one who communicates, that is, their witness. And the same may be said of theology as a whole, whose method is deeply linked with witnessing. It goes to the heart of the Christian message and its transmission.

Joseph Ratzinger once asked how the “banal nihilism” that marks our society could be overcome? He said:

It can only be overcome by witnesses, by people who are really touched by the truth, who do not want to assert themselves, but who have become listeners, hearers, receivers, and who are also prepared to bear witness to the truth. In other words, people with whom we can see that they do not want to become something, they do not want to make a career, they do not want to exercise

[^intro-3]: Cf. Vatican Council II, Const. _Dei Verbum_ (1964), nn. 2, 4, 14, 19; cf. P. O’Callaghan, “L’articolazione tra parola e evento nella Dei Verbum, chiave della testimonianza cristiana,” in Á. Granados and P. O’Callaghan (eds.), _Parola e testimonianza nella comunicazione della fede. Rilettura di un binomio critico alla luce del Concilio Vaticano II_ (Roma: Edusc, 2013), 299–322.

[p. 4]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p4}

power. We need people who have something to communicate and who stand by it, who make it clear that there is something greater that they are serving. Only where there are such people, who are then able to convince others and form the commonality of witnessing, can we simply become visible again, because truth cannot be abstract for us.[^intro-4]

Avery Dulles has the following to say: “In a revealed religion such as Christianity, the key question is how God comes to us and opens up a world of meaning not accessible to human investigative powers. . . . The answer, I suggest, is testimony. Revelation, as God’s word, is a form of divine testimony. Faith is by its nature an acceptance of the word of God, the witness who can neither deceive nor be deceived. God’s word comes to us through human witnesses: the prophets and apostles, the inspired authors of Holy Scripture, and the tradition of the Church, which faithfully passes on and interprets what it has received from Christ and the apostles.”[^intro-5] “Fundamental theology,” he says, “cannot neglect the crucially important factor of testimony.”[^intro-6]

Giuseppe Angelini says it clearly: “Witness is not just one of the tasks the Church has to carry out; neither is it enough to say it is the most important. Witnessing defines the identity of the Church to the very root . . . in favor of the Word of God made man.”[^intro-7]

This text is made up of six chapters. First, we shall consider the different ways in which philosophers have considered the reality of _truth_, its realism, universality, and accessibility. The topic, ofen referred to as “epistemology,” is an important one because people witness precisely to truth. If truth is non-existent or inaccessible, it makes no sense to speak of witnessing. Chapter 2 provides a reflection on certain aspects of theological epistemology, its relationship to philosophy and science, and specifically the role of created mediations in the communication of revelation. The third chapter attempts to provide a general account of the dynamics of witnessing in a philosophical context. In the fourth place, we consider the way in which Jesus Christ, especially in John’s gospel, may be identified as the supreme and ultimate Witness. This is a core chapter of the book. The fifh, applicative, chapter attempts to explain how Christian believers share, under the power of the Holy Spirit, in the witnessing of Christ. Here we shall briefly look into different aspects of Christian evangelization and apostolate in communicating faith to potential believers by witnessing.

The sixth and final chapter goes back to the beginning, as it were, and attempts to answer the question: is “witnessing” a philosophically rigorous category? In

[^intro-4]: J. Ratzinger, “Wir brauchen Zeugen der Wahrheit,” interview with M. Lohmann, in _Rheinischer Merkur / Christ und Welt_ (February 11, 1994), 27, in J. Ratzinger, _Gesammelte Schrifen_, Bd. 13/3 (Freiburg i.B.: Herder, 2017), 1211. The term ‘banal nihilism’ was coined by the philosopher Robert Spaemann.

[^intro-5]: A. Dulles, “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” _First Tings_ (May 2004), 18–23, 21.

[^intro-6]: Dulles, “Fundamental Theology,” 187.

[^intro-7]: G. Angelini, _La testimonianza prima del “dialogo” e oltre_ (Milano: Centro Ambrosiano, 2008), 8.

[p. 5]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p5}

_Introduction_

other words, is it rational and reasonable for humans to believe, to encounter truth, on the basis of the witnessing of Christian believers? Or is it not, rather, a form of power-hungry self-delusion of the individual subject who attempts to monopolize truth in his person and thereby manipulate others? Of critical importance here, as we shall see, is the fact that witnessing is undertaken by human persons, one by one, not by human nature or even institutions, for the person is, in a sense, “superior” to nature, humans to humanity. The high point in this process of course may be found is the “one man” Jesus Christ (cf. Rm 5:12, 15, 17, 18), the supreme Witness.

One final observation regarding methodology. Is the book one of philosophy or theology? Is witnessing and truth a philosophical or a theological issue? It is only possible for us to say “both.” Augustine and Thomas Aquinas appear frequently throughout these pages, and of course the distinction between philosophy and theology would be of little interest to them. Still, we have considered a wide range of philosophers, among them principally: Enrico Castelli, Jacques Ellul, Cornelio Fabro, Claude Geffré, Martin Heidegger, Karl Jaspers, Søren Kierkegaard, Gabriel Marcel, Jean Nabert, Friedrich Nietzsche, Josef Pieper, Karl Popper, Paul Ricoeur, Robert Spaemann, and Gianni Vattimo. And we have also looked into the position of many theologians and Biblical exegetes, among them: Giuseppe Angelini, Ignace de la Potterie, Avery Dulles, Rino Fisichella, Jean-Louis Leuba, Paolo Martinelli, Karl Rahner, Pierangelo Sequeri, Xavier Tilliette, Allison A. Trites, Hans Urs von Balthasar, and Bernard Welte. The interaction between philosophy and theology in the question of truth and witnessing is inevitable and ultimately enriching.

The ultimate conclusion of the book may be found in the last line of the final chapter.

[p. 7]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p7}

## 1. Theories of Truth {#ocallaghan.wtd.1-theories-of-truth}

Verum est id quod est _(Augustine)._[^c1-1]

Theology deals with _truth_, truth about God and the world he created and intends to save. In principle, truth involves what is real, universal, definitive, and unchangeable, what should in principle be held in common by one and all. “Truth,” Augustine says, “is not subject to transformation or change of any kind.”[^c1-2] It stands opposed to whatever is purely subjective, individual, immediate, and transient. Truth has, however, become a decidedly ambivalent category over recent centuries. Not only do philosophers seem to find it increasingly difficult to actually know what is true; their efforts have been directed more and more to considering in what way humans _have access to_ truth in the first place. Perhaps the notion of _truth_ and that of _access to truth_ are not that distant from one another.[^c1-3] If truth exists but there is no access to it, then it is of little interest and advantage. Some would go so far as to say that as humans we are simply not in a position to know truth at all, and that the very word has become meaningless and even redundant. Worse still, the possibility of enunciating a universal, obligatory “truth” is perceived by many as an invitation to violence, to totalitarian imposition, to fundamentalism, especially if it is in the hands of the few. On top of that, if truth as such is considered as inaccessible at a merely _empirical_ level, how much more will it be so in respect of revealed truth, the mystery of God and his design for the universe? Indeed, theological method is meant to reflect critically on _the reliability of human access to revealed truth_, as Pope John Paul II did extensively in his 1998 encyclical _Fides et ratio_.[^c1-4]

### Approaching the Truth of Things: Apprehensio and Judicium

Access to truth may be defined or described in a variety of different ways.[^c1-5] The classic description is that of the so-called “correspondence theory”: truth is

[^c1-1]: Augustine, _Soliloquia_ 2,5.

[^c1-2]: Augustine, _Conf_. 3,6,10.

[^c1-3]: Commenting on the merging between truth and the search for truth, A. De Waelhens says: “La vérité est _recherche_ de la vérité,” “Ambiguïté de la notion de témoignage,” in E. Castelli (ed.), _La testimonianza_ (Roma: Istituto di studi filosofici, 1972), 467–76, here 469.

[^c1-4]: The issue of deconstructionism and postmodernism has been dealt with extensively by John Paul II in this encyclical.

[^c1-5]: For an overview of theories dealing with truth, cf. R.F. O’Neill, _Teories of Knowledge_ (Englewood Cliffs (NJ): Prentice-Hall, 1960); L.B. Puntel, _Wahrheitstheorien in der neueren Philosophie. Eine kritisch-systematische Darstellung_ (Darmstadt: Wissenschafliche Buchgesellschaf, 1978); _Grundlagen einer Teorie der Wahrheit_ (Berlin: Walter de Gruyter, 1990); P. Horwich, ed., _Teories of Truth_ (Aldershot: Dartmouth, 1994); R.L. Kirkham, _Teories of Truth. A Critical Introduction_ (Cambridge (MA): MIT Press, 2001); V. Possenti, “Truth,” in G. Tanzella-Nitti and A. Strumia (eds.), _Interdisciplinary Encyclopedia of Religion and Science_, (2002), www.inters.org/truth; S. Landucci, “Verità,” in _Enciclopedia di Filosofia_ (Le Garzantine) (Milano: Garzanti, 2002), 1192–4; B.A.O. Williams, _Truth and Truthfulness. An Essay in Genealogy_ (Princeton (NJ); Oxford: Princeton University Press, 2002); W. Künne, _Conceptions of Truth_ (Oxford: Clarendon Press, 2003); F.F. Schmitt (ed.), _Teories of Truth_ (Malden (MA); Oxford; Victoria: Blackwell, 2004); S. Blackburn, _Truth: A Guide_ (Cambridge: University Press, 2005); T. Arrigoni and V. Halbach, “Verità,” in _Enciclopedia filosofica_ (Milano: Bompiani, 2006) 12053–97; G. Volpe, _La verità_ (Roma: Carocci, 2012); P. Coda, “Sul concetto di verità in teologia,” in G. Marengo, J. Prades López, and G. Richi Alberti (eds.), _Sufficit gratia tua: miscellanea in onore del card. Angelo Scola per il suo 70. compleanno_ (Venezia: Marcianum Press, 2012), 175–97; C.B. Wrenn, _Truth_ (Cambridge; Malden (MA): Polity Press, 2015). See also: A. Tarski, “The Concept of Truth in Formalized Languages” (1933), in J.H. Woodger (ed.), _Logic, Semantics, Metamathematics. Papers From 1923 to 1938_ (Indianapolis: Hackett Publishing Company, 1983), 152–278; S. Kripke, “Outline of a Theory of Truth,” _Journal of Philosophy_ 72, no. 19 (1975): 690–716.

[p. 8]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p8}

defined as _the conformity between thought and reality_. Such and such a statement is said to be true if there is a correspondence between the concept we have of something and the thing itself. This description is to be found in both Plato and Aristotle.[^c1-6] Thomas Aquinas defines truth as the _adaequatio rei et intellectus_,[^c1-7] the conformity of the thing known and the intellect that knows. It may be noted in passing that for Aquinas the term we have translated as “thing,” _res_ (from which derives the word “real”), is very ample in character. It includes not only inanimate creatures, but also humans, angels, and God himself, and he ofen renders it as “being.” Though truth is located in the intellect and always refers to it,[^c1-8] Thomas says that what gives rise to truth, its source as it were, is the known object, its being, and not the intellect as such. Being is the source of truth, intellect the receiver. Thus he says that _esse rei, non veritas eius, causat veritatem intellectus_,[^c1-9] “being, and not the truth of being, is what causes truth in the intellect.” Beings exist; they _contain truth_, whereas _the intellect knows truth_. Elsewhere he says: _veritas sequitur esse rerum_,[^c1-10] “truth follows the being of things.” All beings may be said to contain a greater or lesser degree of intelligibility,[^c1-11] which Aquinas understood as a kind of “light” that continually emanates from them and the mind captures.[^c1-12] Humans can come to know that intelligibility through a process of abstraction from reality. In that sense, it is probably more precise to define truth in the human context as an _adaequatio intellectus cum re_, “the conformity of the intellect to the thing known.” We are said to know the truth when our idea or concept corresponds to reality.

We just mentioned the term “abstraction” in the process of knowing, which, of course, finds its deepest roots in the philosophy of Aristotle.[^c1-13] “Abstraction” may be considered as an operation of the mind whereby the intellect retains separated and distinct aspects from individually and concretely existing things while omitting other aspects. Thomas Aquinas observes that the process of abstraction by which we come to know about the things we encounter takes place in two stages, what he calls respectively _apprehensio_ and _judicium_.[^c1-14] With the first, apprehension, the knowing subject becomes aware of concepts or impressions produced (or perhaps provoked or occasioned) by the known object, ofen in interaction with concepts the mind already possesses. Apprehension may be found in different ways in every kind of epistemology and may be accounted for in a psycho-organic way.[^c1-15] Animals experience this kind of knowledge, as do plants and all living beings in their own way.

With the second stage of the knowing process, _judgment_, things are different.[^c1-16] The mind is said to go beyond the thoughts or images or concepts or

[^c1-6]: Cf. Plato, _Cratylus_ 385b; _Sophist_ 262e, and Aristotle, _Metaphysics_ IV, 1011b,27f; _Categories_ 4b, 8.

[^c1-7]: Thomas Aquinas, _De Veritate_, q. 1, a. 1c.

[^c1-8]: “Verum per prius invenitur in intellectu quam in rebus,” _De Veritate_, q. 1, a. 3c.

[^c1-9]: Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.I_, q. 16, a. 1, ad 3.

[^c1-10]: Thomas Aquinas, _De Veritate_ q. 12, a. 3, ad 2 and 3.

[^c1-11]: On this topic, cf. J. Pieper, “Wahrheit der Dinge. Eine Untersuchung zur Anthropologie des Hochmittelalters,” in _Schrifen zur philosophischen Anthropologie und Ethik_ (Werke 5) (Hamburg: F. Meiner, 1997), 99–179.

[^c1-12]: Thomas Aquinas, “Ipsa actualitas rei est quoddam lumen ipsius,” _De causis,_ q. 1, a. 6 (n. 168). On this, cf. J. Pieper, “Wahrheit der Dinge,” 136f.

[^c1-13]: Cf. J.J. Cleary, “On the Terminology of ‘Abstraction’ in Aristotle,” _Phronesis_ 30, no. 1 (1985): 13–45; on abstraction in Aquinas’s commentary on Aristotle’s _Physics_, cf. C. De Koninck, “Abstraction From Matter—notes on St Thomas’s Prologue to the Physics,” _Laval théologique et philosophique_ 13 (1957): 133–96; 16 (1960): 53–69; 169–88.

[^c1-14]: “Ad cognitionem enim duo concurrere oportet: scilicet apprehensionem, et iudicium de re apprehensa: et ideo cognitio, qua natura animae cognoscitur, potest considerari et quantum ad apprehensionem, et quantum ad iudicium,” _De Veritate_ q. 10, a. 8c.

[^c1-15]: Important in this regard are the different theories of associationism, especially among English empirical philosophers, in particular D. Hartley, _Observations on Man_ (1749) and J.S. Mill. The theory become fully fledged at a scientific level toward the end of the nineteenth century with H. Ebbinghaus, G.E. Müller (especially the latter’s _Zur Grundlegung der Psychophysik_, 1878), I.P. Pavlov (_Conditioned Reflexes_, 1923–1927) and E.L. Torndike, _Animal Intelligence_ (1898).

[^c1-16]: Cf. J. Owens, “Judgment and Truth in Aquinas,” _Mediaeval Studies_ 32 (1970): 138–58.

[p. 9]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p9}

emotions it encounters and entertains and spontaneously associates, and goes on to establish an intentional connection between the concept and the concrete object apprehended. In the words of Étienne Gilson, “judgment is an act of reason associating or disassociating concepts.”[^c1-17] Convinced of the truth of the affirmation “X is Y,” the mind knowingly links subject and predicate. “The mind judges the apprehended matter to be true when it says that something is or is not,” Aquinas says.[^c1-18] This may seem to constitute a somewhat obvious or even banal move. Yet it is an important one. The fact is, philosophically speaking, that this second stage is not easy to account for. For judgment is an outgoing act, a conscious act, a spiritual act, indeed, to an important degree, a free act. Even though it seems quite obvious to us, and we take it for granted, we should still ask how the mind goes “beyond itself” to identify with the thing known,[^c1-19] thus establishing a connection between itself and its object. How does the mind attain certainty that a faithful conformity has in fact been established between the itself and the object known, in other words, that it has _found_ the truth, as the common expression has it? Two different epistemologies explain the process in somewhat different ways, what may be called the realist and the illuminist.

### Classical Epistemologies

### A Realist Epistemology

In the _first_ place, we may consider the “realist” explanation of knowing truth. This position is typical of Thomas Aquinas. He argues, in line with Aristotle, that humans are equipped with what is called an “agent intellect” capable of receiving and analyzing or _abstracting_ the intelligibility present in things known. From the standpoint of content, intellection is on the whole a passive and receptive process: humans do not _add_ intelligibility to the things they know, but rather encounter it already present, as a kind of light they perceive and receive. In the words of Anselm Rohner, “the human mind, in the very core of its nature, is receptivity, readiness, openness for all reality. . . . The human mind is ultimately the pure receptor of all that is.”[^c1-20] But if humans do not provide intelligibility to the real world, but rather receive it, where does it come from in the first place? According to Thomas, it derives ultimately from God’s creative action: God implants intelligibility in things as he creates them.[^c1-21] In this sense, at a deeper level, truth may be described as the conformity of being with the intellect . . .

[^c1-17]: E. Gilson, _Tomism. The Philosophy of Thomas Aquinas_ (Toronto: Pontifical Institute of Mediaeval Studies, 2002), 270. Judgment is described as “comparatio vel applicatio ad rem per affirmationem vel negationem.”

[^c1-18]: “Intellectus autem formans quidditatem rerum, non habet nisi similitudinem rei existentis extra animam, sicut et sensus in quantum accipit speciem sensibilis; sed quando incipit iudicare de re apprehensa, tunc ipsum iudicium intellectus est quoddam proprium ei, quod non invenitur extra in re. Sed quando adaequatur ei quod est extra in re, dicitur iudicium verum; tunc autem _iudicat intellectus de re apprehensa quando dicit aliquid esse vel non esse_,” _De Veritate_ q. 1, a. 3c. Karl Rahner puts it as follows: “the act of judgement consists in the applying of a cognitive faculty to a being as it is in itself so as to find meaning in it,” “Thomas Aquinas on Truth,” in _Teological Investigations_, vol. 13 (New York: Crossroad, 1965), 13–31, here 21. He speaks of truth and judgment in terms of an affirmative or objectifying synthesis (ibid., 13, 17). Also: “It is only in such judgments that truth exists, for it is only in them that affirmative and objectifying application can be made of the consciously entertained content of our ideas to a reality existing in its own right, and independently of the concept as such that we form of it at the psychic level” (ibid., 17).

[^c1-19]: “Intellectus in actu est intelligibile in actu,” an expression frequently repeated by Aquinas.

[^c1-20]: A. Rohner, “Das Grundproblem der Metaphysik,” in F.-J. Von Rintelen (ed.), _Philosophia Perennis: Abhandlungen zu ihrer Vergangenheit und Gegenwart_ (Regensburg: J. Habbel, 1930), 1073–88, here 1083.

[^c1-21]: “Creatura in Deo est creatrix essentia,” _In Johann_. 1, 2 (Marietti ed., n. 91); “Magis res sunt in Deo quam Deus in rebus,” _S.T.I_, q. 8, a. 3, ad 3. “Omnia in Ipso sunt ipsa vita divina,” _S.T.I_, q. 18, a. 4, ad 2. Cf. P. O’Callaghan, _God’s Gif of the Universe. An Introduction to Creation Teology_ (Washington (DC): Catholic University of America Press, 2022), 191–7.

[p. 10]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p10}

in this case of course with the _divine_ Intellect _(adaequatio rei cum Intellectu_).[^c1-22] Aquinas graphically describes created beings as being suspended, as it were, between two intellects, the divine intellect actively imparting intelligibility to them, the human intellect passively receiving it from them. “All things in nature are constituted in relation to two minds, and are called true as they conform to either the one or the other.”[^c1-23] He explains it as follows: “Reality compares differently to either the active way of knowing or the receptive way of knowing. The mind in its active [divine] intellection is the cause of things, and therefore the ‘measure’ of those things it produces. The mind in its receptive [human] intellection, on the other hand, is the receiver of things, and is in some sense activated by them; those things, therefore, are then the ‘measure’ of the mind.”[^c1-24]

As created by God and known by humans, created beings may be said to _contain_ truth, or to _be_ true. In the words of Josef Pieper, “all that has being also has truth. The truth of a being consists of its orientation toward a knowing mind. . . . Truth of an existing thing consists in its orientation toward the knowing mind.”[^c1-25] However, when all is said and done, God, the Divinity, must be taken as the origin and basis of all truth.[^c1-26] “The knowability of all things by man is secondary to their being known by God,” says Pieper, who goes on to quote Thomas’s _De Veritate:_ “The first meaning of truth is more intrinsic to things than the second, for their relationship to God’s mind takes priority over their relationship to the human mind. Therefore, even in the absence of any human mind, things would still be called true, namely in view of God’s knowing mind.”[^c1-27] The truth of beings in the transcendental sense “means principally that all existing beings are ordered toward the creative knowledge of _God’s_ mind.”[^c1-28] And Aquinas concludes: “Thus every being is true, _because_ every thing in the world, by its intrinsic form, conforms to God’s artistry.”[^c1-29] Augustine said the same thing: we see all things because they exist, but they exist only because God sees them.[^c1-30] T. S. Eliot puts the same thing in a prayerful way: “For all things exist only as seen by Tee, Only as known by Tee, all things exist Only in thy light.”[^c1-31]

### An Illuminist Epistemology

The _second_ epistemology, that may be called the “illuminist” theory of truth, is typical for example of Plato (and Augustine). Thomas’s realist theory of knowledge coincides with Plato’s illuminist one in that the Divinity is considered

[^c1-22]: “Res aliqua principalius dicitur vera in ordine ad veritatem intellectus divini quam in ordine ad veritatem intellectus humani,” Thomas Aquinas, _De Ver._ q. 1, a. 4c; “Prior est comparatio ad intellectum divinum quam humanum,” _De Ver._ q. 1, a. 2c. Cf. G. Poltner, “Veritas est Adaequatio Intellectus et Rei: der Gesprächsbeitrag Des Thomas Von Aquin Zum Problem der Übereinstimmung,” _Zeitschrif für philosophische Forschung_ 37 (1983): 563–76.

[^c1-23]: “Res ergo naturalis inter duos intellectus constituta, secundum adaequationem ad utrumque vera dicitur; secundum enim adaequationem ad intellectum divinum dicitur vera, in quantum implet hoc ad quod est ordinata per intellectum divinum,” Thomas Aquinas, _De Ver._ q. 1, a. 2c.

[^c1-24]: “Res aliter comparatur ad intellectum practicum, aliter ad speculativum. Intellectus enim practicus causat res, unde est mensura rerum quae per ipsum fiunt: sed intellectus speculativus, quia accipit a rebus, est quodammodo motus ab ipsis rebus, et ita res mensurant ipsum. Ex quo patet quod res naturales, a quibus intellectus noster scientiam accipit, mensurant intellectum nostrum, ut dicitur _X Metaph_.: sed sunt mensuratae ab intellectu divino, in quo sunt omnia sicut omnia artificiata in intellectu artificis,” Thomas Aquinas, _De Ver.,_ q. 1, a. 2, c. Elsewhere, he writes: “Sic ergo intellectus divinus est mensurans non mensuratus; res autem naturalis, mensurans et mensurata; sed intellectus noster mensuratus et non mensurans res quidem naturales, sed artificiales tantum,” ibid _._

[^c1-25]: J. Pieper, _Te Truth of All Things. An Inquiry into the Anthropology of the High Middle Ages_ (San Francisco (CA): Ignatius Press, 1989), 37, 41.

[^c1-26]: Cf. J. Pieper, “Wahrheit der Dinge,” 134–6. “In definitione rei verae potest poni visio in actu intellectus divini, non autem visio intellectus humani, nisi in potentia,” _De Ver.,_ q. 1, a. 2 ad 4. “Magis res sunt in Deo quam Deus in rebus,” _S.T. I_, q. 8, a. 3 ad 1.

[^c1-27]: Pieper, _Te Truth of All Things,_ 55. Cf. _De Ver._, q. 1, a. 2, c.

[^c1-28]: Ibid., 43.

[^c1-29]: “Est ut quaelibet res dicatur esse vera secundum quod habet propriam formam, secundum quam imitatur artem divinam,” Thomas Aquinas, _Peri Hermeneias_ I, 3.

[^c1-30]: Augustine, _Conf._ 12, 38.

[^c1-31]: T.S. Eliot, _Murder in the Cathedral,_ final chorus.

[p. 11]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p11}

to be the basis and ultimate source of all truth. God is fundamental to knowledge. However, whereas Aquinas speaks of the knowing process in terms of abstraction from created beings, which are capable of illuminating the intellect, Plato holds that we know reality basically by means of direct divine illumination. Augustine more or less follows him on this.[^c1-32] Plato argues that human souls at the remote origins of their existence contemplated the intelligible world of ideas or forms, but came to forget their intelligible content on account of a primitive fall into the material realm.[^c1-33] To know, therefore, essentially means to _remember_ the original, innate ideas (this process is called _anamnesis_), to overcome forgetfulness, to recognize what was once contemplated in the world of Ideas. Truth has its origin in God, is located in the mind, and is remembered and made explicit by means of our encounter with reality. Whereas with a realist epistemology “it is by turning toward the body that the soul is raised to knowledge of its objects, and not by turning away from it, as the Platonic doctrine of innate ideas would demand.”[^c1-34]

Augustine, it should be said, does not go so far as Plato in that he explains that God illuminates the soul not with _all possible_ concepts, but rather with some basic criteria that guide intellection (abstract truths such as numbers, absolute rules of conduct and the like).[^c1-35] In fact, “Augustine substituted for Plato’s Ideas the essences of all creatures united in the divine mind. All things would be created in conformity with these essences, and through them the human soul would know everything.”[^c1-36]

### Modern Developments of Illuminist Epistemology

A similar epistemology to that of Plato and Augustine may be found in medieval authors of a Platonic bent such as Ps.-Dionysius the Areopagite,[^c1-37] Bonaventure[^c1-38] and Henry of Ghent.[^c1-39] However, similar explanations reappear in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, and a position gradually consolidates which detaches truth from things/beings, and situates it in statements or affirmations. According to Nicholas Malebranche († 1715), a student of Augustine and Descartes, created beings serve as mere _occasions_ for God to act and make himself known directly to the intellect.[^c1-40] For Gottfried Leibniz († 1716), God has already given humans the capacity or disposition to discover ideas; the senses at best provide a stimulus or occasion for the mind to make that discovery.[^c1-41] For this reason he does not accept the idea that “all that exists is true.”[^c1-42] Jacob Tomasius († 1684), the teacher of Leibniz, elaborates this idea further, and asks the question: “Is

[^c1-32]: On the question, cf. J. Hessen, _Augustins Metaphysik der Erkenntnis_ (Berlin; Bonn: F. Dümmler, 1931); C. Boyer, _L’idée de vérité dans la philosophie de saint Augustin (1920),_ 2 ed. (Paris: Beauchesne, 1940).

[^c1-33]: Cf. Plato, _Phaedo_ 74b, 75; _Meno_ 81ff.

[^c1-34]: Gilson, _Tomism_, 248.

[^c1-35]: Cf. Augustine, _De libero arbitrio II_.

[^c1-36]: Gilson, _Tomism_, 249.

[^c1-37]: Cf. Ps.-Dionysius the Areopagite, _De divinis nominibus IV_, 6.

[^c1-38]: Cf. J.-G. Bougerol, _Introduction à saint Bonaventure_ (Paris: Vrin, 1988).

[^c1-39]: Cf. S.P. Marrone, _Truth and Scientific Knowledge in the Thought of Henry of Ghent_ (Cambridge: Medieval Academy of America, 1985).

[^c1-40]: Cf. T. Arrigoni and V. Halbach, “Verità,” 12059. See Malebranche’s work _Recherche de la vérité III_, II, cap. V. “The ideas of all beings reside in God who created them; otherwise he would not have been able to create them” (ibid., cap. VI). And since God is united closely with souls present to him, the human spirit “can see God in the works of God,” ibid.

[^c1-41]: Leibniz speaks of “virtual innatism.” Cf. for example his 1705 work _Neue Abhandlungen über den menschlichen Verstand_ (Leipzig: F. Meiner, 1926).

[^c1-42]: Pieper, _Te Truth of All Things,_ 18.

[p. 12]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p12}

truth, strictly speaking, a property of reality?” And he responds: “No. Why not? Because truth does not reside in reality but in the mind that perceives reality. Can truth, therefore, in no wise be called a property of reality? Truth can be called an extrinsic, or an extrinsically defining, property of reality.”[^c1-43] Before Leibniz and Tomasius, the philosopher Thomas Hobbes († 1679) puts it in the following terms: _veritas enim in dicto, non in re consistit_,[^c1-44] “truth consists in what is said, not in beings.” Likewise, we may say that “for Descartes there is no truth in things.”[^c1-45]

The pantheist Baruch Spinoza († 1677) says that “altogether in the wrong are those who consider truth to be a property of being.”[^c1-46] For him, “truth could only reside in a statement. Things he calls simply ‘mute.’”[^c1-47] He explains that truth is situated between the Idea (the divine) and the concept that exists in the mind. That is to say, truth is situated in the Idea, not in the being that more or less incarnates the idea.[^c1-48]

The theory of knowledge of Immanuel Kant († 1804) moves in the same direction: the synthetic judgments the intellect provide us with are not derived from sense experience, from our encounter with real things, but are already present, _a priori,_ existent in our mind.[^c1-49] No longer rooted in real beings, truth is located in the mind, primarily in the divine mind, and derivatively, only in the human mind.[^c1-50] Thus it is sterile and tautological to speak of the truth of all things.[^c1-51] Truth not a property of things, but refers rather to “certain logical requirements and conditions inherent in any _perception_ of things. . . . In fact Kant explicitly denies truth to be a property of reality as such.”[^c1-52] For Kant and the Enlightenment as the whole, “truth means this: each thing is what it is.”[^c1-53] This tautological description is quite different from Augustine’s dictum _verum est id quod est,_ “that which is, is true,”[^c1-54] in spite of the latter’s illuminism. Similar explanations to Kant’s may be found in Schelling’s early writings,[^c1-55] as well as in Hegel’s.[^c1-56]

According to the modern illuminist theories just described, concrete beings are not considered as essential elements in the process of knowing: truth is relational only in the sense that the knowing subject (and not so much the known thing) is bound up with the original source of illumination. As a result, the idea of truth as correspondence— _adaequatio_, the conformity of being and intellect— becomes less and less meaningful. At best, as fourteenth-century Nominalists held, such a definition of truth is valid at a merely formal or propositional level, as mere tautology.[^c1-57] This notion has consolidated and predominated over recent centuries.

[^c1-43]: J. Tomasius, _Erotemata_, chap. 5.

[^c1-44]: T. Hobbes, _Logica_ 3:7.

[^c1-45]: O. Willmann, _Geschichte des Idealismus,_ vol. 3 (Braunschweig: F. Vieweg, 1897), 241. R. Descartes, _Meditations_ III: “I seem therefore justified in setting forth a general principle that whatever I perceive ‘clearly’ and ‘distinctly,’ is true.” But for Thomas truth is not distinct (Pieper, _Te Truth of All Tings_, 27).

[^c1-46]: B. Spinoza, _Cogitata metaphysica_ 1:6.

[^c1-47]: Pieper, _Te Truth of All Tings_, 17.

[^c1-48]: On Spinoza, cf. the classic but still useful study of R. Lévêque, _Le problème de la vérité dans la philosophie de Spinoza_ (Strasbourg; Paris: Libraire Istra, 1923).

[^c1-49]: The topic is developed extensively in Kant’s first edition of the _Critique of Pure Reason_ (1781).

[^c1-50]: On recent theories of truth, cf. the text corresponding to note 5 above.

[^c1-51]: In the first edition of the _Critique of Pure Reason_, Kant is silent about the matter. In the second edition (1787), the notion of truth within reality “represents a view which, however empty it may seem to be, has maintained itself over this very long period. It therefore deserves to be investigated in respect of its origins,” § 12.

[^c1-52]: Pieper, _Truth of All Tings_, 20. Likewise, Wolff and Baumgarten look upon all consideration of the truth of things as sterile and empty tautology.

[^c1-53]: B. Erdmann, “Mitteilungen über Kant’s metaphysischen Standpunkt in der Zeit um 1774,” _Philosophische Monatshefe_ 20 (1884) 81. This position may also be found in the writings of Antonio Rosmini: cf. T. Manferdini, _Essere e verità in Rosmini_, 2 ed. (Bologna: ESD, 1994).

[^c1-54]: Augustine, _Soliloquia_ 2,5.

[^c1-55]: Cf. F.W.J. Schelling, _System des transzendentalen Idealismus_ (1800).

[^c1-56]: Cf. G.W.F. Hegel, _Te Science of Logic_ (1812–16). On this question, cf. S. Bungay, _Beauty and Truth: A Study of Hegel’s Aesthetics_ (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1984).

[^c1-57]: Cf. O’Neill, _Teories of Knowledge_. The idea of truth as a “true proposition,” is to be found for example in T. Hobbes, _De corpore_, 3, n. 7. As the Nominalist William of Ockham shows (_Summa logicae_, I, 43; _Quodlibeta_, V, q. 24), from the XIV century onwards, the conception of truth as _adaequatio_ or conformity tends to assume a merely logical significance.

[p. 13]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p13}

### Comparing the Illuminist and Realist Theories of Knowledge

At a substantial level, there would seem to be little difference between the classical illuminist and realist theories of truth.[^c1-58] For both of them, truth has its origin in God who illuminates the mind, either directly (illuminism), or indirectly through the agency of creatures (realism). Yet we may observe that the former explanation of the knowing process offers several advantages over the latter one, the Platonic/Augustinian over the Thomistic, as does the latter over the former.

_First_, illuminism provides a straightforward account of human knowledge and truth that is in a position avoid relativism and its derivative, skepticism. Truth exists and is universal and accessible, for it derives from the Divinity, the One. Humans’ access to truth may be prejudiced by the presence of corporeal elements that interfere in the knowing process, on account of a severance of the creature from the Creator occasioned by the original fall. This position may be found not only in Plato but also in Kant.[^c1-59]

_Second_, on the face of things, Thomas’s realist explanation, involving the _individual_ agent intellect abstracting truth from concrete beings is, philosophically speaking, less convincing. Truth is located in creatures that are situated before each individual intellect. If that is so, how can we speak of access to a _common_ truth? Each one would only come to know their _own_ truth. It should come as no surprise to find that Aristotle, who originated the theory, quite probably situated the agent intellect within the ambit of the Divinity itself,[^c1-60] and made it common to all human beings.[^c1-61] In ultimate terms, Aristotle’s explanation (according to Avicenna and other interpreters) simplifies things and actually comes quite close to the illuminist theory, in that a single agent intellect common to all individuals empowers the process of abstraction, and thus the apprehension of truth as common for all. By contrast, Aquinas is decidedly modern in his approach to intellection, and insists on the individuality of each one’s agent intellect. He says openly that _hic homo intelligit_,[^c1-62] “this human being understands,” this individual, this intellect, thinks and knows, but not humanity as a whole. Whether collective or individual, the agent intellect of course is not an easy category to deal with. Still, Thomas’s affirmation on the individuality of the human intellect is of great importance not only in the ambit of epistemology but also in that of anthropology.[^c1-63]

In the _third_ place, the illuminist explanation of truth obviates, in principle, all possible conflict between religion and knowledge, between faith and reason.

[^c1-58]: In _De spiritualibus creaturis_, Thomas Aquinas says as much: “Non multum autem refert dicere, quod ipsa intelligibilia participantur a Deo, vel quod lumen faciens intelligibilia participetur,” a. 10, ad 8. On this text, cf. E. Gilson, “Pourquoi saint Thomas a critiqué saint Augustin,” _Archives d’Histoire Doctrinale et Littéraire du Moyen-Âge_ 1 (1926): 5–127, especially 118f. (reprinted by J. Vrin, Paris, 1986).

[^c1-59]: Cf. the notion of “radical evil,” in Kant’s _Religion within the Limits of Reason alone_ (1793).

[^c1-60]: At least to the mind of Averroes, one of Aristotle’s principal interpreters.

[^c1-61]: On the agent intellect in Aristotle, cf. especially _De Anima III_, 5. On the controversy, cf. the brief study of R. Reyna, “On the Soul: a Philosophical Exploration of the Active Intellect in Averroes, Aristotle, and Aquinas,” _Te Tomist_ 36 (1972): 131–49.

[^c1-62]: Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.I,_ q. 76, a. 1c, and elsewhere.

[^c1-63]: See P. O’Callaghan, _Children of God in the World. An Introduction to Theological Anthropology_ (Washington (DC): Catholic University of America Press, 2016), 440f., and also chapter 6 below.

[p. 14]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p14}

Since the human intellect is illuminated with the truth that derives directly from God, both reason and faith move quite comfortably on the same plane.[^c1-64] Call it illumination, call it divine revelation . . . the difference is slight.

However, over against the realist understanding of the process of human knowledge, the illuminist theory has a pair of important disadvantages, both of which are of relevance in our present circumstances. _First_, that it pays little attention to the consistency and intelligibility of concrete created beings as such, of reality in its down-to-earth character, and _second,_ that it takes the existence and illuminating action of God as a binding point of departure for any epistemology. In other words, the Divinity must needs be present and active in the illuminating process; thus God’s existence is taken for granted as undeniable if we intend to speak of truth. Let us examine these points more closely.

_On the one hand_, in effect, the illuminist theory pays scant attention to the intelligibility proper to particular beings. The created world, whether it be considered as infected by sin or as helplessly beset by mutability, would seem to play little or no part in the process of knowledge. Truth (or wisdom) remains an abstract or general category: abstract in its origin, abstract in its reception by the human mind, attached but superficially to any form of tangible communicating mediation, heedless of the senses. Certainly, the illuminist position does attain full validity in the ambit of faith and vision, when God directly illuminates the intellect, making it possible to accept divine revelation as true and divine vision as definitive. It may be said therefore that divine illumination provides us with the _apprehensio,_ yet substantially takes the place of the _judicium_; both aspects of the knowing process thus merge with one another.

As we already saw, Aquinas’ position is not unlike Augustine’s in that the truthfulness of our knowledge of things is ultimately divine in origin. According to Aquinas, the _lumen intellectuale_ present in humans is indeed a sharing in the _lumen increatum_ which is God.[^c1-65] But for Thomas knowledge is obtained through the positive agency of concrete created objects perceived directly by the senses. Indeed, every single act of human knowledge involves what he calls a _conversio ad phantasmata_,[^c1-66] the turning of the mind toward the concrete image. In this way the dynamic of faith and that of reason are distinguished more clearly from one another than is possible in illuminist epistemologies. In the words of Gilson already cited, “it is by turning toward the body that the soul is raised to knowledge of its objects, and not by turning away from it, as the Platonic doctrine of innate ideas would demand.”[^c1-67]

[^c1-64]: On the close relationship between the dynamics of human reason and Christian faith in Kant, cf. J.M. Odero, _La fe en Kant_ (Pamplona: Eunsa, 1992).

[^c1-65]: “Ipsum enim lumen intellectuale quod est in nobis, nihil est aliud quam quaedam participata similitudo luminis increati, in quo continentur rationes aeternae,” Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.I_, q. 84, a. 5c.

[^c1-66]: Cf. ibid., q. 84, a. 7. “Animae igitur secundum illum modum essendi quo corpori est unita, competit modus intelligendi per conversionem ad phantasmata corporum,” _S.T. I,_ q. 89, a. 1c.

[^c1-67]: Gilson, _Tomism_, 248.

[p. 15]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p15}

As a result, the illuminist theory of knowledge is not in an ideal position to facilitate transdisciplinary dialogue with the empirical sciences, nor does it have much to say in the ambit of the “theologies of the genitive”: theology of life, theology of work, theology of marriage, etc. It should come as no surprise that the great majority of scientists throughout the second millennium have retained a generically realist “correspondence” definition of truth, as if their very livelihood and legacy depended on it. Copernicus, Kepler, Galileo, Newton and many others were convinced that the laws they discovered matched up quite satisfactorily to the nature and the inner workings of things. Among twentiethcentury philosophers of science, the correspondence model is openly defended for example by Alfred Tarski[^c1-68] and Karl Popper.[^c1-69] The latter, though agnostic on the religious plane, understands truth statements always as _hypotheses_ that truly though imperfectly express the reality of nature, without ever explaining it definitively. Galileo’s mistake, he observes, was that of having mistaken a hypothesis for definitive, incontrovertible, demonstrable truth.[^c1-70]

_On the other hand_, the illuminist theory of knowledge and truth has the added disadvantage of taking the existence and action of the Divinity as a binding point of departure for any epistemology, thus leaning either toward fideism (which means we have access to truth exclusively by faith) or ontologism (i.e., we possess an intuitive _a priori_ knowledge of God which makes all knowledge possible). Such an approach might be workable for a theologian, though is not necessarily so for a philosopher or a scientist, or for that matter for the theologian who attempts to dialogue with philosophy and science in a transdisciplinary way.[^c1-71] Understandably, therefore, thinkers of an illuminist or idealistic penchant, who are not prepared to accept the existence and action of a transcendent Divinity as a systematic prelude to their quest for truth, will easily lapse into a subjectivist approach to knowledge and truth, be this in individualistic or collectivistic terms. Many attempts at redefining truth on the margin of the Divinity have been made over recent centuries. Four may be noted.

### Overcoming the Impasse: Secularized Understandings of Truth

Four modern understandings of truth, _etsi Deus non daretur,_ to use the expression of Hugo Grotius, “as if God did not exist,” may be mentioned: coherence, consensus, performative and revelation. In all four, truth, or better, our access to truth, is situated within the bounds of an autonomous humanity and cosmos, in principle independent of the divine. Any reference above and beyond the

[^c1-68]: Cf. especially A. Tarski’s works “The Concept of Truth in Formalized Languages” (1935), already cited, _Introduction to Logic_ (1951); _Logic, Semantics, Metamathematics_ (1923–38, published in 1956).

[^c1-69]: Cf. especially K. Popper’s works _Knowledge and the Body-mind Problem_; _Objective Knowledge; Realism and the Aim of Science_. For an overview of his thought, cf. also his collection of essays _Conjectures and Refutations: The Growth of Scientific Knowledge_, 16 ed. (New York; San Francisco: Harper & Row, 1985). For a brief introduction to his thought, cf. M. Artigas, “Popper, Karl Raimund,” in _Dizionario Interdisciplinare di Scienza e Fede_, vol. 2, 2055–66.

[^c1-70]: On the inadequacy of Popper’s position, cf. J. Naydler, “The Poverty of Popperism,” _Te Tomist_ 82 (1982): 92–107.

[^c1-71]: Cf. Pope Francis, Apostolic Constitution _Veritatis gaudium_ (2017), especially nn. 1–6; _Ad Teologiam promovendam_ (2023). Cf. in particular the articles on this document in the _Vademecum_ prepared by the Pontifical Academy of Theology (Venice: Marcianum Press, 2024) by P. O’Callaghan (23–8), and R. Ferri (47–52).

[p. 16]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p16}

empirical, human and cosmic sphere (extra-mental reality in general and God in particular) remains ambiguous, absent or irrelevant for these theories.[^c1-72] As we shall see later on, however, all four attempts offer valuable insights into the process of access to truth in a theological context.[^c1-73]

1. Among idealist authors, it is common for truth to be understood in terms of _coherence_. Although Kant adopts the classic definition of truth as conformity or correspondence, the correspondence he envisages is experienced by humans as _the coherence of thought with itself_. Truth, in other words, is the agreement of thought with the general laws of the intellect, of reason. What goes against these laws, what is inconsistent with the reasoning process, what is “illogical,” is thus false. That is to say, truth is situated exclusively in the mind.[^c1-74] The “thing in itself” (_noumenon_) plays no essential part in the process of truth, and we can obtain access to it only indirectly. A similar position is to be found in more recent idealistic theories, notably those of Francis Bradley,[^c1-75] Rudolf Carnap[^c1-76] and Otto Neurath.[^c1-77] This approach is useful insofar as it attempts to integrate the different elements and convictions that arise within the mind of each person. Truth must be coherent with itself.

However, understandably, these positions have been criticized extensively, for example by Moritz Schlick,[^c1-78] Ludwig Wittgenstein and others,[^c1-79] on account of their incapacity to provide any univocal, shared or universal criterion for truth. At best they give rise to some version of “my truth,” to each one’s private truth, to what is true for you, for me or for us. The Spanish poet Antonio Machado expressed this as follows: “your truth, no, Truth, and come with me to look for it. Your own truth, keep it for yourself.”[^c1-80] Coherence is clearly bound up with individualism. Still, the theory does have the advantage of according considerable value to the individual human intellect, to what Aquinas openly opposed in Avicenna’s reading of Aristotles’ common agent intellect,[^c1-81] when he stated unequivocally: _hic homo intelligit_, “this man thinks.”[^c1-82] Truth is to be found in other words in the intellect of each person. As we shall see, this is essential if we wish to understand the witnessing process.

2. Aware of the closed and individualistic nature of truth propounded by idealist thinkers in the name of coherence theory, other authors—in particular Karl-Otto Apel[^c1-83] and Jürgen Habermas[^c1-84] —suggest that truth is to be found not so much in the agreement of the individual’s thought with itself, which is ultimately meaningless, but in agreement between different individuals. Truth, in other words, belongs to the ambit of intersubjectivity, is obtained by means of dialogue, and produces _consensus_. The role of dialogue (and therefore consensus)

[^c1-72]: On different modern theories of truth, cf. Puntel, _Wahrheitstheorien in der neueren Philosophie. Eine kritisch-systematische Darstellung_; and _Grundlagen einer Teorie der Wahrheit_; and also Arrigoni & Halbach, “Verità,” 12053–97.

[^c1-73]: Cf. chapter 6 below.

[^c1-74]: Cf. I. Kant, _Critique of Pure Reason: Transcendental Logic, Introduction III_.

[^c1-75]: Cf. F.H. Bradley, _Appearance and Reality_ (1893).

[^c1-76]: Cf. R. Carnap, _Meaning and Necessity: a Study in Semantics and Modal Logic_, 2 ed. (Chicago; London: University of Chicago Press, 1979).

[^c1-77]: Cf. different contributions of O. Neurath to the _International Encyclopedia of Unified Science_ (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1938).

[^c1-78]: Cf. M. Schlick, “Über das Fundament der Erkenntnis,” _Erkentniss_ 4 (1934): 79–99; “Meaning and Verification,” _Philosophical Review_ 45 (1936): 339–69.

[^c1-79]: For different critiques, cf. E.J. Olsson, _Against Coherence: Truth, Probability, and Justification_ (Oxford; New York: Clarendon Press, 2005).

[^c1-80]: A. Machado, _Proverbios y cantares_, n. 89.

[^c1-81]: For a good summary, cf. P. Lee, “Saint Thomas and Avicenna on the Agent Intellect,” _Te Tomist_ 45 (1981): 41–61.

[^c1-82]: _S.T.I,_ q. 76, a. 1c.

[^c1-83]: Cf. K.-O. Apel, “Fallibilismus, Konsenstheorie der Wahrheit and Letztbegründung,” in Forum für Philosophie Bad Homburg, _Philosophie und Begründung_ (Frankfurt a.M.: Suhrkamp, 1987), 116–211.

[^c1-84]: Cf. especially J. Habermas, _Erkenntnis und Interesse_ (Frankfurt am Main: Suhrkamp, 1968); _Wahrheit und Rechtfertigung: Philosophische Aufsätze_ (Frankfurt a.M.: Suhrkamp Verlag, 1999).

[p. 17]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p17}

in the process of coming to know the truth has a long and distinguished career. It goes back to Socrates, and perhaps beyond. It is frequently in a position to give expression to many elements of truth precisely in its universality.

However, the theory is inadequate in that it may likewise “express mere conventionality among the members of a group, society or culture. . . . Conventional basic convictions . . . may reflect the human desire for comfort and the lack of challenges to its basic convictions.”[^c1-85] “It remains subject to doubt whether the agreement sought for is only inter-subjective and linguistic or objective and real, based upon an effective conformity of being.”<sup>86</sup> Kierkegaard’s ironic diatribe is well known in this regard: “There is a view of life that holds that truth is where the crowd is, that truth itself needs to have the crowd on its side.” However, he sentences, “‘the crowd’ is untruth.”[^c1-87] Heidegger likewise denounces collectivism as an inadequate way to obtain truth.[^c1-88] The fact is that truth obtained by means of consensus remains open to manipulation, and requires a carefully applied hermeneutic, which becomes more and more difficult to establish with the passing of time. Besides, it has been observed that Habermas’ consensus theory simply cannot avoid arguing in a circle, for he is forced to have recourse to the idea of a “competent” or authoritative judgement distinguishing objective judgment from a merely conventional one.[^c1-89] All in all, as in the coherence theory, Kant’s dualism between reality and thought prevails in our access to truth.

However, we may still ask: how could the consensus theory provide a valid and useful, though partial, way of accessing truth? Perhaps by ensuring that both _God_, other _humans_, and _extra-mental reality_ are included as fully fledged dialogue partners. Is such an ample dialogue possible? We shall return to this in chapter 6.

3. Dissatisfied with theories that attempt unsuccessfully to encounter preexistent truth in the ambit of human subjectivity or intersubjectivity (what we called coherence and consensus theories of truth respectively), other authors renounce the idea of delineating any form of objective, pre-existent, universal truth awaiting discovery, and speak of truth rather in _performative_ terms. Humans, it is said, are capable of _producing_ truth: we make things come true, we implant intelligibility within reality. Truth may be considered in this sense as a kind of _adaequatio rei cum intellectu_, “the conformity of reality to the mind,” the mind in question of course no longer being divine, but exclusively human.

Specifically Nietzsche suggested that truth should be considered in terms of _utility_.[^c1-90] Truth is not something we encounter already existent in reality, which we assimilate and to which we adjust our lives, but something we create with

[^c1-85]: W. Pannenberg, _Systematic Teology_, vol. 1 (Edinburgh: T.&T. Clark, 1988), 12f. 86 Possenti, “Truth.”

[^c1-87]: S. Kierkegaard, “The Point of View, Supplement,” in _Kierkegaard’s Writings_ 22 (Princeton (NJ): Princeton University Press, 1998), 106.

[^c1-88]: “The public ‘surrounding world’ is already at hand and taken care of in the surrounding world nearest to us. In utilizing public transportation, in the use of information services such as the newspaper, every other is like the next. This being-with-one-another dissolves one’s own _Dasein_ completely in the kind of being of ‘the others’ in such a way that the others, as distinguishable and explicit, disappear ever more. In this inconspicuousness and ‘unascertainability,’ the ‘they’ unfolds its true dictatorship. We enjoy ourselves and have fun the way _they_ enjoy themselves. We read, see, and judge literature and art the way _they_ see and judge. But we also withdraw from the ‘great mass’ the way _they_ withdraw, we find ‘shocking’ what _they_ find shocking. The ‘they,’ which is nothing definite and which all are, though not as a sum, prescribes the kind of being and everydayness,” M. Heidegger, _Being and Time_ (Albany: State University of New York Press, 1996), 119.

[^c1-89]: Cf. the critique of Habermas by A. Beckermann, “Die realistischen Voraussetzungen der Konsenstheorie von J. Habermas,” _Zeitschrif für Allgemeine Wissenschafstheorie_ 3 (1972): 63–80.

[^c1-90]: Cf. M. Clark, _Nietzsche on Truth and Philosophy_ (Cambridge; New York: Cambridge University Press, 1995).

[p. 18]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p18}

a view to reinforcing life.[^c1-91] To the question “what is truth?” he said: “A mobile army of metaphors, metonyms, and anthropomorphisms—in short, a sum of human relations, which have been enhanced, transposed, and embellished poetically and rhetorically, and which afer long use seem firm, canonical, and obligatory to a people: truths are illusions about which one has forgotten that this is what they are; metaphors which are worn out and without sensuous power; coins which have lost their pictures and now matter only as metal, no longer as coins.”[^c1-92]

In a like fashion, the empiricist William James understands truth in strictly _pragmatic_ terms.[^c1-93] Truth-seeking consists of the human capacity to offer effective solutions to problems. Statements are true insofar as they amplify our dominion over the cosmos and society, _insofar as they work_. A similar position is taken by John Dewey, who places the emphasis on an instrumental and functional characteristic capable of etching into nature and society various cognitive procedures endowed with truth, that is, with effectiveness.[^c1-94] This resonates with the teaching of both Jean-Paul Sartre and Michel Foucault.

The position, though inadequate in that it excludes the foundational intelligibility that God implanted in creation, and places an impossibly heavy weight of creative responsibility on human shoulders, has the advantage of recognizing the real role humans are meant to play in knowing, transforming, and humanizing the world, specifically in the _communication_ of truth. Humans, indeed, are creative beings, in that they provide insights and perceptions that change and improve the world they live in, and enrich its truth-value. In the words of Angelini, “the truth of God, indeed truth as such, constitutes an appeal to human freedom. . . . In the life of humans, truth happens.”[^c1-95]

4. A fourth way of redefining truth independently of the transcendent sphere may be described in terms of openness, evidence, direct contact, and discovery. It is to be found among existentialist thinkers such as Heidegger and Jaspers. The former[^c1-96] attempts to recuperate the original meaning of the Greek term for truth, _aletheia_, which literally means “_revelation_” or “uncovering.” This understanding of truth is possible, Heidegger says, because being of its very essence is _self-manifesting_ to humans (he uses the Greek term _phaeinein_, “to show,” “to appear,” “to be made manifest”). However, Heidegger is not referring here to the intellectual identification of the thinking subject with the known being, but rather the other way around: he refers to the identification of being itself with the mind. From the point of view of the subject who knows, he says, all we can say is that “the essence of truth is freedom.”[^c1-97] In other words, we must

[^c1-91]: See Nietzsche’s brief 1873 work “On Truth and Lies in an Extra-Moral Sense,” in W. Kaufmann (trans.), _Te Portable Nietzsche_ (New York: Viking Press, 1976).

[^c1-92]: F. Nietzsche, “On Truth and Lies in an Extra-Moral Sense,” in _Te Portable Nietzsche,_ 46f.

[^c1-93]: Cf. especially, W. James, _Te Meaning of Truth: A Sequel to “Pragmatism”_ (London: Longmans, Green & Co., 1909); _Pragmatism: A New Name for Some Old Ways of Thinking. Together with Four Related Essays Selected from “The Meaning of Truth”_ (New York: Longmans, Green & Co., 1959).

[^c1-94]: Cf. J. Dewey, _Reconstruction in Philosophy_, 1920.

[^c1-95]: G. Angelini, _La testimonianza: prima del ‘dialogo’ e oltre_ (Milano: Centro Ambrosiano, 2008), 66.

[^c1-96]: Cf. M. Heidegger, _Vom Wesen der Wahrheit: zu Platons Höhlengleichnis und Teätet_ (orig., 1930) (Frankfurt am Main: V. Klostermann, 1988). In English, _On the Essence of Truth._ Cf. P.J. Fritz, “Revelation in Heidegger,” in B.M. Mezei, F.A. Murphy, and K. Oakes (eds.), _Te Oxford Handbook of Divine Revelation_ (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2021), 308–21.

[^c1-97]: Cf. Heidegger’s principal work _Sein und Zeit_, § 44. This section deals with the essence of truth.

[p. 19]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p19}

simply _allow being to be_, for only in this way can it manifest itself. In a sense, truth’s place is taken over by _authenticity_.[^c1-98] For Heidegger, as in Kant, a deep gulf remains between the real order and the ideal order;[^c1-99] this is particularly apparent in his later writings. Something of a kind may be said of the philosophy of Karl Jaspers.[^c1-100]

Truth takes place, Heidegger says, as the self-revelation or self-manifestation of beings to humans, each individual existence encountering its own truth. Thus truth as such is not common in content _a priori_; at best, the “truths” of individuals relate to one another, and possibly coalesce, through communication in a gradual _a posteriori_ process.

We have considered four ways of understanding truth alternative to the classic conformity model described in the formula _adaequatio rei et intellectus._ In the coming chapters we shall attempt to provide a theological epistemology, the way in which we encounter truth through theological reflection (Chapter 2); then we shall consider the dynamics of witnessing process to truth in general terms (Chapter 3). Afer that, we shall look into the process of witnessing in the life and work of Jesus Christ (Chapter 4) and in the life of Christian believers, who may be described as “witnesses in the Witness” (Chapter 5). The latter provides the central applicative chapter of the book, and attempts to offer a series of fundamental elements for a “theology of evangelization.” One last chapter will be added with a view to ensuring that witnessing to truth may be considered a philosophically rigorous category (Chapter 6). In it, we shall refer to the four alternative understandings of truth mentioned in the above pages.

[^c1-98]: Cf. ibid.

[^c1-99]: Ibid.

[^c1-100]: Cf. K. Jaspers, _Von der Wahrheit_ (München: R. Piper, 1947) and _Der philosophische Glaube_ (München: R. Piper, 1948). On this, cf. J. Wahl, _La théorie de la vérité dans la philosophie de Jaspers_ (Paris: Centre de documentation universitaire, 1950); G. Masi, _La ricerca della verità in Karl Jaspers_ (Bologna: C. Zuffi, 1952); X. Tilliette, _Karl Jaspers: théorie de la vérité, métaphysique des chiffres, foi philosophique_ (Paris: Aubier-Montaigne, 1960).

[p. 21]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p21}

## 2. In Search of a Theological Epistemology: Mediations and Witnessing {#ocallaghan.wtd.2-in-search-of-a-theological-epistemology-mediations-and-wit}

There is one God, and one mediator between God and men, the man Christ Jesus. _(1 Tm 2:4 KJV)_

It should now be clear that a realist model of truth (defined in terms of correspondence between known being and the mind) offers several important advantages over an illuminist model for developing a rigorous theological epistemology. Three may be mentioned. First, the realist theory makes for a meaningful dialogue between theology, philosophy, and science.[^c2-1] Second, realism allows for a more faithful rendering of the basic tenets of the Christian economy of salvation, given the pervasive presence of the distinction between _apprehensio_ and _judicium_ in the dynamics of revelation, salvation, and conversion. In the third place, a realist epistemology takes into account the critical role of tangible, created, material mediations in the communication of Christian revelation and God’s grace, whose maximum expression may be found in the _incarnation_ of God’s word, and specifically, as we shall see, in the category of _Christian witness_. Let us consider them one by one.

### The Dialogue between Theology, Philosophy, and Science

A realist epistemological model makes it possible for theology to enter into dialogue with philosophy and science at an interdisciplinary (and transdisciplinary) level, where the correspondence model of truth, for obvious reasons, has generally prevailed. It may be said that realism has the advantage of _locating truth_, as it were, and access to it may be had through faith (theology), reason (philosophy), and empirical evidence (science). The issue is ample and complex, and we shall not look into it at the present moment.

[^c2-1]: Cf. O’Callaghan, _Children of God in the World_, 13–34.

[p. 22]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p22}

### The Time-Conditioned Dynamic of Revelation, Conversion, and Salvation

As we saw in the last chapter, an illuminist epistemological model accounts simultaneously and inseparably for both stages of the knowing process, the _apprehensio_ and the _judicium_: the same light that contains intelligibility provides truthfulness. Illuminist epistemology, besides, works on the basis of an understanding of truth as _eternal_. However, the realist theory distinguishes the stages of the knowing process from one another, at least in part. At an epistemological level, the distinction between _apprehensio_ and _judicium_ expresses both the union of the knower with the object known, as well as their separation. Besides, it takes into account the difference between the strictly psycho-organic and the spiritual aspects of the knowing process, the former shared to some degree by animals and plants, the latter predominant in humans and angels. In any case, the distinction may be found in three ambits in which all truth, deriving ultimately from God, may be known: the direct, the remote, and the indirect. Let us consider them one by one.

In the first place, God may communicate truth to us _directly_. God not only infuses concepts into the mind (_apprehensio_) but also provides a special light by which we can come to know that the said concepts are true (_judicium_), that they correspond to reality. According to Thomas Aquinas, this is the case, for example with the prophetic spirit[^c2-2] and with the _lumen gloriae_ that makes beatific vision possible.[^c2-3] In both cases, the content of knowledge (_apprehensio_) may come to be present in the mind through a variety of different instances (scientific, existential, shared, spiritual), but the light by which they are considered as certainly true (the _judicium_) is infused directly by God.[^c2-4] In this case, the _apprehensio_ and the _judicium_ may coincide materially to some degree, though not formally.

In the second place, we can speak of the _remote_ knowledge we have of God’s Word and mind. In that sense, we can come to know truth (and therefore the mind of God, the word of God) through abstraction from material reality in which God has “implanted” intelligibility through creation.[^c2-5] Afer all, God created the world and all it contains “through the Word” (Jn 1:2),[^c2-6] who is the “artisan” of the cosmos, and the divine imprint may be found in everything that exists. Again, the mind not only encounters certain species or concepts in reality (_apprehensio_), but makes a judgment (_judicium_) to the effect that a faithful (or true) coherence may been established between the object encountered and the concept attributed to it.

[^c2-2]: Cf. Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.II-II_, q. 173, aa.1–2.

[^c2-3]: Cf. Council of Vienne (1312), DH 875.

[^c2-4]: Speaking of the charism of prophecy, Aquinas says: “Now two things have to be considered in connection with the knowledge possessed by the human mind, namely the acceptance or representation of things, and the judgment of the things represented. Now things are represented to the human mind under the form of species: and according to the order of nature, they must be represented first to the senses, secondly to the imagination, thirdly to the passive intellect, and these are changed by the species derived from the phantasms, which change results from the enlightening action of the active intellect. . . . As to the judgment formed by the human mind, it depends on the power of the intellectual light. . . . Now the gif of prophecy confers on the human mind something which surpasses the natural faculty in both these respects, namely as to the judgment which depends on the inflow of intellectual light, and as to the acceptance or representation of things, which is effected by means of certain species. Human teaching may be likened to prophetic revelation in the second of these respects, but not in the first. For a man represents certain things to his disciple by signs of speech, but he cannot enlighten him inwardly as God does. . . . Hence it is evident that prophetic revelation is conveyed sometimes by the mere infusion of light, sometimes by imprinting species anew, or by a new coordination of species,” _S.T.II-II_, q. 173, a. 2c.

[^c2-5]: Cf. Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.I_, q. 2, a. 2; q. 12.

[^c2-6]: Cf. my work _God’s Gif of the Universe_, 75–95.

[p. 23]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p23}

In the third place, we can come to know the truth about God personally though _indirectly_ through divine revelation received by faith. According to Scripture, God reveals himself principally by means of the “word,” that of the prophets first, and definitively that of Jesus Christ, God’s own Word made man (Jn 1:14). This takes place through and in the Church, his Body, in the power of the Holy Spirit. Yet we may ask: can the distinction between _apprehensio_ and _judicium_ be applied in this process of communication? It would seem it can.

In effect, humans receive the “word of God,” through Christ or the prophets, in human form, by tangible words, gestures, and events.[^c2-7] The latter communicate ideas, reasonable ideas, plausible, and coherent concepts that stand in need of interpretation, that challenge humans, indeed, that invite conversion without actually achieving it. Thus far the _apprehensio_. But even if we were in a position to interpret correctly _the content_ of Christ’s teachings and those of the prophets, we must still ask a more fundamental question: _are their teachings true?_ They may be beautiful, inspiring, uplifing, coherent, rational. But do the concepts they elucidate correspond to reality? Are they true? Do they faithfully reflect the inner life of God and his saving design for humanity? Do they lead humans to conversion? Do they provide us with salvation? Do they oblige us in conscience? Are the teachings in question capable of putting our lives on the line? Of committing us? And if they do, by what means can we arrive at this conclusion? In real terms, this is the central question of this book. It is the question Augustine asks in his fundamental work _De vera religione_ we already referred to. There, the Bishop of Hippo does not enquire into religion in general, to more or less convincing or inspiring religious views, but rather attempts _to identify and locate true religion_ as distinct from false religion.[^c2-8]

It goes without saying that the question regarding the _content_ of revelation cannot be clearly distinguished from the one that concerns its _truthfulness_, the _apprehensio_ from the _judicium_. In fact, an illuminist account of revelation, faith, and reason hardly needs to distinguish them at all, for God simply illumines the mind by means of the actions and words of the prophet or of Christ, infusing both intelligibility and certainty at one and the same time. In that sense, supernatural and natural revelation would hardly be distinguishable from one another.

But a realist explanation of revealed truth will tend, to a greater or lesser degree, to distinguish the two moments. In effect, the _content_ of revealed truth, though mediated by Christ and the prophets, always points to its ultimate origin, who is God. “If I glorify myself,” Jesus said, “my glory is nothing; it is my Father who glorifies me, of whom you say that he is your God. . . . The works that I do

[^c2-7]: Cf. Vatican Council II, Const. _Dei Verbum_, nn. 2, 4, 14, 19.

[^c2-8]: Cf. Augustine, _De vera religione_, 24–54, on the relationship between faith and reason. In this work he leaned heavily on the philosophy (and epistemology) of Neoplatonism.

[p. 24]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p24}

in my Father’s name, they bear witness to me” (Jn 8:54; 10:25). However, its _truthfulness_ —the second moment—relates to Christ and the prophets in their tangible human actions and words, but in a different, more immediate, way. If some kind of distinction is not made between the _apprehensio_ and the _judicium_ (content and truthfulness) in the process of revelation and faith, a threefold risk may unfold.

The _first_ risk is one of merging the Mediator, Christ, with what is mediated, thus turning the humanity of the Mediator into a kind of idol through an extreme application of the _communicatio idiomatum_, ending up with a kind of monophysitism.
