The truth communicated, the content or _apprehensio_, relates fundamentally to God, the Word, not to the mediating reality, the humanity of Christ the Mediator (1 Tim 2:4), whereas the certainty we have of its truthfulness is linked more directly to the latter. The very structure of the figure of Christ as two natures in one divine Person leads us to distinguish between what is revealed and what reveals.

_Second_, there is the risk of not taking the complexity and harshness of the Christian conversion process fully into account.[^c2-9] The fact is that the word of God received in faith, although ultimately meant to give definitive meaning to human existence, in many ways complicates the lives of potential believers, imposing on them demanding conversion commitments while dissolving the vague reassurances that made their lives pleasantly, though superficially, tolerable before the conversion process began. The prospective believer may be quite capable of coming to know and appreciate _what_ Christians hold, what Christian teaching is, what the demands of Christian life would be, and to some degree they live according to such principles at the level of conviction. In his work _Jesus of Nazareth_, Benedict XVI explains that many people, upon studying the life of Jesus, are drawn to him and imitate him, without fully becoming his disciples, without accepting him as their Savior, without actually “following” him in faith.[^c2-10] They become, we might say, “cultural Christians.”

Nonetheless, those who encounter Christ must still face up to the personally demanding and painful question of whether or not all they are presented with is true, whether or not they are meant to identify themselves definitively with God’s word, or if they are capable of doing so. At the outset, the dynamic of conversion is based on hypotheses and reflection; in its development and final blossoming, it requires a profoundly personal and definitive assimilation and acceptance of God’s gif _as true for me_. Justin Martyr explains the process of his own conversion experience in the following simple terms: “I tried to learn

[^c2-9]: By way of example, see the conversion narrative of H. Ordway, _Not God’s Type: An Atheist Academic Lays Down Her Arms_ (San Francisco: Ignatius Press, 2014).

[^c2-10]: Cf. Benedict XVI, _Jesus of Nazareth,_ vol. 1, _passim._

[p. 25]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p25}

all possible doctrines, but I adhered only to the true doctrine of Christians.”[^c2-11] Thus the two stages—learning and adhering, knowing about revealed truth and identifying oneself personally with it in accepting its truthfulness, _fides quae_ and _fides qua_, _apprehensio_ and _judicium_ —to an important degree may be distinguished from one another.

The _third_ and last risk of not distinguishing sufficiently between _apprehensio_ and _judicium_ in the process of revelation and conversion involves what might be called the Christian “eschatological reserve.”[^c2-12] In effect, the definitive meaning and truthfulness of Christian revelation will only be at the disposal of humanity at the end of time.[^c2-13] Only then will God in Christ be seen to rule the earth, only then will God be “all in all” (1 Cor 15:28). This conviction leads us to distinguish between the objective certainty and substantial quality the faith, guaranteed by the Church under the guidance of the Holy Spirit, and the level of subjective certainty believers manage to attain in respect of the truthfulness of faith affirmations in this life.[^c2-14] The latter will come to fullness only in an eschatological context, when the saved will see God “face to face” (1 Cor 13:12). Doubtless, the _eschaton_ makes its presence felt even during the pilgrim life of the Church.

Wolfart Pannenberg argues that eschatological reserve involves Christian truth affirmations being perpetually provisional _content-wise_ (and not only _certainty-wise_) during our earthly sojourn.[^c2-15] However this position easily precludes the logic and reality of Christian martyrdom. The bottom line is that however much we can get to know on earth about the God we will see at the _eschaton_, we still live in the faith and hope of one who does not yet see nor possess, though we are certain through the light of faith and the power of hope _that_ it _will_ take place, without yet having completely at our disposal _what_ we believe and hope in.

### The Role of Created Mediations in Revelation and Faith

_In the third place_, only a realist epistemology has the possibility of satisfactorily accounting for the fact that salvation and revealed truth, according to Scripture, are received by humanity by means of _concrete created mediations_ not identified with their ultimate source, God. An illuminist theory of truth applied to revelation and faith need not _exclude_ created mediations, but it may well regard them as accessory, occasional, contingent, supporting, temporary elements, designed to overcome the darkness of the sinful human heart or the limits of

[^c2-11]: Justin Martyr, _Acta Martyrum_ 2,3.

[^c2-12]: Cf. J.-B. Metz, _Teology of the World_ (New York: Herder and Herder, 1971), 104.

[^c2-13]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation of the Apocalypse_ (Dublin: Four Courts, 2004), 16–18.

[^c2-14]: Cf. Francis (with Benedict XVI), Enc. _Lumen fidei_ (2013).

[^c2-15]: This thesis of Pannenberg is propounded in my study “Whose Future? Pannenberg’s Eschatological Verification of Theological Truth,” _Irish Theological Quarterly_ 66 (2001): 19–49, which also provides a critique of his position.

[p. 26]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p26}

created reality, yet ultimately unnecessary, superfluous, idolatry-inducing, and destined to disappear.[^c2-16] Or worse still, such an illuminist theory may see itself obliged to _include_ finite mediations as a necessary part of the process of revelation, as if the _kenosis_ of God that revelation involves were a necessary part of divine nature and action, as Hegel might suggest.[^c2-17]

In the Christian economy, the presence of mediations is undeniable. God’s word and love and salvific power come to us through the law, through the word of the prophets, through the people of the Covenant, and above all through the humanity, life, death, and Resurrection of Jesus Christ. The Lord’s insuperable, indeed unique, mediation is well attested to in Scripture.[^c2-18] Nonetheless the Church, Christ’s Body, and God’s people, itself is also a form of mediation, directly dependent on Christ in the Spirit, and in him, on the sacraments and the life of Christian believers. God communicates to us through a wide variety of created, mediating elements.

It is possible for us, potential believers, to obtain reasonable assurance of the truthfulness of the Gospel message—its “credibility”—in a variety of ways,[^c2-19] ways in which the transcendent truth of God is linked in one way or another with finite human categories. Theologians speak commonly of (1) the _praeambula fidei_, rational presuppositions that prepare the way for the commitment of faith; (2) the _ratio fidei_, that is the _a posteriori_ rational justification of the statements of faith (e.g. in respect of the reasonableness of the doctrine of the Blessed Trinity); (3) the _cognitio per connaturalitatem_, spontaneous or connatural knowledge of the truths of faith made possible by the affinity of created human nature to revelation;[^c2-20] and (4) the meaningfulness or relevance of Christian revelation to concrete human beings under three headings: as situated (immanent in the world), as indigent (in need of receiving a word of meaning), and as futureoriented (eschatological).

All in all, however, the arguments of credibility offer guarantees mainly of an anthropological kind, indicating that the word of Christ and the prophets is reasonable, coherent, fulfilling, comforting, meaningful. In an important sense however they do not go beyond humans themselves and their own experience; they may be considered what medieval theologians termed _rationes ex convenientiae_, appropriate reasons. They belong in the main to the ambit of what Luther would call the _theologia gloriae_, positive and affirming reasons, offering little scope for the so-called _theologia crucis_,[^c2-21] which presents and considers the power of God in the light of the defeat and destruction and death of his own Son, Jesus Christ.<sup>22</sup> Though helpful as supporting arguments, paths of credibility

The same position arises, in all probability, in the writings of Calvin. On the topic, cf. the study of J.F. Jansen, “I Cor 15:24–28 and the Future of Jesus Christ,” _Scottish Journal of Teology_ 40 (1987): 543–70.

Mais ces mots, médiateurs ou intercesseurs, ne prennent leur signification que dans un registre ontologique,” “Note sur l’attestation créatrice dans mon œuvre,” in Castelli (ed.), _La testimonianza_, 534.

[p. 27]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p27}

are not sufficient in themselves to provide a water-tight criterion—if such would ever be possible—for divine truth on which a Christian might responsibly and rationally commit his or her life, even to the point of martyrdom.

### The Need for Created Mediations

Still, we may ask: why did God wish to communicate his gifs and word and grace through created mediations in the first place?[^c2-23] In a hierarchical metaphysic, typical of Platonism, mediations of one kind or another, whether spiritual or cosmic, would be considered necessary to establish a relationship between the divine and the creature. Their existence reveals the operative limit of divine nature when faced with the intractable character of matter. But in the Christian vision of the world, it must be said, God stands in no absolute need of any mediator. He has created all things, each and every single creature, material and spiritual, directly, with his own “hands.”[^c2-24] At a personal (or interpersonal) level, God is perfectly capable of communicating directly, immediately, with all beings, and will do so definitively in heaven, when the faithful will see God “face to face” (1 Cor 13:12). The power of God knows no limit with respect to created beings. In the words of Augustine, God is “more intimate to me than I am to myself.”[^c2-25]

But if this is the case, why did God want certain creatures to enjoy the capacity of mediating his presence and saving power? Two reasons may be suggested. First of all, according to Thomas Aquinas, the difference and variety existing between creatures is the very thing that makes creation perfect in its own order. Without mediations, there would be no interaction between created beings, and the universe would be less perfect than it might otherwise be.[^c2-26] In the second place, by means of tangible mediations, free human will is moved, challenged, and elevated in the most human way possible.[^c2-27] Mediations between God and humans respect the intrinsic corporeity, historicity, and sociality of humans, as well as the concrete existential situation in which each one lives. In this way, God comes close to humans not only as Love but also with love, with a love that takes on the concrete human situation of each person in the deepest possible way.

Thus, humans are not forced or conditioned beyond their real possibilities. God speaks to them in their own, created, language. Their response to God is (or at least can be) fully human and authentic. Christians, according to Scripture, actually _believe_ in God, for he does not manifest himself directly to humans in

[^c2-23]: See P. O’Callaghan, “La mediazione cristiana,” in _Inaugurazione dell’Anno Accademico 1998–99 della Pontificia Università della Santa Croce_ (Roma: 1998), 83–105; _Children of God in the World_, 370–2.

[^c2-24]: See P. O’Callaghan, “L’incontro tra fede e ragione nella ricerca della verità’, in G. Maspero and M. Pérez de Laborda (eds.), _Fede e ragione: l’incontro e il cammino. In occasione del decimo anniversario dell’enciclica_ Fides et ratio, vol. 2 (Siena: Cantagalli, 2011), 35–59.” 44–9; _God’s Gif of the Universe_, 96–102.

[^c2-25]: Augustine, _Conf. III._

[^c2-26]: Cf. Thomas Aquinas, _S.T. I,_ q. 47, a. 1.

[^c2-27]: Cf. O’Callaghan, _Children of God in the World_, 370–4.

[p. 28]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p28}

this life (Jn 1:18). Rather, through the most simple, humble, and material human means, he makes himself present before and among them while avoiding to impose himself forcefully on the minds and free wills of humans, though he invites one and all to open their hearts to grace. In sum, mediations are not necessary for God but are perfectly appropriate for humans.

Let us return to this question: why does an all-powerful God, a God who has created the universe directly, galaxy by galaxy, planet by planet, stone by stone, atom by atom, person by person, make use of mediations to establish communion with humans? The purpose of mediation between the Creator and the creature corresponds to the non-reciprocal relationship between the transcendent Creator, who creates humans and invites them to share his inner life forever, on the one hand, and the spiritual creature, metaphysically contingent and sinful besides, on the other. At an objective and ontological level, this mediation makes the close and immediate union between humans and the Divinity possible. At a subjective or existential level, however, it is meant to make humans aware not only of the adoptive filiation God offers them as a gif but also of their own inherent and perpetual otherness and distinction with respect to the Creator. It reminds them besides of their incapacity to enter into communion with God through their own powers. In other words, Christian mediation expresses both the gratuity of all that exists outside God (the created world and grace) and the inherent otherness of humans in respect of their Creator and Savior.

Humans cannot enter into eternal union with God by returning to a primitive ideal knowledge of the divinity, lost—as Platonists would say—by the fall of spirit into matter, that is, by the elimination of mediations.[^c2-28] Nor is it possible to obtain such a union by forgetting the unpleasantness of life lived on earth, as a kind of anti-mundane spiritualism might suggest. The perpetual presence and evocation of created mediations also in the _eschaton_ will serve as a constant reminder to the saved not only of God’s refinement in wishing to attract humans to himself with an exquisite respect for their created condition, but also for their humanity, their freedom and personal history,[^c2-29] and for the fact that eternal beatitude is an unmerited pure gif.[^c2-30]

### Mediations, Immediacy, and Witness

An important issue arises in respect of the theology of mediation. On the one hand, mediations are distinct from the extremes they mediate: they are situated

[^c2-28]: See the document of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith on the role of mediations in Christian meditation, _Orationis formas_ (1989).

[^c2-29]: Irenaeus, in his reflection on the mediation of Christ, speaks of the familiarity that the Incarnation of the Word established between God and humans: _Adv. Haer. III_, 18:7. Maximus the Confessor uses the same terminology: Christ “is mediator on account of his familiarity with both poles,” _Epist_ <u>, 12. See also his</u> _Quaest. ad Talass_., 60.

[^c2-30]: On the question of “mediation” that applies to beatific vision, which is “face to face” with God, see my work _Christ our Hope_ (Washington (DC): Catholic University of America Press, 2012), 184–6.

[p. 29]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p29}

between God and humans; they express and confirm the otherness of God and creatures. On the other hand, mediations are meant to place the believer in direct, immediate, contact with God. How can otherness and immediacy coexist? Pierangelo Sequeri in his work _Il Dio affidabile_ (_Te Trustworthy God_) reflects on the notion that the Church serves as a kind of occasional mediation for Christian revelation, separated from its source and its destination, a _tertium quid_ between God who reveals and humans who receive revelation in faith. Yet he considers this model as mistaken. For mediations primarily _unite_ God (in Christ) with humans. While remaining in existence objectively, they establish an existential union between God and humans who may well be unconscious of any mediation. The union—or better, the communion—is the fruit of grace, it does not involve ontological amalgamation, but is experienced as direct and immediate.

Sequeri observes that the model of the Church as a _tertium quid_ between God and humans “ends up neutralizing the Christological singularity of the manifestation of God, leading it to the contingent area of a universal, pluralistic tradition. . . . But it evades the historical specificity of the relationship of man with the truth of his own salvation understood in terms of pure, preconstituted self-reference. . . . In this way the notion of _witnessing_ loses its intrinsic value, for it is introduced as a kind of _tertium_ between revelation and faith. The point of reference for this relationship is the individual who is protagonist of his own self-understanding and not the historical form of mediation.”[^c2-31]

It is interesting to observe that at this very point Sequeri introduces the category of “witnessing” which we shall consider in the coming chapter.

[^c2-31]: This idea is developed extensively in P. Sequeri’s work _Il Dio affidabile. Saggio di teologia fondamentale_ (Brescia: Queriniana, 1996), especially 557–609. Among other texts, the following is worth noting. “La fede vive una sua intrinseca immediatezza—precisamente nel senso indicato da una corretta teologia della mistica—proprio in quanto essa è già in se stessa una effettiva e personale esperienza della relazione diretta con il Signore. . . . Il senso teologico che abbiamo assegnato alla immediatezza ha reso già riconoscibile il suo insuperabile intreccio con il profilo della mediazione testimoniale, da intendersi appunto come oggettività ecclesiastica della tradizione. . . . La Chiesa ha _la massima cura di non esser confusa_ con il suo Signore: ed esibisce senza gelosia la splendida bellezza della grazia di Lui con la stessa semplicità con la quale ostenta tutte le cicatrici della propria debolezza. . . . Nonostante i problemi da chiarire ritengo che sia vantaggioso attestarsi sul guadagno dell’idea di mediazione e sullo sviluppo della forma testimoniale come prospettiva della _fides_ che diventa _ecclesiastica_.” Were the Church to actually take Christ’s place as mediator, this would neutralize “la singolarità cristologica della manifestazione di Dio, riconducendola al segmento congiunturale di una tradizione universale e pluralistica del senso, analogamente disponibile allo scopo. Ma elude anche la specificità storica della relazione dell’uomo con la verità della propria salvezza: intesa in termini di pura autoreferenzialità precostituita, che presuppone come sempre già dato e mai realmente offerto alla libertà quel rapporto. In tal modo perde il suo valore intrinseco il concetto di testimonianza: che si introduce come un _tertium_ (più o meno incomodo) fra la rivelazione e la fede. La sede di questa relazione è appunto l’individuo protagonista della propria autocomprensione e non la forma storica della mediazione,” _Il Dio affidabile_, 563.567.573f.592.

[p. 31]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p31}

## 3. The Dynamics of Witnessing to Truth {#ocallaghan.wtd.3-the-dynamics-of-witnessing-to-truth}

As was said earlier, the word of God is communicated to humans through Christ and the prophets as a human word. God is present in the word, certainly, though indirectly. We do not hear (or see) God as such, but rather we encounter human words and gestures that speak to us _about_ God. This brings us to ask ourselves not so much (or not only) about the content, consistency, beauty, usefulness, or immediate appeal of God’s word, but _whether or not it is true_: that is, whether or not it obliges us to assent to its truthfulness in conscience, whether or not it is capable of converting us from the aesthetic to the true, from the attractive to the vital, from the inspiring to the committed, from the ethereal to the vocational. In Scripture, the teacher or prophet consistently claims that what is taught is not his own teaching, but rather reflects faithfully and expresses truly the word of another, specifically, God’s own word. In that sense, the prophet or teacher is distinct from the message; that is, to gloss the famous expression of Marshall McLuhan, the messenger is _not_ the message, but the mediator of the message. And this is where the process of _witnessing_ arises, for the dynamic of witnessing is the dynamic of truth, of truth communicated. One gives witness to truth, no more and no less, which may well oblige us in conscience.[^c3-1] The notion of witnessing, while having important juridical and philosophical connotations,[^c3-2] is deeply linked with both revelation and faith.[^c3-3]

The one who bears witness to revelation invites us to accept the words proffered by Christ and the prophets as true because they claim to have seen God, or at least to have received his revelation for humanity. As a result, they consider themselves entitled (and urged on) to become witnesses of divine truth to others. They become “mediators” who establish direct union with the divine. However,

Nabert, _Essai sur le mal_ (orig., 1955) (Paris: Cerf, 1997). In the volume edited by Castelli, cf. also the studies of S. Breton, “Philosophie et témoignage,” 189–205; E. La temporalità della testimonianza,” _Annali di Studi Religiosi_ 2 (2001): 227–37; J. Lackey and Sosa, E. (eds.), _Te Epistemology of Testimony_ (Oxford; New York: Clarendon Press; Oxford University Press, 2006). Later on we shall encounter further studies of Ricoeur, as well as those of Marcel, Lévinas, Marion etc.

[^c3-3]: On the theological side of witnessing, see the bibliography at the beginning of Chapter 4.

[p. 32]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p32}

whereas the role of the _judicium_ in the knowing process is played for Christ and the prophets directly by divine illumination (they perceive its truthfulness directly in God), for believers it is played to a significant degree by the process of tangible witnessing undertaken by another believer. Actual or potential believers come to take the witness’s word as true, sharing in the truthfulness of God’s own word, because the witnessing process is considered to warrant this. So we have to consider what the dynamic of witnessing involves. It is a “truth” issue; in fact, as we shall see, it is a life-and-death issue.

The fact is that humans spontaneously tend to accept the word of others at face value, even though the claim being made might appear exorbitant or even contradictory.[^c3-4] Afer all, trust is con-natural to humans.[^c3-5] Still, at a more reflexive and rigorous level, we may say that the acceptance of the word of one who claims to be telling the truth involves the conviction that something of the witness’s own being and life is in some way at stake. For the witness puts his or her life on the line. At times it is said: “don’t shoot the messenger,” just because he brings bad news. But the messenger knows that a price may have to be paid for the message to be received, especially if it is bad news. The witness ofen perceives himself as one who has been constituted as a recipient and depository of a truth that _must_ be passed on to others, even at the cost of life, honor, and identity.[^c3-6] Yet he is confronted with an important existential dilemma: in what way can he _convince_ those who listen to him of the truth he holds as certain? Is it fair to do so? Perhaps this is simply “my truth.” What reasons can be given so that the witness may not be considered as a manipulator, as self-deluded, or as simply mistaken . . . especially when the truths in question are sublime and spiritual, difficult to understand, or highly committal? Could it not be that the witness is attempting to seek some form of personal advantage, or excessive protagonism, or hidden gratification? Perhaps it could be that he is abusing the natural trust that people put in others as a whole or in him specifically because of his virtues, talents, or presentability.[^c3-7]

### Recent Understandings of Witnessing

The topic of witnessing has acquired particular importance in recent decades, at a philosophical, theological, and pastoral level.[^c3-8] Pope Paul VI, in his 1975 Apostolic Exhortation _Evangelii Nuntiandi_, cites the following text: “Modern man listens more willingly to witnesses than to teachers, and if he does listen to teachers, it is because they are witnesses.”[^c3-9] Pope John Paul II says the same thing

[^c3-5]: Cf. O’Callaghan, _Children of God in the World_, 307–9. On the relationship between faith and human trust, see G. Lingua, “Le forme dell’affidarsi,” in P. Coda and C. Hennecke (eds.), _La fede: Evento e promessa_ (Rome: Città Nuova, 2000), 73–86.

[^c3-6]: During the early centuries of Christian life, a distinction arose between martyrs and confessors which was not found at the earliest stages of Christianity. On the distinction between the two, cf. L. Scheffczyk, “La professione di fede: _confessio_ e _martyrium_,” _Communio (ed. italiana)_ 143 (1995): 24–34, here 32.

[^c3-7]: On the theological roots of abuse and its relationship with mediations, cf. my study “Coscienza, mediazione e abuso,” in I. Vigorelli, J. Pujol, and F. Insa (eds.), _Autorità e mediazione. Le relazioni asimmetriche nella Chiesa_ (Roma: Edusc, 2023), 63–72. See also Chapter 6 below: “Is ‘witnessing’ a Philosophically Rigorous Category?”

[^c3-8]: On the phenomenon of witnessing in Church documents since Vatican Council II, cf. J.M. Prades López, “La nozione di testimonianza nei documenti della Chiesa a partire dal Concilio Vaticano II,” in Granados and O’Callaghan, _Parola e testimonianza_, 355–411.

[^c3-9]: Paul VI, Apost. Exh. _Evangelii Nuntiandi_ (1975), n. 41, citing his _Address to the Members of the Consilium de Laicis_ (2 October, 1974) to lay movements.

[p. 33]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p33}

in the encyclical _Redemptoris Missio,_ citing _Evangelii Nuntiandi_: “People today put more trust in witnesses than in teachers, in experience than in teaching, and in life and action than in theories. The witness of a Christian life is the first and irreplaceable form of mission: Christ, whose mission we continue, is the ‘witness’ par excellence (Rv 1:5; 3:14) and the model of all Christian witness.”[^c3-10] Pope Francis repeats the same notion in the 2013 apostolic exhortation _Evangelii Gaudium_: “Today too, people prefer to listen to witnesses: they ‘thirst for authenticity’ and ‘call for evangelizers to speak of a God whom they themselves know and are familiar with, as if they were seeing him.’”[^c3-11]

Paul VI goes on to explain the witnessing process in greater detail:

Above all the Gospel must be proclaimed by witness. Take a Christian or a handful of Christians who, in the midst of their own community, show their capacity for understanding and acceptance, their sharing of life and destiny with other people, their solidarity with the efforts of all for whatever is noble and good. Let us suppose that, in addition, they radiate in an altogether simple and unaffected way their faith in values that go beyond current values, and their hope in something that is not seen and that one would not dare to imagine. Through this wordless witness these Christians stir up irresistible questions in the hearts of those who see how they live: Why are they like this? Why do they live in this way? What or who is it that inspires them? Why are they in our midst? Such a witness is already a silent proclamation of the Good News and a very powerful and effective one. Here we have an initial act of evangelization. The above questions will be the first to be asked by people to whom Christ has never been proclaimed, or baptized people who do not practice, or people who live as nominal Christians but according to principles that are in no way Christian, or people who are seeking, and not without suffering, something or someone whom they sense but cannot name.[^c3-12]

Still, Paul VI makes it clear that the simple act of witnessing, of merely behaving well, of giving good example, “always remains insufficient, because even the finest witness will prove ineffective in the long run if it is not explained, justified—what Peter called always having ‘your answer ready for people who ask you the reason for the hope that you all have’—and made explicit by a clear and unequivocal proclamation of the Lord Jesus.”[^c3-13] This exhortation of Paul VI has been widely and favorably received in recent decades.

Still, the theologian Giuseppe Angelini, in a brief yet important work published in 2008, observed that in recent years the concept of witnessing has lost a lot of the power and impact it once had and should continue having. A domesticated

[^c3-13]: Ibid., 22, citing 1 Pt 3:15.

[p. 34]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p34}

and irenic view of the witness and the witnessing process has emerged and consolidated.[^c3-14] In the present view of things, he says, “the witness is one who does not presume to teach, who does not trust words, but just acts . . . offering a sign of the truth of the gospel through one’s life, especially through practical forms of solidarity with those in need.”[^c3-15] Less interest is taken in the relationship between humans and truth. “The renewed fortune of the category of witnessing has come about in the post-conciliar period, which clearly privileges the program of ‘dialogue’ and reconciliation with the modern world . . . in which the idea of witnessing is bent to the need of this dialogue.”[^c3-16] It is frequently held that “the Christian does not assert or lay claim to truth, but just attests. . . . Witnesses do not teach, much less do they prescribe: they simply communicate their own experience.”[^c3-17] We ofen hear Christians saying that we are meant “to give witness to the invisible,” in the context of a less aggressive apostolate and a predominance of interior religion.[^c3-18] Witnessing above all would involve “the removal of conflict.”[^c3-19]

Angelini shows, however, that witnessing in the New Testament—especially in John and Acts—is clearly agonistic.[^c3-20] John’s is a “gospel of love,” certainly, but it is also deeply polemical, because it asks the unavoidable question: “who is right!”[^c3-21] The _Logos_ has become flesh and visible and touchable and imposing, provoking a response. Jesus tells us: “I tell you, every one who acknowledges me before men, the Son of man also will acknowledge before the angels of God; but he who denies me before men will be denied before the angels of God” (Lk 12:8f.). Witnessing has come to be understood, the author says, in a subjectivistic way, in terms of authenticity and sincerity, “the fidelity of the subject to itself.”[^c3-22] The subject as the ultimate source of truth has become the holy cause of modernity, he says. And witnessing has followed suit. “The witness is considered and appreciated as the subject which speaks faithfully about itself, that confesses and does not hide under the shade of social convention.”[^c3-23] Whereas in the New Testament, Angelini says, witnessing “is configured always as a denunciation . . . The Christian presence in the world takes on the form of witnessing on the basis of the fact that humans keep truth hidden: that of Christ, certainly, but even beforehand, that which is at the root of their life and of human relationships.”[^c3-24]

### The Characteristics of the Witnessing Process

Avery Dulles lists the following characteristics of the witnessing process: convergence, firmness, novelty, transformation, and illuminative power.[^c3-25] In

[^c3-14]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 5.

[^c3-15]: Ibid., 9, 12.

[^c3-16]: Ibid., 17.

[^c3-17]: Ibid., 27.

[^c3-18]: Cf. ibid., 33ff. Here Angelini cites the text of J. Loew, _Comme s’il voyait l’invisible: un portrait de l’apôtre d’aujourd’hui_ (Paris: Cerf, 1970).

[^c3-19]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 195.

[^c3-20]: Cf. ibid., 12.

[^c3-21]: Ibid., 13.

[^c3-22]: Ibid., 96.

[^c3-23]: Ibid.

[^c3-24]: Ibid., 36.

[^c3-25]: Cf. Dulles, “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 22.

[p. 35]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p35}

effect, the Christian message witnessed to is unitary and thus _convergent_, because it is centered on Jesus Christ. Witnesses besides proclaim the word of God with unwavering assurance and _firmness_. The message is new, ofen disconcerting, thus _novel_: it is the Gospel. It _transforms_ peoples’ lives, giving them a power that is not their own. It fills them with _light_ and promotes a civilization of peace and love.

However, as a rule, the witness is not satisfied simply to declare the truth he or she is convinced of. To convince those who listen, witnesses freely commit their being, honor, and integrity.[^c3-26] In an extreme case, witnesses may be prepared to give their lives, to accept death itself, in order to obtain acceptance of their personal truth-conviction as a universal truth.[^c3-27] Witnessing is committal; it is ofen a path of no return; it is hard, as truth is hard. As Blaise Pascal said: “I only believe in the stories of witnesses who are prepared to be butchered.”[^c3-28] In the words of Paul Ricoeur, “witnessing . . . belongs to the tragic destiny of truth.”[^c3-29] In other words, the witness (the English translation of the Greek _martyrein_) must be in a position to go to the extreme of martyrdom, which is the quintessence of witnessing.[^c3-30] _Martyria_ has a paradigmatic value for Christians.[^c3-31] By contrast, as Ricoeur wryly remarks, in a legal trial in which truth is being sought “it is not the witness who risks his life, but the accused.”[^c3-32]

The simple fact of suffering, and suffering to the point of death, may well provide a _sincere_ witness, but it does not necessarily provide a _trustworthy_ one. Because witnessing is not about _me_, but about _truth_, and therefore refers to God. Above and beyond the sacrifice one is prepared to accept, giving witness is fundamentally an act of love, of self-giving, of faith in Christ. Speaking of Christian witness, Jean-Louis Leuba writes: “The martyr is not a witness because he suffers, but because by his word he is a witness and confessor to the suffering Christ.”[^c3-33] Witnessing, no matter what form it assumes, is at heart a _free act_ of proclaiming the truth, in which the witness knowingly risks losing his life, in whole or in part.[^c3-34] That is to say, witnessing is primarily a gratuitous, not a pragmatic act.[^c3-35] It constitutes a provocation, not a calculation. The suffering involved, such as it is, is subordinated to freedom, is directed by freedom.

Karl Rahner characterizes the witness in the following terms: “a man endowed with freedom and having power over his own self; the possibility of disposing of himself in an ultimate and ‘absolute’ sense so far as the concrete conditions of human life are concerned. . . . In witnessing man exercises his freedom at the most ultimate level of which he is capable of directing himself.”[^c3-36] Claude Geffré observes that “witnessing refers always to the freedom of the

[^c3-26]: “Le témoin est capable de souffrir et de mourir pour ce qu’il croit. Quand l’épreuve de la conviction devient le prix e la vie, le témoin change de nom: il s’appelle un martyr,” P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 42f.

[^c3-27]: “Il martire accetta la morte perché non può rifiutare di testimoniare la sua fede, la quale fede è la fedeltà alla _societas_ di quelli che sono _naturaliter_ cristiani (e quindi degli stessi persecutori), anche se non essendolo coscientemente e perseguendo ciò che ritengono essere la verità (la verità della legge in buona fede), non possono accettare il suo rifiuto, una testimonianza contraria,” E. Castelli, “Il problema della testimonianza,” 28.

[^c3-28]: “Je ne crois que les histoires dont les témoins se feraient égorger,” B. Pascal, _Pensées_ (ed. Brunschvig), n. 593.

[^c3-29]: “Le témoignage est aussi l’engagement d’un cœur pur et un engagement jusqu’à la mort. Il appartienne au destin tragique de la vérité,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 43.

[^c3-30]: On the question of martyrdom, cf. the bibliography cited by R. Fisichella, _La rivelazione: evento e credibilità_, 8 ed (Bologna: Dehoniane, 2002), 549. Interesting the title of the classic work on martyrdom by E. Peterson, _Zeuge der Wahrheit_ (Leipzig: Jakob Hegner, 1937), “witness to truth.” On the question of the relationship between truth and martyrdom, cf. C. Noce, _Il martirio_, 144–8. “Sussiste un nesso stretto tra il fatto che la rivelazione della verità del vangelo conosca il suo compimento sulla croce e il fatto che la testimonianza assuma di necessità la forma di _martirio,_” 118.

[^c3-31]: Cf. Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 113. N. Brox has demonstrated conclusively that “the Old Testament and later Judaism are excluded as the place of origin of the title of martyr,” as it developed in the early history of Christianity, and also that “no real equivalence between martyrs and prophets . . . can be found,” Z _euge und Märtyrer. Untersuchungen zur frühchristlichen Zeugnis-Terminologie_ (München: Kösel, 1961), 172.

[^c3-32]: “Dans un procès, ce n’est pas le témoin qui risque sa vie, mais l’accusé,” P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 43.

[^c3-33]: “Ce n’est pas parce qu’il souffre que le martyr est témoin, mais c’est parce qu’il a été, par sa parole, témoin et confesseur du Christ qu’il souffre,” J.-L. Leuba, “La notion chrétienne de témoignage,” in E. Castelli (ed.), _La testimonianza,_ 309–16, 315. “È la fede confessante, non la morte cruenta il principio della testimonianza,” P.A. Sequeri, _Il Dio affidabile_, 586.

[^c3-34]: According to Ellul, the act of witnessing is a choice, and “ce choix le sépare évidemment des autres hommes, mais il est l’acte de sa liberté,” in “Témoignage et société technicienne,” 442. The only thing that is common about witnesses, in all contexts and cultures, “c’est que, décision de la liberté, il remet en question le système et fait éclater le groupe,” ibid., 444.

[^c3-35]: V. Mathieu, “Testimoniare attraverso l’assurdo,” 169f. C. Geffré explains suggestively that witnessing notably dignifies the individual: “le témoignage conteste tous les _césarismes_, à commencer par le ‘césarisme de la science’,” “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 291f. See Chapter 6.

[^c3-36]: K. Rahner, “Theological Observations on the Concept of Witness,” in _Teological Investigations_, vol. 13 (New York: Crossroad, 1965), 153f. and 155.

[p. 36]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p36}

witness, to his intentionality, and thus to the one before whom he witnesses.”[^c3-37] In fact, the act of witnessing is _a response to an interior call_, it constitutes the fulfillment of a mission. Besides, it attempts _to provoke a response_ in those who receive it.[^c3-38] Thus the witness is never a solitary figure: he assumes the role of a mediator, a mediator situated between the one who sent him and the one to whom he offers witness. In fact, Rahner says that “witnessing is directed to one’s fellow. . . . Witness as such has a public and political character. . . . The public and social character of this witness is, by a special inter-involvement, that of the ‘world’ and that of the ‘Church.’”[^c3-39]

Perhaps we may say that the person in the free act of witnessing (the witness) attempts to establish or reinforce a threefold bond of trust (might we say an _adaequatio_?) with:

- The one before whom he witnesses (the “receiver”);

- The living origin of the truth and power witnessed to (we may call it the “source”); and

- The concrete “truth object” witnessed to.

In respect of this threefold bond, let us consider three moments in the process of witnessing, which later on we shall apply in turn to Jesus Christ the witness (Chapter 4) and to Christian witnesses (Chapter 5).

### The Bond Between the Witness and the Receiver

In the _first_ place, by sacrificing (or being prepared to sacrifice) her life or an important aspect of it (family, children, time, reputation, career, honor, property, etc.), the witness may well _convince_ those who receive the testimony of being fully sincere in her convictions, of being subjectively convinced that she is in the right. A public, exterior profession derives from an interior faith conviction.[^c3-40] The word of the witness is “self-implicating to the very end, even to death,”[^c3-41] and attempts to establish a vital relationship between the witness and the receiver(s). The witness may, of course, be mistaken in his or her conviction, perhaps in good faith. For that reason, observes Paul Ricoeur, “a cause which has martyrs is not necessarily a just cause.”[^c3-42] Still, the communication of conviction does overcome, to some degree at least, the receiver’s initial reluctance in accepting the truth witnessed to.[^c3-43] It serves

[^c3-37]: C. Geffré, “Le témoignage renvoie toujours à la liberté du témoin, à son intentionnalité signifiante et donc à un destinataire,” “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 291.

[^c3-38]: On the anthropological aspects of witnessing, cf. P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza. Verità di Dio e libertà dell’uomo_ (Milano: Paoline, 2002), 61–110.

[^c3-39]: K. Rahner, “Theological Observations on the Concept of Witness,” 164.

[^c3-40]: “Le sens du témoignage semble alors inversé; le mot ne désigne plus une action de parole, le rapport oral d’un témoin oculaire sur un fait auquel il a assisté; le témoignage est l’action elle-même en tant qu’elle atteste dans l’extériorité l’homme intérieur lui-même, sa conviction, sa foi. Et pourtant il n’y a pas rupture de sens, au point que les deux usages extrêmes seraient devenus de purs homonymes,” P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 43.

[^c3-41]: According to S. Breton, the word of the witness “est auto-implicative, et parfois au plus haut degré, c’est-à-dire jusqu’à la mort,” “Philosophie et témoignage,” 191. “Le témoin parle. Et sa parole est une action. Il importe donc de définir et cette parole et cette action,” ibid., 193.

[^c3-42]: “Une cause qui a des martyrs n’est pas nécessairement une cause juste,” P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 43.

[^c3-43]: In the words of Dulles, “accepting what they [witnesses] have been taught as the word of God, they proclaim it with unwavering assurance. The firmness of their conviction is an indication that God has spoken to them with proofs of power,” “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 22.

[p. 37]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p37}

at least as a provocation to thought and enquiry, a pressing invitation to consideration and evaluation, indeed perhaps to acceptance or rejection. In that sense, “there is all the difference in the world between the role of an informer and that of a witness. . . . The witness commemorates an objective order of truth.”[^c3-44] “Supreme witness of faith is given by handing over one’s life, not just by governing it.”[^c3-45] In real terms, witnessing involves an interpersonal encounter between the witness and the receiver.

### The Bond Between the Source of the Truth Witnessed to and the Witness

This brings us to the _second_ stage of the witnessing process, in respect of the origin or source of the truth witnessed to. The dynamic of witnessing involves not only a conviction which might well be mistaken, individualistic, or narcissistic, but also _vindication_. In effect, the witness is aware that reason alone is not enough to communicate truth. Neither is conviction alone, no matter how intense, even to the point of self-sacrifice, capable of carrying the day. It is as if the witness needs to throw himself back on a superior, unquestionable instance, seen as an incontrovertible source of truth and power, establishing with the latter a kind of trust-bond.[^c3-46] _Unus testis, nullus testis,_ says the classical aphorism;[^c3-47] the witness is aware that their own voice is not enough, and instinctively seeks external collaboration from multiple sources to support his truth claims. When the truth of an affirmation is called into question, it is common for one who holds it to say, perhaps improperly: “I swear to God,” “I swear on my forebears,” “I make my pledge on the Bible,” or something of a kind. Ricoeur observes that “witnessing does not belong to the witness. It proceeds from an absolute initiative, in both its origin and content.”[^c3-48] This is a key element of the witnessing process. The source or foundation of truth is called upon, albeit implicitly, to eventually proclaim and confirm the truthfulness of what the witness has declared, to vindicate the witness’s veracity. In other words, just as the act of witnessing involves _trust_ both in those to whom the truth is directed and in the Source from which it ultimately derives, this selfsame trust seeks to prompt a _response_ not only from the receiver but also from the Source. In effect, the witness _mediates_ between the source and the receiver, _trusting_ in one and the other and seeking a confirmative _response_ from both.

[^c3-44]: “Il y a là toute la différence entre le rôle de l’ _informateur_ et celui du _témoin,”_ C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 293. “Il témoigne pour célébrer un ordre objectif de vérité,” ibid. Castelli affirms: Anche i dèmoni sanno . . . ma non possono testimoniare,” in “I significati della testimonianza,” 29. Aristotle, in his explanation of witnessing at a trial, “déplace la crédibilité du témoignage sur celle du témoin, et révèle un trait important sur lequel nous allons revenir: la qualité du témoin, sa bonne foi, qu’une logique du témoignage ne peut suppléer,” P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 42. Karl Rahner observes: “If the ‘witness’ were concerned only with a something, then it would amount merely to a piece of information delivered by an expert on some specific topic, or an eye-witness’s account of some occurrence which he had observed. In other words, it would amount to evidence in a sense which is (ultimately) forensic,” K. Rahner, “Theological Observations on the Concept of ‘Witness’,” 152–67, 153.

[^c3-45]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 112.

[^c3-46]: “Tout discours qui a la prétention de viser le vrai, doit obligatoirement se soumettre à la souveraineté d’un sujet universel, gardien idéal et idéal de toute vérité. Une sujet particulier n’atteint le vrai que dans l’exacte mesure où sa position coïncide avec celle du Tiers-témoin,” A. de Waelhens, “Ambiguïté de la notion de témoignage,” 469.

[^c3-47]: The aphorism is to be found in the _Codex Iustinianus_ 4, 20, 9, ed. P. Krüger (Berlin: Weidman, 1959). A. de Waelhens observes: “La critique du témoignage consiste donc à rechercher systématiquement une éventuelle contradiction,” “Ambiguïté de la notion de témoignage,” 467. If the witness has only his _own_ faith, then nothing would be achieved. “Son témoignage devra obligatoirement être corroboré par d’autres témoignages concordants,” ibid _._

[^c3-48]: “Le témoignage n’appartient pas au témoin. Il procède d’une initiative absolue, quant à son origine et quant à son contenu,” in P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 44. R. Guardini makes the following observation: “Tutte le cose attestano se stesse come reali ed essenziali: ma allo stesso tempo lasciano intuire che non sono l’elemento definitivo, sono piuttosto punti di passaggio, attraverso i quali si manifesta l’elemento veramente definitivo e autentico: forme espressive che lo manifestano,” _Religione e rivelazione_ (Milano: Vita e Pensiero, 2001), 23.

[p. 38]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p38}

### The Bond Between the Truth Witnessed to and the Witness

And so a _third_ element of the witnessing process may be considered, this time in respect of the concrete truth object being witnessed to. The truth witnessed to is, and should be, _closely linked to_ or even _identified with the witness._[^c3-49] This is so because the truth witnessed to is a truth involving life itself that the witness is prepared to sacrifice. In that sense, we referred above to a _third bond_, between the person of the witness and the truth they proclaim. The principle is found at the heart of the Christian gospel, which repeatedly prompts believers to “practice what they preach” (Mt 23:2). The dilemma existing between the person and their proclamation may be found when Christ himself says to the disciples, in respect of the leaders of the people: “do what they say but not what they do” (Mt 23:3). That is to say, words only go so far; for corresponding deeds are meant to confirm them and reveal them. The alternative, as we know, is incurrence in hypocrisy, repeatedly and unreservedly condemned by Jesus.[^c3-50]

The linguist Ferdinand de Saussure contrasts the phenomenon of a technically perfect yet contentless communication, with the phenomenon of the convergence of the communicator and the message communicated in the process of witnessing.[^c3-51] Likewise Hermann Pottmeyer speaks of the coincidence between the witness and what is witnessed to.[^c3-52] Karl Rahner confirms this by saying that witnessing is authenticated by itself and no other exterior instance.[^c3-53]

This identification may be extended likewise to the one who is witnessed to (which we call the “receiver”),[^c3-54] in that the truth witnessed to is not a mere individualistic conviction of the witness who attempts to turn others into epistemological “clones” of himself, but indeed a universal, binding truth destined to be shared with other people. This affirmation just made requires further explanation.

The witness may seek corroboration of the message communicated in three possible ways. _Firstly_, through a _reasoning_ process by which the receiver reflects on what has been said and attempts to establish and be convinced by its rational coherency. In accepting what people tell us, we ofen respond saying “I understand,” “I know what you are saying,” “that makes sense.” There is no transfer of new knowledge and truth here, but just an acceptance or confirmation of what one already knows.

_Alternatively_, corroboration of the message witnessed to may be sought in the common, parallel declaration of one or more witnesses who numerically

[^c3-49]: In the process of witnessing, the witness is situated, at one and the same time, above and beyond the receiver, while sharing a common existence with him. Cf. the observations made by J. Ellul on this paradox, commenting the acclamation of some priests who said that “nous ne voulons plus être des témoins parmi les hommes, mais des hommes avec les hommes,” in “Témoignage et société technicienne,” 441.

[^c3-50]: Cf. M. Silva (ed.), NIDNTTE 4, 562–4, s.v. ὑποκρίνομαι.

[^c3-51]: On the conjunction of the signifier and what is signified, cf. P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 105ff., who refers to the works of F. de Saussure, especially the latter’s _Course in General Linguistics_ (Glasgow: Fontana Press/W. Collins, 1977) that comment on the modern phenomenon of a perfect yet contentless communication. Thus also Dulles, “New Testament testimony is early, multiple, and convergent. The Evangelists come from different communities and give different perspectives on the life of Christ, yet they agree on substantials. The New Testament contains many theologies but only one vision of Christ and the Christian life. All the accounts present Jesus as the Son who speaks and acts with sovereign majesty, who lays down his life for the redemption of the world, and who rises triumphant from the grave. They agree that he held forth a revolutionary ideal of human life, exalting poverty, humility, love of neighbor, and patience in suffering as the way to eternal salvation,” “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 22.

[^c3-52]: Cf. B. Körner, “‘Einswerden von Zeugnis und Zeuge in der Gemeinschaf der Zeugen’. Erkenntnis im Glauben und Spiritualität der Gemeinschaf,” _Path_ 5 (2006): 55–71.

[^c3-53]: Rahner states that the act of witnessing may not be externally or independently authenticated: “The very nature of the reality involved is such as not to admit of any justification or authentication of the witness from without. Witness involves a total human act (albeit one that is specified with reference to the world at large). And this can neither be authenticated by any factor outside itself, nor consist of a combination of individual acts or elements such that this combination could be justified by the individual elements making it up,” “Theological Observations on the Concept of Witness,” 166.

[^c3-54]: Speaking of the one who receives witness, Martinelli describes “la testimonianza come segno che acquista il suo carattere significativo quando offre all’altro ciò di cui - l’altro è in ricerca,” _La testimonianza_, 109.

[p. 39]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p39}

reinforce and confirm the truth claim being made. In this case, however, both the witnessing process and truth itself are presented as separate and separable instances, both of them on the weak side. In effect, the need for a multiplicity of witnesses may well reveal the weakness of the witnessing process, for the arrival of each new witness merely shows up the unreliable and provisional character of the previous ones. In any case, the truth proclaimed and the person of the witness are presented as separate, non-reinforcing instances. Besides, the witness could be “anybody,” even a paid informer. Clearly, the bond between the witness and the truth he proclaims remains tenuous. In fact, _truth_ itself is presented as weak, because it stands in need of a multiplicity of witnesses (as we saw when considering the consensus theory of truth).[^c3-55] In other words, objectivized truth is sought at the expense of the person who witnesses to it.[^c3-56] In fact, it should be enough for one person to witness to the truth for it to be accepted.

_Finally_, in line with the second stage of witnessing described above (vindication of the witness by the ultimate Source of truth), such a vindication may consist of the restitution to the witness of what was once sacrificed for the sake of the truth communicated (life, good name, property, reputation, family), in that way confirming and proclaiming the truth for which the sacrifice was originally made. This modality has the “advantage” of vindicating truth _in and through_ the restitution to the witness of what he willingly forfeited with a view to proclaiming that truth. In this case, besides, if the truth witnessed to is more or less identifiable with the person and identity of the witness, then the vindication of the witness and the proclamation of truth merge with one another, and further reinforce the trustworthiness of the witness.

To sum up this chapter on the dynamics of witnessing, we may say the following: the person in the free act of witnessing (the witness) attempts to establish or reinforce a triple-bond of trust: with the one before whom he witnesses (the receiver); with the origin of the truth and power being witnessed to (the Source); and with the concrete truth object witnessed to, because truth witnessed to is, or at least should be, _closely linked_ or even _identified_ with the person who witnesses.

This is the case in a singular way with Jesus Christ, whom the New Testament calls “the faithful (or true) Witness” (Rev 1:5; cf. 3:15).

[^c3-55]: Cf. Chapter 1, 4.

[^c3-56]: P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 62, speaks of two inadequate forms of witnessing: one in which a kind of neutral truth, unconnected with the person, is handed on, the other in which all objective truth is obliterated by subjectivism: “il primo modello sembrerebbe fornire un’immagine di testimonianza che solo tengenzialmente tocca la libertà del testimone, mentre il secondo appare esclusivamente autoreferente,” ibid., 63. We shall return to the question of witness and person in Chapter 6.

[p. 41]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p41}

## 4. Christ the True Witness {#ocallaghan.wtd.4-christ-the-true-witness}

For this cause came I into the world, that I should bear witness unto the truth. _(Jn 18:37 KJV)_

### The Living Center of the Christian Gospel

“Witnessing constitutes the fundamental structure of Christian revelation,” writes Claude Geffré, “even more so than revelation itself, which is central to the New Testament, because it is what makes revelation possible.”[^c4-1] In an important sense, revelation is superior to witness, for revelation, the revelation of God, is what engenders faith and communicates truth. But the communication of revelation takes place fundamentally through the process of witnessing. Thus witnessing is essential to both revelation and faith. Besides, we are told in the New Testament, Jesus Christ is the true, supreme Witness.<sup>2</sup> The book of Revelations speaks of him as the “true witness” or the “faithful witness.”[^c4-3] In this chapter, we shall consider New Testament teaching on Christian witness, specifically the way in which Jesus Christ bears witness to the Father in the Spirit, and the Father to Christ.

In many ways, witnessing goes to the heart of the Christian Gospel. However, some would hold that the _kerygma_, the content of the preached word, constitutes by right the living center of the Gospel.[^c4-4] But others would ask: “does not the word _martyria_ [witness] and its cognates better describe the primitive and indispensable core of the Christian message?”[^c4-5] And in the words of van Pelt, “the idea of witness as related to Christ and His gospel plays an essential and highly important part in the New Testament writings and in the Christian faith and life universally. Not only in the primitive preaching, but also in all effectual preaching throughout the history of the Church, the gospel is conceived not as a speculative system, but as a witness to Jesus the Christ as being Himself God’s

[^c4-1]: “La notion de témoignage constitue une structure fondamentale de la révélation chrétienne, plus encore qu’elle justifie la notion de révélation pour désigner la réalité dont il est question dans le Nouveau Testament,” C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 311. 2 We have drawn on the following sources of a Biblical and theological kind: especially the studies in E. Castelli (ed.), _La testimonianza_, of C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage (a propos de la résurrection du Christ),” 291–307; J.-L. Leuba, “La notion chrétienne de témoignage,” 309–16; P. [p. 42]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p42}

Witness to the world.”[^c4-6] In effect, _kerygma_ refers to a communicated truth, a word, a description, whereas _martyria_ involves an interpersonal communication of truth. And Christianity as we know is not only a word but more fundamentally an _event_.

The theological dynamics of witnessing may be found to an important degree throughout much of the Old Testament, especially in a juridical context.[^c4-7] Let us begin with that.

### The Old Testament Roots of Witnessing

In the words of Allison Trites,

The New Testament use of witnesses rests squarely on the Old Testament concept of justice in the gate. In the Old Testament the lawsuit or controversy theme grows out of the legal assembly and plays an important part. It is evident in the frequency of quarreling in Israelite life, the juridical terminology of the Old Testament, the controversy in the Book of Job, the use of traditional names, phrases and oaths, the preaching of the prophets, the law-court scenes of Isaiah 40–55, and in certain passages in the Apocrypha, the Dead Sea Scrolls, Josephus and Philo. The principal background for the New Testament concept of witness, then, is to be found in the Old Testament lawsuit.[^c4-8]

The Old Testament looks upon lying, or witnessing to falsehood, transgressing the second commandment of the Decalogue, as a singularly grave crime (Dt 5,20),[^c4-9] for it offends not only God but humans, who are made in his image. Reflecting on the centrality of the utterance of truth in the context of the Old Testament, Paolo Martinelli concludes that “man is only himself when he gives witness to the truth.”[^c4-10] The Psalmist calls out to God in person against false witness (Ps. 27:12; 35:11). Besides, the just man falls back not only on God as a witness (Gn 31:50), but also on heaven and earth (Dt 4:25; 30:19), which, of course, have been created by God.

The idea of witnessing is particularly present among the prophets. Ricoeur suggests four characteristics of prophetic witness,[^c4-11] citing for this purpose central texts of the prophet Isaiah (43:8–13; cf. Is 44:6–8). _First_, the Biblical witness is not simply someone who presents himself and deposes in a court of law, but rather one who “is sent in order to witness. On account of its origin, witnessing comes from beyond.”[^c4-12] God sends the witness, who in turn is meant to proclaim

[^c4-6]: J.R. van Pelt, “Witness,” in J. Hastings (ed.), _Dictionary of Christ and the Gospels_, 2 vols (Edinburgh: T. & T. Clark, 1906), vol. 2, 830.

[^c4-7]: “The Jewish law of multiple witness was important throughout biblical times. The law of Moses demanded the evidence of several witnesses to convict a person of a capital crime (Deut 17:6–7; Num 35:30),” Trites, “Witness,” 877.

[^c4-8]: Trites, _Te New Testament Concept of Witness_, 223.

[^c4-9]: Cf. also Dt 19:16; Ex 20:16; 23:1; 1 Kg 21:13; Prv 6:19; 19:5.9; Mal 3:5.

[^c4-10]: Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 11.

[^c4-11]: Cf. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 44f.

[^c4-12]: “Qui est envoyé pour témoigner. Par son origine, le témoignage vient d’ailleurs,” ibid., 44.

[p. 43]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p43}

divine truth. This characteristic may clearly be applied to Jesus Christ, who was sent by the Father. In the _second_ place, the witness does not simply communicate isolated or contingent facts, information, and data, but the radical and global meaning of human experience. Ricoeur puts it as follows: “it is God himself who testifies.”[^c4-13] In other words, the _whole truth_ is witnessed to. Again this is applicable to Christ, “the way, the truth and the life” (Jn 14:6).

_Tird_, the witness given by the prophet is universal in its scope, just as truth is, just as God is. It is directed to all peoples, as may be seen especially in Deutero-Isaiah. “It is for the sake of all peoples that one people is a witness,” says Ricoeur.[^c4-14] In the witness, the universal meets the particular. The prophet communicates a concrete, apparently contingent, instance of truth which assumes a universal character. In effect, the union between the concrete and the universal is an essential aspect of witnessing throughout Scripture, as we shall see in Chapter 6. And Jesus said: “Before Abraham came to be, I am” (Jn 8:58). _Lastly_, witnessing according to Ricoeur requires a real dedication of one’s own existence and life, “to the utmost limit, to the sacrifice of life.”[^c4-15] The prophets give themselves entirely as they give witness to truth, even to the point of risking their lives, as may be seen especially in the book of Jeremiah. And certainly this was the case with Jesus: “the Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve and to give his life as a ransom for many” (Mt 20:28). In brief, according to the analysis of Ricoeur, prophets as witnesses are _sent_ to communicate the _whole truth_ to _the whole_ of humanity to the point of _giving their lives_.[^c4-16]

Summing up his reflection on prophetic witness, Ricoeur writes, in a text already cited, that “witnessing does not belong to the witness. It proceeds from an absolute initiative, in respect of its origin and in respect its content.”[^c4-17] In other words, the prophet speaks on behalf of another, and ultimately of God. This is particularly so in the case of Jesus Christ. Although it goes without saying that the life and saving work of Christ may be studied from many points of view, in the following paragraphs we shall reflect on his identity as the definitive, faithful Witness to Truth, particularly in John’s gospel.

### Jesus Christ as the True Witness in John’s Gospel

It may seem strange to read the following words in a passage of John’s gospel that goes to the heart of the witnessing process: “If I bear witness to myself, my testimony is not true; there is another who bears witness to me, and I know that

[^c4-13]: Ibid.

[^c4-14]: Ibid.

[^c4-15]: Ibid.

[^c4-16]: Cf. Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 101: “Accettare il compito della testimonianza equivale a confessare mediante la propria vita libera la verità annunciata dagli inizi . . . il compito della testimonianza è appunto quello di onorare questi vincoli che legano per sempre.”

[^c4-17]: Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 44f.

[p. 44]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p44}

the testimony which he bears to me is true” (Jn 5:31). In fact, the Pharisees on one occasion spoke to Jesus in the following terms: “you are bearing witness to yourself, so your witness is not true” (Jn 8:13). Jesus responds to them, saying that the witness he gives _is_ trustworthy because “I know where I come from, and where I am going” (Jn. 8:14), clearly referring to his Father. The dynamic of revelation and witnessing in John’s gospel is a profoundly relational or interpersonal one. According to the fourth gospel,[^c4-18] witness is given to Jesus by a wide variety of actors: by John the Baptist (5:33–35), by Jesus’s own “works” (5:36), by the Father himself (5:32.37–38), by Scripture (5:39–40), by the people (Jn 15:27), and especially by the Holy Spirit (Jn 15:26; cf. 14:16–17, 26). In other words, the dynamic of revelation that gives rise to faith is not a simple unidirectional process, initiating from God and descending perpendicularly into the human heart. It involves a complex theology and anthropology in which a variety of personal and non-personal elements and actors intervene to ensure that revealed truth deriving from God alone “reaches” the mind and heart of potential believers. This conviction decisively affects our understanding of the process of the communication of God’s word, and thus the dynamic of Christian evangelization. And this is what witnessing is about.

Allison Trites observes the continuity between the Old Testament covenant lawsuit and the Johannine notion of witness: “In the Fourth Gospel witness is a central issue. John follows rather closely the formal pattern of the covenant lawsuit which is worked out in detail in Isaiah 43–48. There God has a lawsuit with his people and takes them to court, calling witnesses and laying charges against them. In John, God incarnate in Jesus has a controversy with the world as it is incarnate in ‘the Jews’, the Jewish leaders who epitomize the world in its opposition to the Gospel (Jn 5:16, 18; 6:41; 7:1; 10:31; 11:8).”[^c4-19] Giuseppe Angelini states succinctly that “the Gospel of Jesus has the objective form of a universal judgment,” and insists for this reason that “we need to recuperate the judicial profile of Christian witnessing.”[^c4-20]

The dynamics of the covenant lawsuit may be observed in four miracles interspersed throughout John’s gospel (Jn 5–12): the healing of man at the pool (Jn 5), the feeding of the five thousand (Jn 6), the healing of a man born blind (Jn 9), and the resurrection of Lazarus (Jn 11). “In each case a common pattern is observed. The miracle takes place, followed by a debate with opponents in which the claims of Jesus are asserted, objections are handled and further development of the argument takes place. This results in a division of opinion, some coming to faith in Christ, and others taking offense at his teaching (6:60–69; 7:43;

[^c4-18]: Cf. Beutler, _Das Zeugnisthema_; Boice, _Witness and Revelation in the Gospel of John_; S. Pancaro, _Te Law in the fourth Gospel: the Torah and the Gospel, Moses and Jesus, Judaism and Christianity according to John_ (Leiden: E.J. Brill, 1975), 208–31; Trites, _Te New Testament Concept of Witness_; J.C. Hindley, “Witness in the Fourth Gospel,” _Scottish Journal of Teology_ 18 (1965): 319–37; M.C. Tenney, “The Meaning of Witness in John,” _Bibliotheca Sacra_ 132 (1975): 229–41.

[^c4-19]: Trites, “Witness,” 879. Trites, _Te New Testament Concept of Witness_, 226: “The Fourth Gospel provides the setting for the most sustained controversy in the New Testament. The lawsuit in John seems to be patterned on the controversy material of Isaiah 40–55. There the controversy between Yahweh and the false gods is really a lawsuit between God and the world, God being represented by Israel, and the world by the pagan nations. Similarly in the Fourth Gospel God incarnate in Jesus has a lawsuit with the world. His witnesses include John the Baptist, the scriptures, the words and works of Christ, and later the witness of the apostles and the Holy Spirit. They are opposed by the world, represented in the Fourth Gospel by the unbelieving Jews. In Isaiah 40–55 the debate is over the claims of Yahweh as the Creator, the only true God and the Lord of history; in John it is over the Messiahship and divine Sonship of Jesus. John, like his prophetic counterpart, has a case to present, and for this reason he advances his arguments, asks his juridical questions, and presents his witnesses afer the fashion of the Old Testament legal assembly.”

[^c4-20]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 55.

[p. 45]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p45}

9:16; 10:19). The argument wages fast and furious, some charging Jesus with demon-possession (7:20; 8:48) or deception of the people (7:12), while others acknowledge him as ‘a good man’ (7:12), ‘a prophet’ (9:17), ‘the Christ’ (7:41) or ‘Lord’ (9:38). A decisive judgment follows (Jn 11 and 12).”[^c4-21]

Later on in the Gospel comes the stage of “glorification,” or confirmation by God of the truthfulness of the witnessing process. This function takes place under the aegis of an “other Paraclete,” the Holy Spirit: “The Holy Spirit bears witness in two ways. First, he reminds the disciples of the things which Jesus had communicated to them (14:26). Then he explains to them how the scriptural promises find their proper fulfillment in the long-awaited Christ (2:17; cf. Ps 69:9; Jn 19:23–24; cf. Ps 22:18). The Spirit functions both as a witness and an advocate of Christ.”[^c4-22]

In the following sections of this chapter we shall examine the three aspects or stages of the witnessing process, referred to already (Chapter 3), applying them one by one to the life and person of Jesus, as follows: first, Christ gives witness to the Father before humanity, to the point of dying on the Cross; then, the Father vindicates Christ and the truthfulness of his testimony by raising him from the dead; and third, in the person and saving work of Christ, truth professed and witness given are seen to merge seamlessly with one another.[^c4-23] Paradoxically, though witnessing is complex in itself, evangelizers and potential believers experience it, nonetheless, as a simple, powerful process. And at an existential level in many ways it is simple. Yet from the analytical standpoint, the process of the communication of God’s word is quite complex, for it involves the interaction of a variety of actors and instances. Let us examine them one by one.

### Christ Witnesses before Humanity to the Father with His Words, Actions, and Life

Jesus Christ not only proclaims through his words and actions a sublime and definitive knowledge of the Father and his saving design for humanity, but places his energies, his reputation, and his very life at the disposal of his Father and of humans to show forth the truth of what he proclaims. Jesus does not spare himself. What he said he meant. He sacrificed his life freely. As he preached, he put his life on the line. We saw above that the topic of witnessing is to be found in the New Testament (especially John’s gospel) in the context of a multi-faceted trial,[^c4-24] culminating in Jesus’s indictment before Pilate (Jn 18:28ff.), followed

[^c4-21]: Trites, “Witness,” 879.

[^c4-22]: Ibid.

[^c4-23]: In the words of Dulles, “All the accounts present Jesus as the Son who speaks and acts with sovereign majesty, who lays down his life for the redemption of the world, and who rises triumphant from the grave,” “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 22.

[^c4-24]: Cf. the extensive bibliography cited at the beginning of this chapter.

[p. 46]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p46}

by his death on the Cross. In the words of Angelini, “Christ was killed not on account of the good works he accomplished, but because of his incredible and unacceptable pretense of calling all to conversion.”[^c4-25] From the beginning of his public ministry, heralded by the Baptist’s witness to him,[^c4-26] Jesus goes on to give witness to the truth. All his actions and words constitute public manifestation of the Father’s love, mercy and closeness to humanity. In a moment of singular solemnity he confirms this in the presence of his Apostles: “Whoever has seen me has seen the Father” (Jn 14:9).

And as his earthly ministry draws to a close and reaches its culmination, to Pilate he says solemnly: “You say that I am a king.” And he confirms it: “For this I was born, and for this I have come into the world _, to bear witness to the truth_. Every one who is of the truth hears my voice” (Jn 18:37). Here Christ openly reveals his kingly identity, that of bearing witness to the truth. Jesus had received “a mission from God to bear witness to the truth of God’s saving sovereignty, and to reveal it in word and deed.”[^c4-27] And he does so for the sake of those who are “of the truth.” Three aspects of Jesus’ self-witnessing may be usefully mentioned: his witness to himself; inseparably, his witness to the Father; lastly, the fact that his willingly accepted death was directly occasioned by the message he proclaimed and his fidelity to his saving mission.

1. Those listening to Jesus realized he was not simply teaching a doctrine handed on from others, but truly one that came from the depths of his very being. When he spoke at the Synagogue of Caphernaum, the people “were astonished at his teaching,” Mark tells us, “for he taught them as one who had authority (_exousía_), and not as the scribes” (Mk. 1:22).[^c4-28] In effect, the scribes, by the very nature of their profession, attempted systematically to confirm the truth of what they taught by making reference to the writings of earlier authors. On some occasions, certainly, Jesus drew on Old Testament texts, for example in Mt 19,4–6. But for the most part, he spoke from the heart with a simplicity, consistency, and power that resounded deeply and directly in the minds, hearts, and lives of his listeners. This rings well with Jesus’s own words: “everyone who is of the truth listens to my voice” (Jn 18:37). The truth of his teachings sprang from within his own spirit. And he spoke to the heart of the people. _Cor ad cor loquitur_, as Card. Newman would say. At the very end of the Sermon on the Mount, Matthew makes the following observation: “When Jesus finished these sayings, the crowds were astonished (_ekplēssō_)[^c4-29] at his teaching, for he taught them as one who had authority, and not as their scribes” (Mt. 7,28).

[^c4-25]: Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 8.

[^c4-26]: Jn 1:7f.15.19; 3:26; 5:33; 10:41.

[^c4-27]: G.-R. Beasley-Murray, _John_ (WBC 36), 332.

[^c4-28]: On the notion of _exousía_ in Mark, cf. for example J. Starr, “The Meaning of ‘Authority’ in Mark 1:22,” _Harvard Theological Review_ 23 (1930): 302–5; D. Daube, “The Meaning of _Exousia_ in Mark 1,22 and 27,” _Journal of Theological Studies_ 39 (1938): 45–59. It refers ultimately to divine power: NIDNTTE 2, 216–21, s.v. ἐξουσία.

[^c4-29]: From the Greek _ekplēssō_, to amaze, astound, bewilder.

[p. 47]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p47}

This is confirmed in several peculiar ways Jesus has of speaking, ofen repeated throughout the Gospels. For example, we ofen find expressions like: “You have heard that it was said to the men of old, ‘You shall not kill. . . .’ _But I say to you_ that . . .” (Mt. 5:21f., etc.). This motif is repeated time and again throughout the gospels. Elsewhere Jesus used the phrase “truly, truly I say to you” as a way of insisting on the truth that emanated from his own Person. It comes as no surprise, therefore, that in John’s gospel, Jesus would openly claim that he, in Person, was “the Truth” (Jn 14:6). Besides, his vociferous rejection of all kinds of hypocrisy revealed the power that witnessing to the truth _should_ have among humans, and the power it does have when there is a perfect consonance, or unity of life, between what one does and what one thinks. “Let what you say be simply ‘Yes’ or ‘No’; anything more than this comes from evil” (Mt. 3:37), he said. Jesus pointed this out while speaking of the scribes and pharisees: “Practice and observe whatever they tell you, but not what they do; for they preach, but do not practice” (Mt. 23:3).[^c4-30]

2. Nonetheless, in all he does and teaches, Jesus does not rest merely on himself and his own conviction and power. He is not a soloist, a lone player. Rather, he gives witness _to his Father_, as the One who sent him:[^c4-31] he reveals the Father in all he does. He does not speak on his own authority. In John’s gospel, in fact, witnessing is virtually equivalent to revelation.[^c4-32] “For John what is essential is the revealing capacity of the witness,” says de la Potterie. “Witnessing is meant to make Christ known to humanity.”[^c4-33] The following series of Johannine texts should demonstrate this.

“No one has ever seen God,” we read in the prologue to the fourth gospel, “the only Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he has made him known” (Jn 1:18). “I have come in my Father’s name,” Jesus said to the Jews, “and you do not receive me” (Jn 5:43). In the Temple, Jesus cried aloud: “You know me, and you know where I come from; but I have not come on my own errand, I was sent by one who has a right to send, and him you do not know. I know him, because I come from him; it was he who sent me” (Jn 7:28f.). “And Jesus cried out and said, ‘He who believes in me, believes not in me but in him who sent me. And he who sees me sees him who sent me. . . . For I have not spoken on my own authority; the Father who sent me has himself given me commandment what to say and what to speak’” (Jn 12:44f.49). Speaking of his disciples with marked conviction, Jesus said that he had “given them the words which you [the Father] gave me, and they have received them and know in truth that I came from you” (Jn 17:8). The truth he communicates comes from the Father and is directed toward his disciples.

[^c4-30]: Cf. D.A. Hagner, _Matthew_, vol. 2 (WBC 33b), 659f.

[^c4-31]: Jn 3:11.31f; 5:26; 10:25; cf. 1 Tm 6:13; Rv 1:5; 3:14.

[^c4-32]: Cf. Rev 1:2; P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 48.

[^c4-33]: “Essentiel pour Jean est la fonction révélatrice du témoignage . . . Le témoignage a pour but de faire connaître le Christ aux hommes,” I. de la Potterie, “Jean-Baptiste et Jésus témoins de la vérité,” 329.

[p. 48]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p48}

This is what the process of witnessing entails. It is a handing-on of truth, from person to person, a _traditio._

A discrepancy seems to arise in the gospel text, however. On the one hand, Jesus presents himself as the Source of truth, as Truth in person; on the other hand, he refers constantly to the Father and proclaims his utter dependence on him. So where does truth lie, where is it ultimately located?
