The discrepancy, of course is but an apparent one, given that in all things Jesus filially identifies his will and mind and heart with that of his Father, making it fully his own. In fact, the heart of Jesus’s witnessing is his free filial _obedience_ to his Father. “My food is to do the will of him who sent me, and to accomplish his work” (Jn 4:34). Thus, Christ’s self-witness to humanity and the witness of the Father to him merge with one another: “The works that I do in my Father’s name, they bear witness to me” (Jn 10:25).[^c4-34]

Besides, on account of his obedience, Christ always listens to the Father, but the Father also listens to him. For example, at the resurrection of Lazarus, Jesus prays: “Father I thank you that you have heard me. I know that you hear me always” (Jn 11:41f). And in the priestly prayer, Jesus addresses the Father in the following terms: “I glorified you on earth, having accomplished the work which you gave me to do, and now, Father, glorify me in your own presence with the glory which I had with you before the world was made” (Jn 17:4f). In the words of Beasley-Murray, “The prayer for glory . . . is for a restoration of that which the Son enjoyed with the Father prior to creation.”[^c4-35] It speaks of the identification of Father and Son. Later on, Jesus says to his Father: “All mine is yours, and yours are mine, and I am glorified in them” (Jn 17:10), the latter referring, of course to Christian believers. Elsewhere, he says: “The learning which I impart is not my own, it comes from him who sent me” (Jn 7:16).

The same message that is powerfully present in John’s gospel is to be found likewise in the Synoptics. In the discourse of the beatitudes, Jesus proclaimed: “Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good works and give glory to your Father who is in heaven” (Mt. 5:16). The good works of believers, in other words, are not meant to draw attention to themselves, but rather to the Father who acts mysteriously in them and through them. Obviously, this teaching is applicable to Jesus himself in the first place, and to believers only insofar as they belong to him. In effect, when the crowds saw him working miracles, Matthew tells us, “they glorified _God_, who had given such authority to men” (Mt 9:8). In all things Jesus’ witness was not directed toward himself, but to the Father.[^c4-36]

[^c4-34]: “La circolarità tra autotestimonianza di Cristo e testimonianza del Padre, in un certo senso, si compie in riferimento alle opere del Padre che Gesù esegue . . . Gv 10,25,” P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 122.

[^c4-35]: G.-R. Beasley-Murray, _John_ (WBC 36), 297.

[^c4-36]: Sequeri writes: “La fede testimoniale nell’attirare a sé al Signore: e dunque propriamente non attrae a sé per se stessa. Essa dà corpo alla figura del testimone dimentico di sé, disinteressato alla propria sopravvivenza, disposto alla scelta della croce pur di rendere trasparente il gesto di Dio come grazia,” _Il Dio affidabile_, 567.

[p. 49]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p49}

Besides, Jesus gives witness to the Father to the extreme of obediently accepting death on the Cross, as we shall now see.[^c4-37]

3. On several occasions, Jesus predicted his forthcoming trial, death, and burial,[^c4-38] as the definitive saving act and as a prelude to his resurrection and glorification. Two things should be kept in mind about Jesus’ death on the Cross.

_First_, that he died willingly (Jn 10:17).[^c4-39] He could have avoided death had he so wished, either by biding his time (Lk. 4:28–30) or moving away temporarily to some other part of the country, thus allowing the passionate and precipitous reactions to his word and person to wane (cf. Jn 11:7f.57). Alternatively, he could have simply watered down his message and the demands he placed on his followers (Jn 6:60.66), waiting for better days. Yet he did neither. By speaking the truth as he understood it, he ended up provoking his own assassination. He openly witnessed against the world, “for its works are evil” (Jn 7:7). He told the Jews: “you seek to kill me, because my word finds no place in you” (Jn 8:37). He would die giving witness to truth; indeed, he died because he gave witness to truth. Not even as he was dying on the Cross did he make any attempt to retract and escape (Mt. 27:40).

In the _second_ place, Jesus was put to death precisely _on account of_ what he did and taught. The openness and perseverance with which he portrayed a Messianism in open contrast with the inclinations of many,[^c4-40] provoked a rejection that directly occasioned his death. Afer the resurrection of Lazarus, the chief priests and the Pharisees asked: “What are we to do? For this man performs many signs. If we let him go on thus, every one will believe in him, and the Romans will come and destroy both our holy place and our nation” (Jn 11:47f.). To this observation, the high priest Caiaphas replied: “It is appropriate for you that one man should die for the people, and that the whole nation should not perish” (Jn 11:50; cf. Jn 18:14). For many of Jesus’s listeners, his teaching, though beautiful and inspiring, seemed exorbitant and unrealistic. It was excessively committal, in that it involved painful personal conversion. It came across as unworldly, promising at best an eschatological reward, not a worldly one. Besides, his self-identification as the Son of God his Father seemed blasphemous and provocative: “We have a law, and by that law he ought to die, because he has made himself the Son of God” (Jn 19:7; cf. Lev. 24:16). Proclaiming his Sonship ultimately occasioned his death. Yet it is undeniable that his very death, willingly and lucidly accepted in obedience to his Father, was precisely what confirmed and proclaimed the particular Messianic identity he portrayed, the truth of his message, his Divine Sonship. His disposition to die for what he held, in other

[^c4-37]: “Gesù patendo vicariamente per noi la morte del peccatore, afferma il rapporto con il Padre, rendendogli la suprema testimonianza, proprio nel cuore della negazione,” P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 134.

[^c4-38]: Cf. Mt 16:21; Mk 8:31f; Lk 9:22; 13:33; Jn 11; 49–52.

[^c4-39]: On the freedom of Christ, cf. P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 123ff.

[^c4-40]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation_, 187–231, _passim_.

[p. 50]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p50}

words, was an unequivocal witness to the truth of his message. Yet it was still a truth proclaimed, but not yet accepted by the people.

Of course, his crucifixion was not a mere once-off, unfortunate, unforeseen accident. It was the crowning moment of a life of faithful, continuous witnessing, of putting his life on the line.[^c4-41] As a prelude to the sacrifice of his life on Calvary, Jesus consistently renounced his _reputation_ and the immediate _efficacy_ of his mission.[^c4-42] He was even prepared to be looked upon as a sinner in order to save the fallen human race. This may be seen especially at his Baptism in the Jordan and during the temptations in the desert.[^c4-43] And during his public ministry, Matthew tells us, “As he sat at table in the house, many tax collectors and sinners came” (Mt. 9:10). And when the Pharisees saw this, they said to his disciples, “Why does your teacher eat with tax collectors and sinners?” But when he heard it, he said, “Those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are sick” (Mt. 9:11–12). He resisted the temptation of obtaining an easy, spectacular victory over the forces of evil (Mt. 4:1–11). He consistently refused to work extraordinary signs that would involve modifying the nature of his Messianism just for the sake of gaining a few more followers in the short term (Mt 12:38f; 16:3f).[^c4-44] “An evil and adulterous generation seeks for a sign,” (Mt. 12:39) he sentenced unequivocally. Speaking of Jesus’s trial and ensuing crucifixion, Ricoeur observes that “it is in the context of response and accusation that the confession-profession takes on the quality of witness.”[^c4-45]

Those who encountered Jesus knew well what he was saying and understood the demands he was placing on people. They perceived he was convinced of the truthfulness of his message and of the urgent need to communicate it to humanity, to the point of being ready to forfeit his reputation and even his life in the process. He perceived his mission and message to be of greater value than his own life or success or reputation. Hence he sacrificed it in obedience to his Father in order that his saving mission be accomplished, that his message be communicated to one and all. “Greater love has no man than this, that a man lay down his life for his friends” (Jn 15:13). Thomas Aquinas noted that the sacrifice of Christ was objectively greater than any other, insofar as he loved his human life more than anybody else did.[^c4-46]

As John’s gospel recounts, his insistent witnessing to divine truth moved some of his listeners to follow him more closely (“Every one who is of the truth hears my voice,” Jn 18:37), convinced as they were that he had “words of eternal life” (Jn 6:68), even to the point of Apostleship and eventual martyrdom. Yet it moved others to abandon him, to draw “back and no longer go about with him”

[^c4-41]: Gabriel Marcel establishes a close link-up between witnessing and fidelity. “La fidélité comme reconnaissance d’un permanent. On est au-delà de l’opposition de l’intelligence et du sentiment . . . Le témoignage comme origine. L’Église comme témoignage perpétué, comme fidélité . . . Il est de l’essence de l’être auquel va ma fidélité de pouvoir être non seulement trahi, mais affecté en quelque manière para ma trahison. La fidélité comme témoignage perpétué; mais il est de l’essence du témoignage de pouvoir être effacé, oblitéré. Se demander comment cette oblitération peut se produire. Idée que le témoignage est périmé, qu’il ne correspond plus à la réalité. L’être comme attesté. Les sens comme témoins—ceci capital est neuf, me semble-t-il; systématiquement méconnu par l’idéalisme,” in _Être et avoir_ (Paris: Aubier-Montaigne, 1935), 118f.

[^c4-42]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation_, 209–12.

[^c4-43]: Cf. ibid., 193–206.

[^c4-44]: Cf. ibid., 206–9.

[^c4-45]: “C’est donc toujours dans une perspective de contestation et l’accusation que la confession-profession prend couleur de témoignage,” in Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 51.

[^c4-46]: Cf. Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.III_, q. 46, a. 6c.

[p. 51]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p51}

(Jn 6:66), to the point of eventually crying out: “Crucify him, crucify him!” (Jn 19:6). The rejection of truth witnessed to, and the willing death of Christ formed part of a single act.

### The Father Vindicates Christ by Raising Him from the Dead

The fact that Jesus willingly accepted death demonstrated to one and all that he was _convinced_ of the truth of the message he preached and its universality. His sincerity deeply moved his listeners. His very death convinced some of those present by the Cross that he was the Son of God (Mt. 27:54). But was such a conviction sufficient to persuade people of the truth he proclaimed? In principle, conviction is a mental state that does not necessarily involve an affirmation of truth, although doubtless it shows that humans on the whole are keenly interested in it. Truth affirmations, as we have seen, are meant to establish and manifest a vital link or _judicium_ between the mind and reality, with reality _as it is,_ with things _as they are_. People may be convinced of something, even in good faith, but may well be mistaken in their convictions. In fact, several of Jesus’s followers had seriously considered the possibility that he might be the Messiah and were deeply moved by his death, yet they had not come to believe firmly that “he was the one to redeem Israel” (Lk. 24:21). They had not learned to believe as they would subsequently do when he broke bread with them at the inn of Emmaus (Lk. 24:30f). Others, afer willingly listening to his words and even appreciating the signs he worked, eventually “walked no longer in his company” (Jn 6:66). They rejected the _truthfulness_ of his teaching and stopped being his disciples.

Besides, Christians from the beginning did not consider _the death of Christ_ as the principal point of departure for their own witnessing to divine truth.[^c4-47] Doubtless the Cross qualified Jesus’s message decisively (1 Cor. 1:17–31), but the unequivocal point of reference for believers’ witness was actually Jesus’s _Resurrection_.[^c4-48] According to the Acts of the Apostles,[^c4-49] the Resurrection was the true moment, the tangible manifestation, of Jesus’s vindication by the Father in the power of the Holy Spirit. On the morning of Pentecost, Peter proclaimed: “The God of Abraham . . . the God of our fathers, glorified his servant Jesus, whom you delivered up and denied in the presence of Pilate. . . . You denied the Holy and Righteous One . . . and killed the Author of life, _whom God raised from the dead_” (Act 3:13–15). This explains why “with great power the apostles _gave their testimony to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus_, and great grace was

[^c4-47]: It should be noted, however, that the centurion, upon seeing Christ dying, found faith, and exclaimed: “Truly, this was the Son of God” (Mt 27:54).

[^c4-48]: Cf. Act 3:15; 4:2.33; 17:18; 1 Cor 15:12; 1 Pt 1:3. On the centrality of the Resurrection in the process of witnessing, cf. C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” cit. Geffré calls the resurrection “un événement interprété,” (ibid., 298), even though the interpreting “word” derives from God.

[^c4-49]: On the theology of witnessing in Acts, cf. C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 294f.; J.-N. Aletti, “Testimoni del Risorto. Spirito Santo e testimonianza negli Atti degli Apostoli,” cit.; R. Filippini, “Per una teologia lucana della testimonianza. Un’indagine nel libro degli Atti degli Apostoli,” cit.; A. Barbi, “La struttura della testimonianza secondo _Atti_”; Angelini, _La testimonianza_.

[p. 52]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p52}

upon them all” (Act 4:33).[^c4-50] And before the high priest, Paul said: “The God of our fathers raised Jesus whom you killed hanging him on a tree. God exalted him [the Christ] at his right hand as Leader and Savior, to give repentance and forgiveness of sins. And _we are witnesses to these things_, and so is the Holy Spirit whom God has given to those who obey him” (Act 5:30–32). Thus, in the words of Martinelli, the Resurrection of Jesus is “the definitively valid manifestation of the Truth of God.”[^c4-51]

In brief terms, Christ gives witness before humanity to the Father, principally by dying on the Cross, yet the Father gives witness to Him in return, in the Spirit, mainly by raising him from the dead.[^c4-52] In his first letter, John explains: “If we receive the testimony of men, the testimony of God is greater; for _this is the testimony of God that he has borne witness to his Son._ He who believes in the Son of God has the testimony in himself. He who does not believe God has made him [the Son] a liar, because he has not believed in _the testimony that God has borne to his Son_. And this is the testimony, that God gave us eternal life, and this life is in his Son” (1 Jn 5:9–11). This is the truth Christ gives witness to: that God gives eternal life to believers, and that life consists of a partaking in the life of the Son, the heart of the Christian Gospel.

On other occasions throughout the Gospel, the Father is seen to give witness to the Son. Jesus’s Baptism in the Jordan involves a kind of mystical death and resurrection: death through Jesus’s solidarity with sinners as he descended into the waters; resurrection as he rose out again accompanied by a special manifestation of divine power: “The heavens were opened and he saw the Spirit of God descending like a dove, and alighting on him; and a voice came from heaven saying _, ‘This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased’_” (Mt. 3:16f). The Father _approves_ the Son’s way of acting, if that is the right word; a more precise biblical word perhaps would be _glorifies_ the Son: the Father joyfully proclaims Jesus as his Son, and—to use human language—takes a deep paternal satisfaction in his manifest solidarity with fallen humanity.

A similar vindication takes place in the presence of the Apostles afer Jesus’s Transfiguration: “A bright cloud overshadowed them, and a voice from the cloud said, ‘This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased; _listen to him_’” (Mt. 17:5). In John’s gospel, Jesus explains why the witnessing of the Father is so important: “If I bear witness to myself, my testimony is not true; _there is another who bears witness to me_, and I know that the testimony which he bears to me is true. . . . The testimony which I have is greater than that of John [the Baptist]; for the works which the Father has granted me to accomplish, these very works

[^c4-50]: On this text, cf. M. Tábet, “Grazia, parola e testimonianza in At 4,33,” in Granados and O’Callaghan (eds.), _Parola e testimonianza,_ 147–56.

[^c4-51]: P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 137.

[^c4-52]: “La testimonianza del Padre ha nell’opere giovannea come una trascendenza e una causalità su tutte le altre, è la sorgente di ogni altra testimonianza che si rende a Gesù,” M. Bianchi, “La testimonianza nella tradizione giovannea,” 128.

[p. 53]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p53}

which I am doing, bear me witness that the Father has sent me. And the Father who has sent me has himself borne witness to me” (Jn 5:31f.36f). “I bear witness to myself, and the Father who sent me bears witness to me” (Jn 8:18). Or again, with an equivalent terminology, that of glorification, Jesus says: “If I glorify myself, my glory is nothing; it is my Father who glorifies me” (Jn 8:54).

Christ’s persevering witness to his Father’s saving love for humanity occasioned his death, his “laying down his life for his friends” (Jn 15:13), thus convincing his followers of the sincerity of his love and the value of his message. And even though his death and resurrection are inseparable elements,[^c4-53] the Resurrection makes a critical addition to the process. It provides an interpretation, as it were, of Jesus’s death, indeed the definitive interpretation.[^c4-54] Resurrection becomes a kind of divine hermeneutic of the Cross. The meaning many onlookers gave to Jesus’s crucifixion was as reasonable as it was mistaken: it seemed that he had been repudiated by God (Dt 21:23) as a failed Messiah.[^c4-55] Yet the Father himself, not the onlookers, offered the true interpretation in proclaiming the salvific power of the Cross, by giving glorious, perpetual life back to his Son at the Resurrection and with him sending the Holy Spirit, the Lord and giver of Life. Jesus willingly forfeited his life for love of humans and in obedience to his Father; yet the Father restored that very life, making it glorious and eternal. “He humbled himself and became obedient unto death, even death on a cross. _Terefore_ God has highly exalted him and bestowed on him the name which is above every name” (Phil. 2,8–9). God proclaims in Christ and through Christ that the cause he died for was the cause of truth, God’s own cause. Divine witness directly corroborates human witness.[^c4-56]

People understood instinctively that only God could give life back to the dead, for he is the God of the living, the source of all life. As Bultmann observed, “the expression ‘resurrection of the dead’ is a paraphrase of the word ‘God.’”[^c4-57] Whoever encounters the risen Christ is confronted not only with the admirable conviction of One who was prepared to die for the sake of a vision he wished to communicate to humanity, but with the Father’s public vindication of his Son’s fidelity, with the proclamation of divine truth that takes place in Christ and through Christ. The _judicium_ in respect of Jesus’s claim to reveal the truth of the Father is made visible and tangible as we behold the risen Christ and give witness to him to others. “Put your finger here,” Jesus said to hesitant apostle Thomas, who was aware of the teaching of Jesus but had not quite managed to accept it as true, “and see my hands [that carry the marks of the Crucifixion, and thus the identity of the Risen One with Jesus of Nazareth]; and put out your

[^c4-53]: Nevertheless, as Ricoeur explains, Jesus’s vindication (or manifestation) by the Father through the Resurrection is fully complementary, within the process of witnessing, to his death on the Cross. “L’engagement du témoin est aussi son témoignage; le témoignage du Christ ce sont les œuvres du Christ, sa Passion, et le témoignage du disciple c’est, analogiquement, la passion. Un étrange cercle herméneutique se met à tourner: le cercle de la Manifestation et de la Passion. Le martyr ne prouve rien, disions-nous; mais une vérité qui ne saisit pas l’homme jusqu’au sacrifice manque de preuve. Qu’est-ce qui fait preuve: la manifestation ou la passion? L’herméneutique du témoignage elle aussi est prise dans cette spirale qui ne cesse de passer, à des altitudes différents, par ces deux pôles opposés,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 56.

[^c4-54]: “Un fait ne témoigne pas s’il n’est pas interprété,” C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 291.

[^c4-55]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation_, 209f.

[^c4-56]: Castelli states: “Non ha senso una testimonianza umana senza una testimonianza divina,” “Il problema della testimonianza; i significati della testimonianza,” 27. Something of a kind is referred to by Stanislas Breton when he speaks of the “ontotheology of witnessing.” In the witnessing process, “nous observons un mouvement de transcendance qui relève le témoignage à la dignité d’une réponse devant Dieu. C’est le forme que nous appellerions onto-théologique du témoignage,” “Philosophie et témoignage,” 199. “Nous appellerons essence du témoignage cet ensemble ordonné, et totalement ordonné, où le mouvement de transcendance parcourt les différentes valeurs qu’étale la hiérarchie,” ibid.

[^c4-57]: R. Bultmann, _Glaube und Verstehen_, vol. 1 (Tübingen: J.C.B. Mohr, 1961), 40. Then he goes on to explain why: God is the one who raised Jesus from the dead (cf. 1 T 1,10; cf. 1 Cor 6,14; Rm 8,11; 10,9; 2 Cor 4,14).

[p. 54]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p54}

hand, and place it in my side; do not be faithless, but believing” (Jn 20:27). To which Thomas, incapable of denying the evidence of the Risen Lord, replied with firm faith: “My Lord and my God” (Jn 20:28).

This brings us to the third stage of Jesus’s witnessing.

### The Identification Between Message and Witness

The Father vindicated Christ as the true Witness in many ways, among them through the miracles he worked.[^c4-58] On account of the signs and wonders, “his disciples believed in him,” John tells us (2:11). Yet the miracles were not merely spectacular manifestations of Jesus’s divine power and factual dominion over creation. Particularly in John’s gospel, they are also meant to clarify the content of Jesus’ message and teaching,[^c4-59] and thus constitute an important form of revelation. Hence he calls them “signs,” divine actions with significance that instruct us, not just miracles that surprise us. The multiplication of loaves and fish and the miracle at Cana (Jn 2) speak of the abundance of God’s gifs to saved humanity. The healing of the invalid at the pool of Bethsaida (Jn 5) speaks to us of the saving power of God over a wounded humanity. Examples could be multiplied. But the point is an important one: when Christ performs miracles, the Father gives witness not merely to the powerful works he carries out, but also to the message and intelligibility and truth they contain.

It would be mistaken to establish in the New Testament, as Strathmann attempts to do,[^c4-60] an opposition between witnessing to facts and a confessing to truth. Ricoeur shows this convincingly.[^c4-61] Referring to Luke, he argues that “the playoff between meaning and fact, between confession and narration, takes place in the essential confession that is the Resurrection.”[^c4-62] There is a perfect rapport and consistency between what Jesus teaches and who he is, between his doctrine and his person, between his words and his actions, between what he preached and what he practiced (Mt. 23:2–3). As we noted above, perhaps this was the reason, among others, why the people “were astonished at his teaching, for he taught them as one who had authority (_exousia_)” (Mk 1:22).[^c4-63] “No man ever spoke like this man,” the temple guards exclaimed (Jn 7:46).

However, if this seamless rapport between teaching and person, between action and being, is present throughout the earthly life of Jesus, it is even more so in his definitive, risen state. The supreme “sign” in the New Testament is that

[^c4-58]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation_, 220–6.

[^c4-59]: Cf. for example R. Latourelle, _Teology of Revelation_ (New York: Alba House, 1966), 398–405.

[^c4-60]: Cf. H. Strathmann, “μάρτυς, μαρτυρέω, μαρτυρία.”

[^c4-61]: “On ne peut témoigner pour un sens, sans témoigner que quelque chose est arrivé qui signifie ce sens. La conjonction du moment prophétique, ‘je suis Jahvé’, e du moment historique, ‘c’est moi, Jahvé, ton Dieu, qui t’ai fait sortir du pays d’Egypte de la maison de servitude’ (Ex 20,2)—est aussi fondamentale que la conjonction du moment prophétique et du moment juridique. Une tension est ainsi créée entre confession de foi et narration des choses vues, à l’intérieur de laquelle est reprise la tension déjà présente dans le concept ordinaire entre le jugement du juge qui décide sans avoir vu et narration du témoin qui a vu. Il n’est donc point de témoin de l’absolu qui ne soit témoin des signes historiques, point de confesseur du sens absolu qui ne soit un narrateur des actes de délivrance,” Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 46. As a result, in John “le témoignage-confession ne saurait se détacher du témoignage-narration sous peine de virer à la gnose,” ibid., 50. Jn 19:35 closely associates seeing and witnessing.

[^c4-62]: “Le moment d’immédiateté de la manifestation est essentiel à la constitution du témoignage comme témoignage. C’est principalement sur la confession essentielle— celle de la résurrection—que se joue pour Luc la dialectique du sens et du fait, de la confession et de la narration. Tout indique que les ‘apparitions’ ont joué le rôle décisif, en ce qu’elles prolongeaient la manifestation au-delà de la mort,” Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 47.

[^c4-63]: “Le témoignage de Dieu que l’on a en soi n’est pas une invitation intérieure à croire, un motif intérieur de crédibilité, mais la révélation objective de Dieu,” De la Potterie, “La notion johannique de témoignage,” 207. This coincidence is clear in Christ.

[p. 55]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p55}

of the Resurrection, in which Jesus not only “recuperates” the life he willingly surrendered, but recovers his true identity, in abundance, gloriously and forever. His risen humanity becomes the unique point of reference and vital source of what he promised to those who believe in him, that is, _life eternal_. In the Resurrection, Christ “manifests man to man,” not only in respect of his present fallen, pilgrim condition but also in respect of his eschatological destiny, that is to say, his definitive identity.[^c4-64] In that sense, the Resurrection becomes an iconic, perpetual confirmation of all that Jesus did and said, of the fact that he had come “to seek and save what was lost” (Lk. 19:10). The resurrection of the dead may be understood in terms of “the resurrection of a life once lived.”[^c4-65] In the risen Jesus, the vindication of the Person of the witness by the Father and the proclamation of the truth merge with one another.

Summing up what we have seen so far in this chapter, we may say: Christ witnesses before humans to the Father in all he says and does, even to the point of accepting death; at the same time, the Father vindicates Christ and reveals him to believers, above all by raising him from the dead and sending the Spirit; finally, in the person of Christ, truth professed and witnessing proffered become ultimately identified with one another. Indeed, as we read in the book of Revelation, Jesus Christ is the true, faithful witness (Rv 1:5).

### Final Judgment and the Eschatological Side of Witnessing

The eschatological outworking of Christ’s witnessing is of particular interest, also in a juridical context. In fact, the definitive fruit of Christ’s witnessing to humanity will be shown up in the moment of final judgment.[^c4-66] “Witness has been given to God, but if that witness does not lead to repentance and faith, it will provide evidence which will be brought forth against that community on the day of judgment. This ominous note is even stronger in Luke, where the action is viewed as a threat (‘testimony against them’, Lk. 9:5).”[^c4-67] “One of Matthew’s most gripping scenes is that of the final judgment (25:31–46). The judge is the Son of man. With him are the angels who function as court officials. There is a judgment seat or throne on which the judge sits to judge (25:31). The nations are gathered and a process of judgment takes place in which the sheep and the goats are separated. Verdicts are formally pronounced on the two classes (25:34–36;

[^c4-64]: On this question, cf. my _Children of God in the World_, 64–85.

[^c4-65]: See O’Callaghan, _Christ Our Hope_, 109–12; and also the doctoral thesis of G. Cremin, _Anthropological Implications of the Doctrine of Final Resurrection in XX Century Teology_ (Rome: Pontifical University of the Holy Cross, 2019).

[^c4-66]: Cf. O’Callaghan, _Te Christological Assimilation of the Apocalypse_, 85–99, 158–70, 252; _Christ our Hope_, 130–48.

[^c4-67]: Trites, “Witness,” 877.

[p. 56]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p56}

41–43). Christ is here described as the judge at the Last Day while in similar passages he is depicted as a witness (Mt. 10:32–33; cf. Lk. 12:8–9).”[^c4-68]

We can see that the witness Christ gives involves every single stage of his life: his preaching, death, Resurrection, the sending of the Holy Spirit, and his final return in glory at the end of time to judge the living and the dead.

[^c4-68]: Ibid., 878.

[p. 57]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p57}

## 5. Christian Believers, Witnesses in the Witness {#ocallaghan.wtd.5-christian-believers-witnesses-in-the-witness}

Yet not I, but Christ liveth in me. _(Gal 2:20 KJV)_

Jesus Christ, John tells us, is _the_ faithful Witness (Rev. 1:5) to the Father. Yet the Apostles in their turn are presented as privileged witnesses to Christ’s Resurrection, having “seen the Lord” (Jn 20:18.25; Act 9:27). Witnessing, we may say, by its very nature is a chain phenomenon: the truth is handed on from one person to another; its very truthfulness is grounded in each personal exchange, in each _adaequatio_ as it were. Thus the insistence throughout the New Testament of telling the truth, that is, of not “bearing false witness” (Mt. 15.19; 19:18; Mk 10:19; 14:56; Lk. 18:20). On account of their singular experience of the Risen Lord, the Apostles obtained the conviction of being “in the truth” (2 Pt 1:12; cf. Jn 17:19). And just as Jesus gave witness to the Father, so also the Apostles and all Christians are called to give witness to Christ before others (2 Cor. 5:14), indeed, before the whole of humanity (Mt. 28:18–20). This is what evangelization means. And just as Jesus died and rose from the dead, so also Christian apostles put their lives on the line, many of them dying as martyrs in the hope of the resurrection. In the words of Angelini, “the truth of the Gospel is only accessible through the mediation of those who believe in it and, in witnessing to its truth, are prepared to offer their own lives.”[^c5-1]

In the words of Avery Dulles, the Apostles “did not take possession of it [the Christian message], but were possessed by it. From timid disciples, anxiously bolting the doors of the Upper Room, they became apostles openly proclaiming their faith at the risk of imprisonment, scourging, and martyrdom. No external opposition can keep them from speaking of what they have seen and heard. Their extraordinary dynamism is evidence of God’s transforming power.”[^c5-2]

[^c5-1]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 105.

[^c5-2]: Dulles, “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 22.

[p. 58]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p58}

### The Witness of John the Baptist and Others

Even before the Apostles and other Christians take on their essential mission of witnessing to Christ and to the Father, an important figure appears throughout the gospels: John the Baptist, the one who “prepared the way of the Lord.”[^c5-3] His life and mission tell us a great deal of what it means to be a Christian witness. “There was a man sent from God,” we read in the prologue to John’s gospel, “whose name was John. He came for testimony, _to bear witness to the light_, that all might believe through him” (Jn 1:6f.). His task was an important one, but the essential point of reference of his mission was not himself but Jesus. Thus John affirmed of himself that he “was not the light, but came to bear witness to the light” (Jn 1:8). In effect, Jesus was the “life, and the life was the light of men” (Jn 1:4), a life that illumined a fallen world. Perhaps that is what it means to be a witness: _a life that illumines others_ and leads them to Jesus. He, “the true light that enlightens every man, was coming into the world” (Jn 1:9). And John was a privileged witness to him in handing on that light. To those who enquired into his mission, he openly said, “I am not the Christ. . . . I am the voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord’, as the prophet Isaiah said” (Jn 1:20.23; cf. Is 40:3).

The high moment of John’s testimony took place as he “was standing with two of his disciples and he looked at Jesus as he walked, and said ‘Behold, the Lamb of God!’” (Jn 1:35f.). The truth—a deeply “personal” truth as it were— enunciated by John was accepted by the disciples, for they had learned to trust him, to believe in him, “they heard him say this, and they followed Jesus” (Jn 1:37), thus becoming the latter’s disciples. John handed over his own disciples to Jesus afer having given them witness to him.

When informed that Jesus was baptizing in the Jordan and that “all are going to him” (Jn 3:26), John made it clear that “you yourselves bear me witness that I said, I am not the Christ, but I have been sent before him. He who has the bride is the bridegroom; the friend of the bridegroom, who stands and hears him, rejoices greatly at the bridegroom’s voice; therefore this joy of mine is now full. He [the Christ] must increase and I must decrease” (Jn 3:28–30). For the Baptist, it was an honor, a joy, and a privilege to serve as witness to the Lord, his friend, his leader, the bridegroom. In fact, Jesus himself confirmed that John “has borne witness to the truth” (Jn 5:33). Also, the people recognized the witnessing of the Baptist, saying: “John did no sign, but everything that John

[^c5-3]: Cf. I. de la Potterie, “Jean-Baptiste et Jésus, témoins de la vérité d’après le IVe Évangile,” 317–29; O’Callaghan, _Te Christological Assimilation_, 193–200; Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 170–5.

[p. 59]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p59}

said about this man was true” (Jn 10:41). And as a result, “many believed in him” (Jn 10:42).

Likewise, the Samaritan woman who encountered Jesus at the well of Jericho bore witness to him. “Many Samaritans from that city believed in him because of the woman’s testimony, ‘He told me all that I ever did’” (Jn 4:39). “Many of the Samaritans believed through the testimony that the woman bore . . . that is the task of a disciple! She joined with John the Baptist as a witness to Jesus even before the disciples bore any testimony to her people.”[^c5-4]

### The Witness of the Apostles

In Mark’s gospel we read that Jesus “called to him the twelve and began to send them out two by two” And he said to them: “Where you enter a house stay there until you leave the place. And if any place will not receive you and they refuse to hear you, when you leave shake off the dust on your feet _for a testimony against them_” (Mk 6:7.10–11). In other words, in their preaching and giving witness to the truth, the Apostles, in a public way, also give witness to the rejection of the truth. The Apostles were meant to be witnesses, Luke explains (Lk. 24:48), to the life, teachings, miracles, passion, and Resurrection of Jesus. And as the people rejected him, they would also reject the Apostles: “they will persecute you just as they have persecuted me,” Jesus said (Jn 15:20). Before his Ascension into heaven, Jesus uttered his last words to the Apostles (Act 1:2), his final testament on earth: “It is not for you to know times or seasons which the Father has fixed by his own authority. But _you shall receive power when the Holy Spirit has come_ upon you; and _you shall be my witnesses_ in Jerusalem and in all Judea and Samaria and to the end of the earth” (Act 1:7f). That is to say, in the power of the Spirit the Apostles themselves would become witnesses to Christ.

The following powerful text from _1 Jn_ is particularly relevant: “That which from the beginning, which we have heard, which we have seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon and touched with our hands, concerning the word of life—the life was made manifest, and we saw it, _and testify to it_, and proclaim to you the eternal life which was with the Father and was made manifest to us—that which we have seen and heard we proclaim also to you, so that you may have fellowship with us; and our fellowship is with the Father and with his Son Jesus Christ” (1:1–3). What believers receive from the Gospel of Christ,

[^c5-4]: Beasley-Murray, _John_, 64.

[p. 60]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p60}

the word of life, is not a mere verbal message, doctrine, or communication, but rather a divine, living reality that comes to them by hearing, seeing, touching, and handing it on to others, _testifying_ to it, proclaiming it to all. And in that process, they encounter and express their communion with the Father and the Son (and therefore, we might add, in the Holy Spirit).

Allison Trites speaks of Paul giving witness in the Acts of the Apostles: “In Lk 21 there is a warning of hard times, but the crisis situation is seen as an opportunity for bearing witness: ‘This will result in your being witnesses to them’ (21:13). The fulfillment of these predictions is amply illustrated in Acts, where Paul appears before governors, kings, and the house of Israel on numerous occasions (Acts 22:1–22; 25:1–27; 28:23–31). Witnessing was to be a joyful, but precarious, activity of the church, fraught with the possibility of arrest, imprisonment, and death. The death of Stephen is a case in point (7:54– 60). Luke describes him as a ‘witness’ who paid the supreme price of martyrdom (22:20).”[^c5-5]

### The Dynamics of Witnessing by Christian Believers

The point is, of course that the zeal and conviction of Christians in witnessing to Christ’s resurrection, in many cases to the point of accepting death in imitation of the true Witness (or _Martyr_), are the very things that makes the faith of others _humanly_ possible. Indeed, the faith of Christians may be said to be “ecclesial” not only in the sense that Christians are meant to share the same fundamental beliefs and teachings with others, the same doctrine, but in that it is rooted in the common living witness of Christians who have handed on the faith from one generation to the next. This “ecclesial” handing on of the faith from generation to generation is commonly called _tradition_.

Of course it might still be argued that Christians, no matter how thoroughly their faith brings them to witness to the risen Christ, even to the point of martyrdom, are presenting the revealed truth they believe in to others primarily in terms of personal _conviction_, backed up perhaps by virtuous and coherent behavior. Putting their lives on the line and their reputation at risk, partaking in the death of Christ, their sincere, sacrificed witness comes across as authentic, challenging, perhaps even convincing. But is it _true_? King Agrippa, who was clearly impressed by Paul’s zeal and sincerity, and may even have toyed with the possibility of converting to Christianity, rhetorically at least, said to him: “In a

[^c5-5]: Trites, “Witness,” 878.

[p. 61]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p61}

short time you think to make me a Christian!” And Paul replied, “Whether short or long, I would to God that not only you but also all who hear me this day might become such as I am—except for these chains” (Act 26:28f).

So we can see that the first bond referred to above, the existential one established in their humanity between the witness and the receiver (the listener or potential believer), is present in the witnessing actions of Christian believers. But what of the other two?

What of the bond established between the witness and the Source of truth that vindicates him? Specifically, what parallel point of reference do recipients of Christian witness have in those who witness to them? Who gives corroborating witness to the Christian’s witness? What link does this witnessing have with the encounter the Apostles had with the risen Christ, in whom the Father _openly vindicated_ the truth of revelation by giving life back to his Incarnate Son? We can appreciate the way in which Christ’s _death_ is made present in the life of believers (Rm 8:17). But how is Jesus’ _Resurrection_ made present in their life and actions? It is interesting to note that the New Testament posits no fundamental difference as regards faith and conversion between those who have seen the risen Lord with their own eyes, and those who come to believe on account of the witness of other people.[^c5-6] _Ceteris paribus_, it would seem that to have seen the risen Lord has the same effect on humans, in principle, as to hear about Him from other believers. But if this is the case, in what way is the power of Christ’s Resurrection made present in the life of the latter?

And what of the third bond, between the person of the witness and the truth they confess? Where or how are potential believers meant to “perceive” the truthfulness of those who witness to Christ? Why should their words and actions be _trusted_?

Two reflections may be made to clarify these issues. _First,_ it may be said that the power of the risen Christ is present and active in and through each Christian. And _second,_ divine revelation remains unimposing not because it does not contain or convey truth, but because it is characterized by eschatological reserve and is directed toward awakening a fully free, generous, human response in those who receive it. In other words, the fact that the witnessing of Christians ofen goes without effect does not mean it is not authentic witnessing; it is just that truth will not be revealed definitively until the eschatological end of time,[^c5-7] and that truth, along with grace, requires a real free response on the part of potential believers. Let us look at the two elements more closely.

[^c5-6]: “Le fait de n’être pas témoins oculaires n’enlève aucune validité à leur témoignage car, para la réception croyante du témoignage apostolique, il sont ‘le témoignage en eux-mêmes’ (1 Jn 5,10),” Leuba, “La notion chrétienne de témoignage,” 315. Speaking of Paul, Ricoeur says: “Entre les témoignages oculaires de la vie de Jésus et la rencontre du Ressuscité, le christianisme primitif n’a jamais perçu de différence fondamentale,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 47. “Entre les faits et gestes de Jésus de Nazareth, les apparitions du Ressuscité et les manifestations de l’Esprit dans les communautés pentecôtistes, nulle différence intrinsèque, mais au contraire la continuité de la même manifestation, justifiant une extension correspondante du témoignage rendu aux choses vues et entendues,” ibid. “. . . L’unité profonde entre témoin des faits, des événements et témoin du sens, de la vérité, a pu être ainsi protégée pendant quelque temps,” ibid., 47f. With the deacon Stephen, “les ‘témoins de la Résurrection’ seront de moins en moins des témoins oculaires à mesure que la foi se transmettra par l’écoute de la prédication: la ‘voix’ a beau renvoyer à la ‘vue’, la voix n’est déjà plus la vue; or la foi vient par l’ouïe,” ibid., 48.

[^c5-7]: As we saw in Chapter 4, n. 7.

[p. 62]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p62}

### The Power of the Risen Christ Acts in and Through Each Christian

In effect, the power of the risen Christ acts in and through the Christian believer. Christian believers give witness not only to the death of Christ, by their willing self-denial to the point of a martyrdom inspired by the love of humanity, but also to his resurrection. They do so in that they proclaim (or at least they are meant to proclaim), by their words, deeds, and lives, the hope-filled Gospel message, or “good news,” to those around them and to succeeding generations. They give witness to Christ through the words they pronounce, through the example of the virtues they live, and through the acts of Christian charity and service they willingly and joyfully undertake. But even this is not enough. To put it simply, to give witness is not just to give good example.[^c5-8] That would be Pelagian. We must go further and affirm that the risen Christ actually lives and works in and through Christian believers, adopted children through Baptism, who become in turn _instruments_ in the communication of divine grace. This affirmation requires further explanation.

Through baptismal grace, _Christians carry the life of the risen Lord_ within themselves, and thus proclaim, in and through their lives and words and actions, the Father’s vindication of the Son’s obedience. “You were buried with him [Christ] in baptism, in which you were also raised with him through faith in the working of God” (Col. 2:12; cf. Rm 6:3–5). Here Paul is not speaking about final resurrection, but rather of the power of resurrection present in the baptized person. On account of baptismal regeneration, Paul says of himself in his letter to the Galatians, “it is no longer I who live, but Christ who lives in me” (2:20). Speaking of Christian martyrs, Cyprian of Carthage glosses Gal 2:20 in saying: “it is Christ who struggles within us; it is Christ who fights beside us.”[^c5-9] Origen explains that martyrs partake in Christ’s passion and also in his triumph.[^c5-10] Christian witness therefore not only conveys the authentic, selfsacrificing appeal of the death of Christ, the example of a sacrificed life, but also the vibrant abundance of his risen life, as we read in Paul’s graphic exhortation to Corinthian believers: “always carrying in the body the death of Jesus, so that the life of Jesus may also be manifested in our bodies” (2 Cor. 4:10). In the words of Schlatter, “As Jesus’s herald, Paul told the story of the passion; he not only told it, but he experienced it too.”[^c5-11]

We can get some idea of the power of the resurrection in the life of Christians in the Lukan episode that presents the disciples on the way to Emmaus (Lk.

[^c5-8]: Ricoeur distinguishes clearly between witnessing and giving example: “Dans l’action exemplaire, le cas s’efface devant la règle, la personne devant la loi. La conscience n’est augmentée que d’elle-même et de la norme qu’elle impliquait déjà. L’exemplarité de l’exemple ne constitue pas une manifestation de l’affirmation originaire,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 36.

[^c5-9]: Cyprian, _Epist_. 10,4.

[^c5-10]: Cf. Origen, _Exh. ad Mart._ 42. He also observes that whereas periods of persecution may be good for Christians, times of peace make it easy for them to err (_In Jer. hom_. 4:3).

[^c5-11]: A. von Schlatter, _Paulus, der Bote Jesu. Eine Deutung seiner Briefe an die Korinther,_ 4 ed. (Stuttgart: Calwer, 1969), 553.

[p. 63]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p63}

24). Before encountering the risen Lord they had lost all hope and energy. Afer talking with him, listening to his words and his interpretation of Scripture, and breaking bread with him in the inn, they were renewed in their hope (“Did not our hearts burn within us while he talked to us on the road, while he opened to us the Scriptures,” v. 32) and rendered capable of communicating that selfsame hope to the Christians in Jerusalem (“the Lord has arisen indeed,” they proclaimed, v. 34).

Indeed, spiritual writers have always understood that Christians, divinized by grace, carry the life of Christ within them, among them Leo the Great,[^c5-12] Augustine,[^c5-13] Bernard,[^c5-14] Francis of Assisi,[^c5-15] Thomas Aquinas,[^c5-16] John Eudes,[^c5-17] Antonio Rosmini,[^c5-18] Columba Marmion,[^c5-19] and Josemaría Escrivá. The latter explains that the saints should be considered not just “other Christs” (_alter Christus_), but Christ himself (_ipse Christus_).[^c5-20] The life of Christ within them is projected mystically toward the whole of humanity.[^c5-21] Escrivá preached that the risen Christ is quite literally “alive in Christians.”[^c5-22] He expresses this as follows: “Christ is alive in Christians. Our faith teaches us that man, in the state of grace, is divinized—filled with God. We are men and women, not angels. We are flesh and blood, people with sentiments and passions, with sorrows and joys. And this divinization affects everything human; it is a sort of foretaste of the final resurrection. ‘Christ has risen from the dead, the first-fruits of those who have fallen asleep. For since by a man came death, by a man also comes resurrection of the dead. For as in Adam all die, so in Christ all will be made to live’” (1 Cor. 15:21f.).[^c5-23]

Augustine, in his work _De doctrina christiana,_ says “the human condition would be abject if God could not communicate his word to humans through other humans.”[^c5-24] And likewise, Basil the Great, speaking of the Holy Spirit, says the following: “In the same way as bodies become resplendent and transparent when they enter into contact with a ray of light, and thus communicate a powerful beam from within themselves, so also those who carry the Spirit and are illumined by the Spirit, become themselves spiritual, and project grace toward others.”[^c5-25]

Christians, as they strive to be faithful to this new life within them, though not always fully aware of the fact, become—in Christ—salt of the earth, light of the world (Mt 5:13f), the good fragrance of Christ (2 Cor. 2:15).[^c5-26] This may occur even in spite of their limitations and defects. Josemaría Escrivá has the following striking text: “Not so much despite our wretchedness but in some way through it, through our life as men of flesh and blood and dust, Christ is

[^c5-12]: “There is no doubt . . . that human nature is deeply bound up and connected with the Son of God, not only in the man Jesus Christ, who is the first-born of all creation; besides, in all the saints Christ is one and the same; and just as members are united to the head, the members cannot be separated from the head,” Leo the Great, _Sermon 12, The Passion_, 3:6–7 (PL 54, 355–7).

[^c5-13]: “Let us rejoice and give thanks: we have become not only Christians, but Christ himself . . . Be amazed and joyful: we have become Christ,” Augustine, _In Evangelium Ioannis tractatus._

[^c5-14]: “I will always honor Christ whose members you always are,” Bernard, _Epistula_ 382, n. 1.

[^c5-15]: It was said of Francis: _Christus vivebat in eo_, “Christ lived in him,” Thomas Celano, _Legenda secunda_, n. 211; _Videbatur [quod] Christi et beati Francisci una persona foret_, “It may be seen that Christ and blessed Francis were one and the same person,” n. 291.

[^c5-16]: “Christ is our life, because the ultimate principle of our life and action is Christ. Thus the Apostle says: _mihi vivere Christum est_ (Phil 1:21), because only Christ moved him,” Thomas Aquinas, _Ad Phil_. 1, l. 3.

[^c5-17]: “Unis avec lui [Jésus] spirituellement par la foi et par la grâce qu’il nous a données au saint Baptême; unis avec lui corporellement par l’union de son très saint corps avec le nôtre en la sainte Eucharistie; il suit de là nécessairement que, comme les membres sont animés de l’esprit de leur chef et vivant de sa vie, nous devons être animés de l’esprit de Jésus, vivre de sa vie, marcher dans ses voies, être revêtus de ses sentiments et inclinations, faire toutes nos actions dans les dispositions et intentions dans lesquelles il faisait les siennes, en un mot continuer et accomplir la vie, la religion et la dévotion qu’il a exercée sur la terre,” John Eudes, _La vie et le royaume de Jésus_, II, 2 (_Oeuvres complètes_, vol. 1 (Beauchesne: Paris, 1906-), 161–6). Cf. also _De admirabile corde Iesu_ I, 5 (_Oeuvres complètes_, vol. 6, 107–15).

[^c5-18]: “Per la partecipazione della vita umana-divina di Cristo noi ci trasformiamo nella stessa immagine di Cristo, ci trasformiamo in qualche modo in Cristo, diveniamo in un certo senso altrettanti Cristi viventi in lui, tale essendo la forza di quella parola immagine [. . .], ponendosi l’immagine per la stessa cosa incipiente, siccome in quel luogo: _Umbra enim habens lex futurorum bonorum non ipsam imaginem rerum_ (cf. Eb 10,11), cioè _non ipsa bona quasi in embrione_, sentendo noi quello che sente Cristo, piacendoci quello che piace a Cristo, dispiacendoci quello che a Cristo dispiace, volendo quello che vuole Cristo, diventiamo amabili al Padre che ama in noi le stesse cose che sono in Cristo e sono in noi, ama Cristo in noi,” A. Rosmini, _Introduzione al Vangelo secondo Giovanni,_ ed. S.F. Tadini (Città Nuova: Roma 2009), 314–16.

[^c5-19]: _“Christianus, alter Christus_: ‘The Christian is another Christ.’ That is the true definition of a Christian given by tradition, if not in the same words, at least equivalently. “Another Christ,” because the Christian is first of all, by grace, a child of the heavenly Father and brother of Christ here below in order to be his co-heir above; “another Christ,” because all his activity—thoughts, desires, actions— plunges its root in this grace to be exercised according to the thoughts, desires, sentiments of Jesus, and in conformity with the actions of Jesus (Phil 2:5),” C. Marmion, _Christ the Life of the Soul_ (London: Sands & Co. 1939), 43.

[^c5-20]: On this question in the writings of Josemaría Escrivá, cf. my studies “The Inseparability of Holiness and Apostolate. The Christian ‘alter Christus, ipse Christus’ in the Writings of Blessed Josemaría Escrivá,” _Annales Teologici_ 16 (2002): 135–64; _Children of God in the World_, 369–74.

[^c5-21]: Cf. R. Latourelle, “La sainteté, signe de la révélation,” _Gregorianum_ 46 (1965): 36–65.

[^c5-22]: Cf. Josemaría Escrivá’s homily “Christ’s Presence in Christians,” in _Christ Is Passing By_ (Dublin: Four Courts, 1982), nn. 102–116. Cf. my study “The Inseparability . . . ,” 155–63.

[^c5-23]: Josemaría Escrivá, “Christ’s Presence in Christians,” n. 104.

[^c5-24]: Augustine, _De doct. christ_., prol. 6.

[^c5-25]: Basil, _De Spiritu Sancto_ 9:23.

[^c5-26]: The notion of “compelling” people to come in in Christian apostolate is referred to in Luke’s gospel (14:23), as long as it is properly interpreted. Cf. my study “The Inseparability . . . ,” 161–3.

[p. 64]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p64}

shown forth: in our effort to be better, to have a love which wants to be pure, to overcome our selfishness, to give ourselves fully to others—to turn our existence into a continuous service.”[^c5-27] As we read in the preface of martyrs of the Roman Liturgy, _vires infirmas ad testimonium roboras_: o God, “you strengthen our weak powers so they can give witness.” Not only through the virtues they acquire, not only through the harmony and beauty of their lives, not only through the good example they give (all that could easily be Pelagianism), but also by becoming humble (and ofen humiliated) “instruments of grace,”[^c5-28] sharing in God’s saving action, do Christians give witness to the Risen Christ.[^c5-29] This is made possible, of course, by the action of the Holy Spirit that gives life to virtue and unity to life.

Karl Rahner gives the following theological explanation of the dynamics of Christian witness that stems from God’s grace. “The breadth of human transcendentality, freed from all limits and radicalized (i.e., vivified by the hope of the immediacy of God), which carries its own authentication within itself because it is, and _ex definitione_ can have no basis outside itself, is called, in Christian terminology, the state of being engraced and sustained by the Holy Spirit. In Christian terms, therefore, we can say that, _as a witness affected by the Spirit, a witness is true and authenticated in itself_. As such it also renders the specific and concrete content of this witness credible. _God himself bears witness to himself in the witness_. And only in this selfsame grace can it be hearkened to and accepted.”[^c5-30]

Pope Benedict XVI in his 2005 encyclical _Deus Caritas Est_ says: “Nor has the Lord been absent from subsequent Church history: he encounters us ever anew, in the men and women who reflect his presence, in his word, in the sacraments, and especially in the Eucharist. In the Church’s Liturgy, in her prayer, in the living community of believers, we experience the love of God, we perceive his presence and we thus learn to recognize that presence in our daily lives. . . . Those who practice charity in the Church’s name will never seek to impose the Church’s faith upon others. They realize that a pure and generous love is the best witness to the God in whom we believe and by whom we are driven to love.”[^c5-31] As Paul said, _caritas Christi urget nos_, “the love of Christ moves (or controls) us” (2 Cor. 5:14): Christ’s own love in the first place, and the charity present in those who have been regenerated by grace, as well.

This understanding has the advantage of making it clear that Christian witness never takes over or substitutes Christ’s role as the perfect Witness and only Mediator. Immediacy to Christ, and immediacy to the Father in Christ, is never lost. Sequeri insists on the importance of “avoiding the risk of an ecclesiology

[^c5-27]: Josemaría Escrivá, “Christ’s Presence in Christians,” n. 114.

[^c5-28]: O’Callaghan, _Children of God in the World_, 369–74.

[^c5-29]: On the relationship between witnessing and holiness, cf. P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 16–27.

[^c5-30]: Rahner, “Theological Observations on the Concept of ‘Witness’,” in _Teological Investigations_, 13, 167. Emphasis added.

[^c5-31]: Benedict XVI, Enc. _Deus Caritas Est_ (2005), nn. 17, 31.

[p. 65]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p65}

that more or less surreptitiously becomes a kind of substitutive Christology of the intermediate period. . . . The Church is totally committed to excluding,” he says, “a purely self-referential understanding of the faith that she lives, and of the word that takes Christ as its very foundation.”[^c5-32] Christian witness does not refer the potential believer to the believing witness (to their virtues or lifestyle), but to the One witnessed to. Jesus says so quite succinctly in Matthew’s Gospel: “Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good works and _give glory to your Father_ who is in heaven” (Mt. 5:16). The light emanating from Christian life refers others to God, not to the believers in question.

### The Veiled Character of Divine Truth

The second question to be asked is the following: why does divine truth, present in the words and actions of Christ and the prophets, and as a result in the lives of Christian believers, remain veiled, provisional, and hidden? In brief, would it not make sense for divine truth to _impose_ itself once and for all on those who receive it? If objectively true, then why not subjectively certain? And, even more to the point: why does _God_, the Source of all truth and power, make it apparently so arduous for humans to grasp divine revelation _as eternal truth_ by linking it to apparently contingent historical mediations and events, concretely to the life, death, and resurrection of Christ, and then to the witnessing power of the Church and of particular Christians? Two reasons, already mentioned, may be suggested for this. First, that Christian “revelation” (the term is from the Greek _apokalypsis_) always involves some form of eschatological reserve;[^c5-33] and second, that it consists of a divine invitation to humans to offer a free and generous response to God’s revelation and grace.[^c5-34] Let us examine these points.

1. _Definitive revelation of truth is eschatological_. In effect, divine truth is never totally clear or definitively revealed on earth.[^c5-35] Revelation will only be complete, both in content and certainty, as regards _apprehensio_ and _judicium_, at the _Parousia_, when the glory of God will be contemplated by a risen humanity, openly, unconditionally, definitively, in and through the risen Christ, the Judge of the living and the dead. Only then, when God is “all in all” (1 Cor 15:28), will truth be totally apparent and utterly undeniable. Only then will it serve both to vindicate the righteous and condemn obdurate sinners.[^c5-36] Only then will judgment be complete and definitive. “For now we see in a mirror dimly, but then face to face,” Paul tells us. “Now I know in part; then I shall understand

[^c5-32]: Sequeri holds that witnessing is always referred to Christ. “La mediazione va accuratamente concepita in positivo contrasto con ogni surrettizia dinamica di _Aufebung_ (incorporazione e oltrepassamento) della incarnazione cristologica nella appartenenza ecclesiastica e della rivelazione cristologica nella fede ecclesiastica . . . Si tratta . . . di evitare il rischio di una ecclesiologia che si risolva—più o meno surrettiziamente—in una cristologia sostitutiva del tempo intermedio,” _Il Dio affidabile_, 591. “La Chiesa è assolutamente interessata ad escludere . . . la qualità puramente autoreferenziale della fede che essa vive, e della parola che nomina Lui come suo fondamento,” ibid., 593. The same idea is to be found in Ricoeur, who says that “il y a, dans le témoignage, une immédiateté opère comme origine, comme _initium_, en deçà de quoi on ne peut remonter. A partir de là, l’interprétation sera l’interminable médiation de cette immédiateté. Mais sans elle, l’interprétation ne sera jamais qu’une interprétation d’interprétation,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 54.

[^c5-33]: See Chapter 2, note 12.

[^c5-34]: “L’appello della verità, infatti, non è mai generico ma coglie sempre l’uomo personalmente e lo pone sulla vertigine della propria responsabilità,” P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 69.

[^c5-35]: See my study, “Eschatology and Revelation,” in B.M. Mezei, F.A. Murphy, and K. Oakes (eds.), _Te Oxford Handbook of Divine Revelation_ (Oxford: Oxford University Press, 2021), 102–20.

[^c5-36]: Cf. my study _Te Christological Assimilation_, 170–8.

[p. 66]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p66}

fully” (1 Cor 13:12). The signs Jesus performed on earth “are referred to a future truth, which we do not encounter in the present moment.”[^c5-37]

Wolfart Pannenberg[^c5-38] explains that in Scripture truth becomes manifest not once and for all, but gradually so, throughout history, and that this process culminates in the _eschaton_.[^c5-39] Truth “does not seek to grasp the identity of the true as an eternal present behind the flux of time [as the Greeks thought]. It seeks to grasp it as _that which shows and confirms itself to be lasting as time progresses_.”[^c5-40] For truth emerges, Pannenberg says, as history progresses. In that sense, all truth affirmations are _anticipations_. However, this apparent affirmation to the effect that all truth is relative “does not have to mean that there is nothing absolute and that there is thus no truth, which as such is always absolute. . . . [But] . . . _at least for us_, the absoluteness of truth is accessible only in the relativity of our experience and reflection. As Dilthey has shown, as regards the historicity of experience, this means that we cannot definitively determine the true meaning of things and events in our world so long as the course of history continues.”[^c5-41] Truth is absolute, but human knowledge of it is contingent and takes place throughout history. Truth affirmations, such as they are, are indeed anticipations of the _eschaton_, already present in the Passover of the Lord.

Still, in spite of Pannenberg’s observations, it is by no means impossible for the Church to enunciate fully valid truth affirmations,[^c5-42] for they are anticipations based on the fact that the _eschaton_ has already made its appearance in the life, Passion, Death, and Resurrection of Jesus Christ. However, _the undeniable and universal quality_ of their truth will become fully apparent to all only when the power of Christ’s resurrection is communicated to humanity at the _Parousia_, at the end of time, with general judgment.

2. _Revelation and grace seek out a free and generous response_. The imperfection of the witnessing process—the distinction between witnessing and vindication, the imperfect coincidence between the message witnessed to and the one who witnesses to it—opens an ample space for free human response to God’s gifs. What seems to be a defect in the witnessing process in fact turns out to be a powerful anthropological affirmation: we are invited to respond to God’s gifs freely and generously. Witnessing essentially appeals to freedom. However “visible” and undeniable divine truth may become in the life of Christ, of the Church, and of Christians, its acceptance invariably requires on the part of the prospective believer an interior openness to divine grace and a personal, free, conscious response that cannot be anonymous or simply taken for granted.[^c5-43] For von Balthasar, in Angelini’s words, to take the reception of truth for granted

[^c5-37]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 22.
