Vogel) (Berlin: Lettner, 1966), 214–39, and also in _Grundfragen systematischer Teologie. Gesammelte Aufsätze_, vol. 1 (Göttingen: Vandenhoeck & Ruprecht, 1967); _Systematic Teology_, vol. 1, 8–61, and my study “Whose Future? Pannenberg’s Eschatological Verification of Theological Truth.”

[^c5-39]: On the interpretation of Scripture throughout history, cf. my study “La Biblia en la configuración de la teología,” _Scripta Teologica_ 36 (2004): 855–75.

[^c5-40]: W. Pannenberg, _Systematic Teology_, vol. 1, 54. Emphasis added.

[^c5-41]: Ibid., 54f. Emphasis added.

[^c5-42]: John Paul II in the 1998 encyclical _Fides et ratio_ insists time and again on the notion - that _truth is accessible_ to human reason.

[^c5-43]: According to Ellul the witness cannot be anonymous: “il ne peut jamais être anonyme, ni de la part du porteur ni de celle du récepteur. Il n’est pas un code, il est un parole personnalisée: et le premier effet du témoignage quand il est entendu, c’est individualiser celui à qui il s’adresse. Il le cerne et l’isole des autres parce qu’il le met dans l’obligation, lui et lui seul, de recevoir ou rejeter ce témoignage, par conséquent à son tour de choisir. Mais s’il choisit, il ne peut le faire que contre l’adhésion au groupe et la croyance collective,” “Témoignage et société technicienne,” 443. See the critique of Angelini and von Balthasar of Rahner’s notion of anonymous Christianity, in Angelini _, La testimonianza_, 54f.

[p. 67]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p67}

would amount to “a selling-out of the truth of the Gospel in exchange for a generic humanism, for which it would be easy to obtain the consensus of all.”[^c5-44]

Natural law, such as it is, does not impose itself on the lives of people. It speaks to them, it reaches out to them, it inclines them, but it does not force them. Neither does witnessing impose on potential believers a _judicium_ in respect of its truth content, but gently and firmly (Wis 8:1) induces them to _make_ a _judicium_ in respect of the revealed truth, to embrace the truth it contains and identify themselves with it. God’s grace and revelation indeed require a fully human reception and acceptance for them to take root in the soul. They never involve violence on humans.[^c5-45] According to Augustine, God acts invincibly on humans (he speaks of _gratia invicta_) while fully respecting their will and freedom (according to what he calls the _suavitas amoris_, the gentleness of love).[^c5-46] In the words of Sequeri, “witnessing is the historical mediation of the revelation of an unconditional justice that wishes to be appreciated, not just sustained.”[^c5-47] Revelation seeks appreciation and understanding on the part of the one who receives it. Ricoeur says the same thing from a philosophical stance: “Witnessing always offers something that stands in need of an interpretation.”[^c5-48] The liberty of each recipient, he says, must exercise a kind of hermeneutical action within the witnessing process. In the case of Christian witness, this is called _faith_. The fact is that faith and witnessing go hand in hand,[^c5-49] for the power of the divine is both present and hidden in the act of witnessing: present in that God himself acts within the witness by sending the Spirit of his Son; hidden in that the act of witnessing is a human act that both obscures and channels divine power.

In reality, the consoling, convincing, and fortifying power of the Holy Spirit, present in the witnessing process, is what makes it possible for the receiver to generously take the step of faith and conversion,[^c5-50] thus overcoming innate reluctance and whatever obstacles may present themselves. Two such obstacles may be mentioned.

_First,_ the sinfulness of humans, which brings them to be mistrustful, to remain uncommitted to a truth that, though clear and unequivocal, undoes old certainties and invites them to renounce the warm and comfortable cocoon their own “riches” surround them with (Mt. 19:22; Lk. 12:19), following the Lord wherever he goes in simplicity and poverty (Mt. 8:20). Yet the Holy Spirit, God as Gif, accompanies the action of the witness from within, and the potential believer experiences with certain faith that both the commitments and sacrifices involved in conversion and the acceptance of revealed truth are both possible and worthwhile.[^c5-51] “Are you able to drink the cup that I am to drink?” Jesus asked

[^c5-44]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 54f.

[^c5-45]: The notion that violence has no place in Christian evangelization is common among the Fathers of the Church. “Non enim cum vi, sed secundum suadelam,” Irenaeus, _Adv. Haer. 5_,11. God “sent his Son in the best way: as a Savior, as one who persuades, not constrains. For in God there is no violence,” Anon., _Letter to Diognetus_ 7:4 (SC 33,68). Origen: “Christ wins over nobody except by persuasion (_peithon_), since he is the Word of God ,” _Sel. in Ps._ (PG 12,1133b).

[^c5-46]: On the notion of _gratia invicta_ in Augustine, and on grace which works in the soul as _suavitas amoris_, cf. respectively _De corr. et grat._ 12,38; and _Enn. in Ps._, 13; _Enn. in Ps_. 13. See the interesting study of P.R.L. Brown, “St. Augustine’s Attitude to Religious Coercion,” _Te Journal of Roman Studies_ 54 (1964): 107–16.

[^c5-47]: “La testimonianza è la mediazione storica adeguata della rivelazione di un incondizionato della giustizia che vuole essere apprezzato, non subito,” P.A. Sequeri, “Coscienza credente e mediazione della testimonianza,” _Saggio introduttivo_, in M. Neri, _La testimonianza in H.U. von Balthasar_, 7.

[^c5-48]: Ricoeur says: “Le témoignage _donne_ quelque chose à interpréter,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 54. Emphasis in the original.

[^c5-49]: Martinelli says: “la fede testimonia la verità che riconosce,” _La testimonianza_, 146. And Bianchi: “Non solo il testimoniare si finalizza al credere, ma la testimonianza è intrinseca alla fede fin quasi a esserli sovrapponibile . . . La testimonianza è dentro il credere e il credere è dentro il testimoniare. Il rendere testimonianza è il modo proprio del credere che si verifica nel discepolo,” “La testimonianza nella tradizione giovannea,” 135.

[^c5-50]: H. Verweyen says: “the path that brings us to a definitively valid meaning can only pass through a request directed unreservedly to all freedoms so that they are presented to one another in reciprocal recognition, as images of unconditioned being,” _Gottes letztes Wort_, 299.

[^c5-51]: Cf. my study “L’agire dello Spirito Santo, chiave dell’escatologia cristiana,” _Annales Teologici_ 12 (1998): 327–73, especially 367–70.

[p. 68]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p68}

his disciples. In other words: Are you able to make the sacrifices required to follow Me closely and be my witnesses? They said to him, “We are able” (Mt 20:22). They are _able_, not because of their own capabilities and energies, but because they are accompanied by the grace and call of Christ’s presence and word in the Spirit.

By contrast, sinful action normally involves the rejection not of revealed truth as such, but of the witness to that truth. “If they persecuted me, they will persecute you; if they kept my word, they will keep yours also” (Jn 15:20). As Marcel points out, it is of the very nature of witnessing that it can be refused, and as a result, its potential content obliterated.[^c5-52] And Ricoeur has the same thing to say: “One must distinguish between the false witness and the true witness, between the father of lies and the faithful witness. Witnessing is at one and the same time a manifestation and a crisis of appearance. . . . Witnessing takes place when there is rejection.”[^c5-53] Yet it is clear that the appeal of truth requires a free response.

The _second_ obstacle, though related to the first, is more strictly anthropological in character. Before accepting God’s revelation _as true_ through the witnessing action of another person, under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit, the recipient needs to perceive that the truth received and its witnessing mediation are anthropologically consistent, that they respond to the fundamental desires and aspirations of the human heart: for love, for meaning, for immortality, for corporeity, for realism. Christian witness should appeal _to the whole of the human person_, for it is meant to be an act of the whole person; it requires _credibility_. As Marcel says, in a text already cited, “the essence of man, is it not that of being one capable of giving witness?”[^c5-54]

Of course Christ as the prime Witness is the one who establishes the basic structure and dynamic of Christian testimony. He gave witness to the world in body and soul, with all his heart, with all his soul, and mind, and strength (cf. Mk 12:30). Giving witness involved _every single aspect_ of his life: _love_ that brought him to offer his life for those who were as yet sinners (Rm 5:6); _giving meaning_ through the richness and simplicity of his preaching and the coherent example he offered; _immortality_ that shines forth from his glorious, risen humanity for the whole of the human race; _corporeity_ that is likewise fully present and vibrant in the risen Christ; _realism_ that fully recognizes both the limits of human existence as expressed in death and in the variety of human situations,[^c5-55] and its extraordinary dignity reflected in the promise of eternal life.

[^c5-52]: “La fidélité comme témoignage perpétué; mais il est de l’essence du témoignage de pouvoir être effacé, oblitéré,” _Être et avoir_, 119. Cf. S. Plourde, _Marcel, philosophe et témoin de l’espérance_ (Presses de l’Université du Québec, 1975).

[^c5-53]: “Il faut toujours trancher entre le faux témoin et le témoin véritable, entre le père du mensonge et le témoin fidèle. Le témoignage est à la fois une manifestation et une crise de l’apparence . . . On atteste là ou on conteste,” Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 55.

[^c5-54]: “L’essence de l’homme ne serait-elle pas d’être un être qui peut témoigner?,” Marcel, _Être et avoir_, 140.

[^c5-55]: Indeed, the witnessing process must take the situations of the recipients of witness into account. Jacques Ellul explains that we must “envisager le témoignage dans sa relation avec le milieu ou il es porté . . . Quand une société change de structure, le témoignage qui peut y être rendu devient différent en tout, et non pas seulement dans son contenu ou ses références, mais dans sa réalité même,” in “Témoignage et société technicienne,” 444.

[p. 69]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p69}

We can probably say that the truth involved in our _personal_ filial relationship with God may only come to be known in plenitude by means of witnessing,[^c5-56] for the simple reason that it does not just provide _information_ about God, but constitutes an essential aspect of establishing a _relationship_ with him.[^c5-57] Thus, in the words of Sequeri, “it is impossible for the subject of witnessing to remain external—or even extraneous—to the evidence of revealed truth.”[^c5-58]

It should now be clear that Christians, in Christ (who is the only Mediator, 1 Tim. 2:4), by giving witness to divine truth, are in a position to establish the threefold bond of trust and response described earlier on: with the _receiver_, who is moved by the sincerity and coherency of the witness’s words and actions; with _God_ as the ultimate Source who by means of their “anthropological authenticity,” vindicates truth by making it shine out through their “Christified” lives; and with _themselves_ in that the witness they give is (or should be) in consonance with the lives they live and with their very being: it is an _authentic_ witness. Always taking account, of course, both the eschatological reserve that characterizes Christian revelation, and the free, personal, and untransferable response that each person is called to give to divine grace.

This brings us to the final aspect of our theological reflection on witnessing, its Trinitarian side. Specifically, we shall consider the role played by the Holy Spirit as Witness to the Father and the Son, the divine power behind all witnessing to truth. Indeed, all that is true comes from ultimately the Holy Spirit (Jn 16:13).

### Boldly Witnessing in the Holy Spirit

The Holy Spirit does not only play an auxiliary role in providing believers with strength, conviction, and consolation in overcoming sinfulness and reluctance, thus facilitating the acceptance of the truth as proclaimed by Christian witness. It is clear from Scripture that the Holy Spirit plays a critical role in the very process of witnessing.[^c5-59] “The witness of the disciples is made possible on the basis of a double origin. It is possible on account of the experiences they had in precedence in the company of the Master. . . . In that reality was inscribed a truth which they were not completely aware of when they encountered it. . . . It was also possible thanks to the Spirit sent from the Father and together with the Risen One; the Spirit would bring them to the truth of the previous encounter which they had not fully understood.”[^c5-60]

[^c5-56]: Sequeri observes: “della _verità_ dell’ _abbà_ Dio, proprio in ragione della sua qualità intrinseca, si può essere realmente ma soltanto testimoni,” _Il Dio affidabile_, 579.

[^c5-57]: Cf. ibid., 582.

[^c5-58]: “È impossibile che il soggetto della testimonianza rimanga esterno—o addirittura estraneo—alla evidenza della verità rivelata,” ibid., 582.

[^c5-59]: Scripture speaks of three elements that together give witness: Spirit, water, and blood (1 Jn 5:7–10). Martinelli states: “il Paraclito è il vero ermeneuta, l’interprete e l’espositore della divina verità,” _La testimonianza_, 146. “Nella promessa dello Spirito, fatta da Gesù prima della passione, il Paraclito viene descritto in relazione alla verità e alla testimonianza,” ibid., 129.

[^c5-60]: Angelini, _La testimonianza_, 189. Cf. also T. Preiss, _Das innere Zeugnis des Heiligen Geistes_ (Zürich: Evangelischer Verlag, 1947).

[p. 70]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p70}

According to Paul, the Spirit is said “to give witness to our spirit” that we are children of God (Rm 8:16). For John the Evangelist, the witnessing of the Holy Spirit _goes side by side with_ that of the Apostles. “When the Consoler comes,” Jesus tells us, “whom I shall send to you from the Father, the Spirit of truth, who proceeds from the Father, _he will bear witness to me_, and _you also are witnesses_, because you have been with me from the beginning” (Jn 15:26f). This two-fold integrated witness, that of the Spirit and that of believers, recurs elsewhere in Scripture, albeit indirectly. In a text already cited we read that the apostles with great power “gave their testimony to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus, _and great grace was upon them all”_ (Act 4:33), grace in this case being the fruit of the Holy Spirit (Act 2:4). In a parallel text, also mentioned earlier, Peter exclaimed before the high priest that “the God of our fathers raised Jesus whom you killed, hanging him on a tree. God exalted him [the Christ] at his right hand as Leader and Savior, to give repentance and forgiveness of sins. _And we are witnesses_ to these things, and _so is the Holy Spirit_ whom God has given to those who obey him” (Act 5:30–32). In brief terms, the external witnessing of Christ and Christian believers is inseparable from the interior witnessing of the Spirit. In John’s gospel, Jesus tells us that “the Counsellor, the Holy Spirit, whom the Father will send in my name, he will teach you all things, and bring to your remembrance all that I have said to you” (Jn 14:26).[^c5-61] For he is “the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither sees him nor knows him; you know him, for he dwells with you, and will be in you” (Jn 14:17). The Spirit is the one who places truth deeply within the human heart, who provides us with the _judicium_ in respect of truth, who cries out “this is true!”

Allison Trites explains that simple human “evidence fails to convince opponents . . . unless it is accompanied by the inward witness of the Holy Spirit (Jn 15:26; cf. 14:16–17, 26). When the Spirit comes, he drives home the truth, convicting the world of sin, of righteousness and of judgment (16:8–11).”[^c5-62]

In the Acts of the Apostles, we see that with the coming of the Holy Spirit the disciples were empowered for witnessing (Acts 2:4; cf. 1:5). Luke notes that “with great power the apostles gave their testimony to the resurrection of the Lord Jesus” (Acts 4:33; cf. 1:22; 2:32; 3:15). Trites observes: “In times of persecution the church prayed for boldness (Gk. _parrēsia_) in witness, and asked for divine signs and wonders to be performed in the name of Jesus (4:29–30). . . . This remarkable ‘boldness’ is a striking feature of apostolic witness, and it is clearly in evidence in church leaders such as Peter, John, Stephen, Philip, Barnabas, and Apollos (2:29; 4:9f., 13; 7:2–53; 8:30–35; 14:3; 18:26). It is most prominent in the

[^c5-61]: On this, cf. my study “L’agire dello Spirito Santo,” 346–9.

[^c5-62]: Trites, “Witness,” 879.

[p. 71]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p71}

case of Paul (9:27f.; 19:8; 28:31), whose speeches in Jerusalem, Rome, and before Felix, Festus, and Agrippa are remarkably bold and fearless (22:3–21; 23:1–6; 24:10–21; 25:8–11; 26:2–29; 28:16–20, 23–31; cf. 13:9–11; 14:14–18; 17:30f.). This outspoken quality is given by the Holy Spirit, who energizes the witness and directs the entire mission of the church (8:29; 10:19–20; 11:12; 16:6–7).”[^c5-63]

### The Trinitarian Structure of Christian Witness

It may be noted, as we saw earlier, that Christ refers to his _Father_ as the other witness of the two that the Old Testament prescribes for justifying a legal truth. “In your law it is written that the testimony of two men is true” (Jn 8:17; cf. Dt 19:15), Jesus says.[^c5-64] And he continues: “I bear witness to myself, and the _Father_ who sent me _bears witness to me_” (Jn 8:18). Also in John’s gospel he teaches: “if we receive the testimony of men, the testimony of God is greater” (1 Jn 5:9). Besides, Jesus confirms the validity of the witness he gives of himself, saying: “Even if I do bear witness to myself, my testimony is true, for I know where I have come from and where I am going” (Jn 8:14): he comes from the Father and is going back to the Father. This “double witness” is implicit in his witnessing to himself.

However, if both the Father and the Son are said to give witness, what place does the witness of the _Spirit_ occupy? What does it involve? Of course, the Father and the Son may not be considered as two distinct witnesses who might numerically reinforce the truthfulness of the message being communicated. Rather, as we have already seen, the Father in raising up Jesus from the dead vindicates the latter’s life and teaching as his own, for “I and the Father are one” (Jn 10:30). And logically enough, this seamlessly perfect witnessing action takes place in the Holy Spirit, the loving communion of Father and Son, whom John Paul II significantly calls “the direct witness of their love” (of the Father and the Son).[^c5-65]

This is why Scripture also calls the Spirit a witness. He is a witness to the perfect union of the Father and the Son, and in that sense, the Spirit expresses and reinforces—if this is the proper word—their witness. It is not as if there are three different instances of witnessing in God, each one added to the next, but rather one single, unbroken process of witnessing of the Triune God.[^c5-66] In effect, the Spirit is the Eternal Gif who, in expressing the union between the Father and the Son as well as their distinction, illuminates the mind of the potential believer

[^c5-63]: Ibid., 879.

[^c5-64]: Cf. also Mt 18:16; 26:60; Rev 11:3.

[^c5-65]: John Paul II, Enc. _Dominum et vivificantem_ (1986), n. 34.

[^c5-66]: On the Trinitarian aspect of witnessing, cf. G. Maspero, “Dire l’amore: spunti trinitari per la formazione cristiana,” in Granados and O’Callaghan (eds.), _Parola e testimonianza_, 413–24.

[p. 72]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p72}

and interiorly establishes the truthful conviction (or _judicium_) to the effect that the death of Jesus has been vindicated by the Father at the Resurrection. The Spirit thus _convinces_ the believer of the truthfulness of the witness and communicates the power of salvation.

And what would that “convincing” look like? What would it consist of? Speaking of the presence of the risen Christ, Sequeri suggests that “the Spirit knows how to make himself unimaginably persuasive regarding the truth of this presence.”[^c5-67] And for Paul Ricoeur, the second witness spoken of in 1 Jn 5:9f. is, precisely, “the Holy Spirit, the one who achieves the greatest possible interiorization” in the process of witnessing.[^c5-68] It is worthwhile to reflect on the power of the terms applied to the action of the Holy Spirit, “persuasion” and “interiorization.”

In the preceding sections, we have considered different aspects of the apparently provisional and non-imposing character of Christian witness, and the Holy Spirit as the definitive witness. Witnessing seems to be weak, but in reality, it is strong _in the Spirit_ (2 Cor. 12:10). The following conclusions may now perhaps be drawn. _First,_ that revelation, though complete in Christ, is open to an ever greater comprehension throughout the history of the Church, precisely under the guidance of the Holy Spirit.[^c5-69] _Second_, that the convincing power of Jesus’ resurrection is made present, through the Holy Spirit, in and through the lives of Christians, who become instrumental—at times in spite of and unaware to themselves—in the handing on of the faith to other people by means of a kind of “sof power”[^c5-70] which is Christian apostolate and evangelization. And _thirdly_, the free, generous response to divine action by which Christians accept, even unto death, the truth of their faith takes place under what Augustine called, as previously noted, the _suavitas amoris,_ the gentleness of love, which is the fruit of the action of the Holy Spirit.[^c5-71]

[^c5-67]: Sequeri, _Il Dio affidabile_, 594.

[^c5-68]: Cf. P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 49. “Le témoignage intérieur du Saint-Esprit prend tout son sens dans la contestation qui se poursuit entre le Christ et le monde devant le tribunal de l’histoire,” ibid., 52. “Le témoignage, au niveau humain, est double: il est le témoignage intérieur, le sceau de la conviction; mais il est aussi le témoignage des œuvres, c’est-à-dire, sur le modèle de la passion du Christ, le témoignage de la souffrance,” ibid. Ricoeur attempts to combine the juridical and mystical aspects of witnessing, in line with the “juridical mysticism” of Téo Preiss, “La justification dans la pensée johannique (1946),” in _La vie en Christ_ (Neuchâtel: Delachaux et Niestlé, 1951). Ricoeur says: “Interprété en termes purement mystiques, le témoignage se réduit à la confession de la vérité; interprété en termes juridiques, il est l’attestation que rend vainqueur de la contestation. Ne pourrait-t-on dire alors que c’est le moment juridique que fait tenir ensemble les deux moments qui nous avaient parus sur le point de se dissocier: le témoignage comme confession (de foi) et le témoignage comme narration (des faits)?,” “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 52.

[^c5-69]: Cf. my study “La Biblia en la configuración de la teología,” cit.

[^c5-70]: Joseph Nye, of the John F. Kennedy School of Government at Harvard University, speaks of example as a form of “sof power.”

[^c5-71]: Cf. Augustine, _Enn. in Ps_. 13.

[p. 73]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p73}

## 6. Is “Witnessing” a Philosophically Rigorous Category? {#ocallaghan.wtd.6-is-witnessing-a-philosophically-rigorous-category}

The single individual—with this category the cause of Christianity stands or falls. _(Kierkegaard)_[^c6-1]

We have seen that the process of witnessing is situated at the very heart of Christian revelation: that the life, work, teaching, death, and Resurrection of Christ all revolve around it (Chapter 4), and that the passing on of Christian faith from one generation to the next (_traditio_) essentially boils down to Christians giving witness to it (Chapter 5). It lies at the heart of Christian evangelization, for it provides potential believers with the key to _accessing_ divine truth through human agency. “Witnessing constitutes the fundamental structure of Christian revelation, even more so than revelation itself, which is central to the New Testament, because it is what makes revelation possible,” says Claude Geffré.[^c6-2] But an important question remains to be asked: is witnessing a philosophically and anthropologically rigorous category? Is it in a position to provide common ground between philosophy and theology, between reason and faith, between science and belief? Is it workable on the hypothesis of God being as yet unknown to us? In other words, does witnessing rely necessarily on an epistemology that involves _a priori_ the existence of God? That is to say, is witnessing previous to the establishment of faith in God, or is our knowledge of God the presupposition for witnessing to be possible?

### The Value of Alternative Models of Truth

In Chapter 1, we saw that a realist epistemology would seem to be in a better position than an illuminist one to facilitate a fruitful dialogue between theology

[^c6-1]: S. Kierkegaard, _Philosophischen Broken_ (Jena: E. Diederichs, 1910).

[^c6-2]: “La notion de témoignage constitue une structure fondamentale de la révélation chrétienne, plus encore qu’elle justifie la notion de révélation pour désigner la réalité dont il est question dans le Nouveau Testament,” C. Geffré, “Le témoignage comme expérience et comme langage,” 311.

[p. 74]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p74}

and science, to appreciate the consistency of the mediating structures of Christian faith, and to assimilate at the deepest possible level the central theological category of witnessing. Earlier on we made mention of a variety of what might be called “secularized” theories of truth typical of modernity: “secularized” in the sense that they avoid the notion of a supreme Divinity as an obligatory point of departure for understanding and having access to truth.[^c6-3] In briefly considering the four of them again, our concluding hypothesis is the following: the inclusion of the category of witnessing within theological method, that is, within the process of having access to truth, stands in an excellent position to draw from these four understandings the full value they have.

_First_, we considered the _coherence_ theory, which locates truth in its correspondence with itself, the agreement of thought with the general laws of the mind, its rationality in a word. This understanding, which in many ways is not in a position to give rise to a unitary sense of truth, common to all humans, has the notable advantage of according particular value to the individual human intellect, and thus to the concrete person. Precisely in this context does the category of individual witnessing—from one person to another—take on its full meaning.

_Secondly_, exponents of the _consensus_ theory hold that truth is obtained by means of dialogue and agreement between individuals. The theory may have the disadvantage of confusing truth with mere local conventionality that is open to manipulation and conformism. However, it has the advantage of situating truth in the context of intersubjectivity, and in that sense is eminently amenable to the category of witnessing between people. Still, to avoid the subjectivistic drawbacks of the consensus theory, it should not be possible to exclude _a priori_ either the word and action of the tri-personal God, Creator of the universe, or impersonal (or material) reality, as valid dialogue partners in the establishment of consensus.[^c6-4] Which is not the same thing, of course, as saying that God is the necessary point of departure for any kind of intellection.

The _third_ theory of truth referred to above we called _performative_. Truth is no longer considered as pre-existent and waiting to be discovered, but is the product of the person, who as it were “makes things true,” who _gives birth_ to truth. Truth in the performative sense involves primarily the human capacity to offer effective solutions to problems. In simple terms, something is true if it works. This explanation, if taken on its own, and especially if the Divinity is systematically excluded, is quite obviously defective. It may be of considerable

[^c6-3]: Cf. Chapter 1 and the text corresponding to notes 74ff.

[^c6-4]: On the Trinitarian aspect of witnessing, cf. Chapter 5 and the text corresponding to notes 64ff.

[p. 75]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p75}

interest, however, in the context of witnessing. In effect, the process of witnessing involves the transmission of a truth that existed beforehand but remained hidden. Before, it was veiled; now it is unveiled or revealed. Before, it was present in one mind; later it was revealed to many minds. Though permanent and unchangeable in itself, truth as it were needs to “become incarnate” in time, through witnessing. In so far as God is said to be the origin of truth, and truth is revealed historically through Christ and the prophets, then the performative theory of truth may well be in a position to play a useful role in overcoming the apparent opposition between truth as universal and eternal, on the one hand, and mediations as contingent and concrete, on the other.

It should be kept in mind besides that human activity—work—is a partaking in God’s task of establishing divine sovereignty over creation.[^c6-5] In this sense, human activity _is_ capable of producing a kind of “new truth,” a new reality situated between the person and the world, which is “fruit of the earth and work of human hands.”[^c6-6] This involves a kind of _adaequatio intellectus cum re_, in this case, the intellect being human and not divine, and the reality being the object modified or acted upon or molded by the human being.[^c6-7] Thus witnessing involves not only the communication of an abstract truth but also a real co-redemptive remodeling of the world so that the Sovereignty of Christ may shine forth, and God may be “all in all” (1 Cor 15:28).[^c6-8]

A _fourth_ and final attempt to redefine truth, undertaken in the main by existentialist thinkers, explains that truth (_alétheia_) in real terms is an unveiling, a _revelation_. Being is self-manifesting to humans, Heidegger would say in his earlier works, and its truth must be received in freedom (“the essence of truth is freedom,” he said).[^c6-9] Although this position, if taken on its own, may well involve an insuperable breach between the real order and the ideal order, it is also true that it links admirably with the notion of witnessing, which is a free act of communicating the truth that attempts to awaken a fully free response. The theologian Bernard Welte refers openly to Heidegger and Jaspers in his explanation of witnessing. The fact is that truth, universal and binding truth, may well be revealed by the witness, whose very fragility and finitude ensure that it is never imposed, but freely assimilated and received.

Nonetheless, in spite of the value of alternative truth theories, quite a number of modern philosophers have had serious doubts regarding the viability of the witnessing process in the communication of truth and the generation of faith. Let us examine some of them.

[^c6-5]: Cf. my study _Children of God in the World_, 99–103.

[^c6-6]: From the prayer of the Offertory of the Mass.

[^c6-7]: This involves the anthropological and theological relevance of work and human activity. On the issue in respect of the question of truth, cf. M. Horkheimer, _Zur Kritik der instrumentellen Vernunf: aus den Vorträgen und Aufzeichnungen seit Kriegsende_ (1947) (Frankfurt a.M.: S. Fischer, 1967). Cf. also H. Arendt, _Te Human Condition_, 2 ed. (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1959).

[^c6-8]: In the words of Angelini, “the defense of _homo faber_ is directly opposed to the Christian view of life; the latter in fact takes it that the meaning and value of life in reference to its value as testimony. Human life is valuable for what it attests, well before and well beyond for what it produces . . . Christians are encouraged to be patient rather than agonic in the face of suffering and all its causes,” _La testimonianza_, 120. “We need to accept detachment from our actions: they can produce fruits only if we entrust them to the One who sees in secret . . . Our actions must be handed over to God, as the seed must be surrendered to the earth,” ibid., 122.

[^c6-9]: M. Heidegger, _Being and Time_, § 44.

[p. 76]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p76}

### Philosophical Reflections on the Conjugation of the Universal and the Concrete in Witnessing

In spite of what we said in previous chapters, the fact is that many modern and contemporary philosophers have shown a distinct hostility to the category of witnessing, considering it as lacking in epistemological reliability. Understandably, authors with an illuminist or idealistic approach to truth and knowledge are ill at ease when it comes to explaining a particularly critical aspect of Christian revelation and of truth in general: the concreteness, contingency, historicity, and modesty of the created mediations through which it is received.[^c6-10] One is Baruch Spinoza, for whom the knowledge of the divine “must be derived from general ideas, certain in themselves and well known, in such a way that historical truth has little or nothing to do with obtaining the highest good.”[^c6-11] Truth is general or abstract, eternal, he holds, but not concrete or historical. Even better known in this regard is the categorical affirmation of Gotthold E. Lessing (ofen referred to as “Lessing’s ditch”) to the effect that “the accidental truths of history (_zufällige Geschichtswahrheiten)_ can never become a proof of the necessary truths of reason (_notwendige Vernunfwahrheiten_).”[^c6-12] That is to say, the concrete can never beat or channel the abstract. Truth is superior to events, as eternity is to time.

Interestingly, Kierkegaard openly opposed Lessing on this point, explaining that the truth communicated by concrete historical mediations goes beyond the mere contingent historical event.[^c6-13] Revealed truth, he says, involves an encounter between the disciple and Christ whose life and action are contemporaneous with all periods in history and with each human being.[^c6-14] It is an encounter between persons of which witnessing forms an important part. The potential believer is invited, therefore, obliged indeed, to make a leap of faith. Kierkegaard’s explanation gives the key to understanding the dilemma of Lessing and others, for it locates truth and its communication in the person, the individual, not in nature as a generality. However, in spite of its best intentions, Kierkegaard’s position tends to assume illuminist (or fideistic) connotations, in that the historical event itself is as much as bypassed.[^c6-15]

Even more severe is Friedrich Nietzsche’s critique of the category of witnessing, and in particular of the supreme and paradigmatic form of witnessing that is Christian martyrdom, the willing acceptance of death in the cause of truth and faith. It would be ridiculous, he affirms, to hold that martyrdom manifests truth. “Blood is the worst possible witness to truth; it poisons the purest of doctrines, transforming them into delirium and heartfelt hate.”[^c6-16] “Die for the truth? We

[^c6-10]: A similar position may be found in Bayle, Locke and Rénan, who reject the category of witnessing considering it epistemologically invalid.

[^c6-11]: B. Spinoza, _Tractatus theologico-politicus_, 4.

[^c6-12]: G.E. Lessing, “Über den Beweis des Geistes und der Kraf,” in L. Zscharnack (ed.), _Teologischen Schrifen_, vol. 4 (Berlin: 1929), 47. On Lessing’s acceptance of Spinoza, cf. F.H. Jacobi, _Concerning the Doctrine of Spinoza in Letters to Moses Mendelssohn_ (Breslau: Löwe, 1789). In the same direction, Rahner speaks of the “inevitability of such an incongruence between the relative certainty of historical knowledge on the one hand and the absoluteness of his commitment on the other,” _Foundations of Christian Faith. An Introduction to the Idea of Christianity_ (New York: Crossroad, 1987), 234. On this problem in Lessing and others, cf. A. Rizzacasa, _Il tema di Lessing. È possibile provare una verità eterna a partire da un fatto storico?_ (Cinisello Balsamo: San Paolo, 1996).

[^c6-13]: Cf. especially S. Kierkegaard, “Concluding Unscientific Postscript to Philosophical Fragments,” in _Kierkegaard’s Writings_, 12 (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 1992). Cf. K. Kienzler, “Der garstige Graben zwischen Vernunf und Offenbarung,” in Aa.vv., _Auf der Suche nach dem verborgenen Gott: zur theologischen Relevanz neuzeitlichen Denkens_ (Düsseldorf: Patmos, 1987), 35–54.

[^c6-14]: Cf. Kierkegaard, “Concluding Unscientific Postscript.”

[^c6-15]: For a critique of Kierkegaard, cf. G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 125ff. Vattimo writes: “La rivendicazione kierkegaardiana del pensatore soggettivo e dell’angoscia esistenziale, con tutte le sue propaggine nella filosofia successiva, appare da questo punto di vista qualcosa di estremamente remoto, ancora totalmente incluso nell’ambito della tradizione borghese-cristiano. Anche il discorso sulla testimonianza sembra svuotarsi completamente di significato: sparisce infatti il soggetto di essa; il testimone è ridotto egli stesso a puro sintomo; non è un centro ultimo e attivo di interpretazione, ma si offre a sua volta, come oggetto, a interpretazioni ulteriori,” ibid., 130.

[^c6-16]: F. Nietzsche, “Priests,” in _Tus spake Zarathustra II_.

[p. 77]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p77}

will not allow ourselves be burned for our opinions: we are not sufficiently sure of them. At best we will allow ourselves be burned in order to be able to _change_ our opinions.”[^c6-17] It is nonsensical and ingenuous to allow oneself be seduced by martyrdom or its equivalents, he says, for, at best, it attests to “the conformity of a particular proposition to the interests of a way of life the individual belongs to.”[^c6-18] According to Nietzsche, the witness, the martyr, at heart is one who thinks that truth is at his disposal.[^c6-19] As a result, he concludes, “martyrs damage the cause of truth.”[^c6-20]

What seems to the martyr to be universally true, says Nietzsche, is no more than a passing conformity to certain practical and utilitarian schemes characteristic of the time he lives in. In real terms, the martyr simply cultivates and favors his own interests or those of the group (period, social class, sect, etc.) he belongs to and of which, unconsciously, he is a spokesman. The martyr in real terms is not to be trusted, Nietzsche concludes, because he is a minor, unable to take full responsibility for his life.[^c6-21] Thus he considers martyrdom a form of neurosis.

At the heart of Nietzsche’s rejection of witnessing is his atheism, the positive conviction or belief that there is no such thing as a God, for we have already “killed” him. Since one cannot by definition witness to a God whose existence we deny, he perceives witnessing, and especially martyrdom, as a form of selfdivinization. The witness pretends to speak on God’s behalf, and if there is no God to witness to in the first place, then the witness becomes _ipso facto_ an impostor. To some degree, Ricoeur confirms this logic, pointing out that the philosophical problem involved here is one of witnessing to the Absolute, or better, providing an absolute witness to the Absolute.[^c6-22]

Likewise, Martin Heidegger criticizes the notion of witnessing as a path to truth.[^c6-23] Witnessing, he says, involves the establishment of a constitutive relationship between the individual and truth. In earlier works, especially _Sein und Zeit_, he takes it that the notion of witnessing is as viable as that of authenticity.[^c6-24] But in later works, he considers the category invalid because humans think and give witness simply to “average” thoughts or personal convictions. The point of reference for truth, he says, is located in powers that go beyond the individual (historical destiny and being),[^c6-25] and not within individual human subjectivity.[^c6-26] For Heidegger, the reason for this is “the insufficiency of the bourgeois-Christian notion of subject as an interpreter of the historical experience for modern man.”[^c6-27] At the end of the day, whether they realize it or not, collectivist superstructures exert enormous power over individuals. In Oscar Wilde’s inimitable expression,

[^c6-17]: F. Nietzsche, _Te Traveller and His Shadow_, n. 333.

[^c6-18]: G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 128.

[^c6-19]: Cf. F. Nietzsche, _Te Antichrist_ (South Carolina: CreateSpace, 2017), n. 53.

[^c6-20]: Ibid.

[^c6-21]: Cf. G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 130.

[^c6-22]: Cf. P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 35.

[^c6-23]: Cf. G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 130ff.

[^c6-24]: Cf. ibid., 131.

[^c6-25]: Löwith and Adorno criticize Heidegger, says Vattimo, “di aver teorizzato il tramonto del soggetto di fronte a potenze più grandi di lui: il destino storico o, senz’altro, l’essere,” ibid., 134.

[^c6-26]: Cf. M. Heidegger, _Holzwege_ (Frankfurt: Klostermann, 1950), 103.

[^c6-27]: G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 134f.

[p. 78]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p78}

“Most people are other people. Their thoughts are someone else’s opinions, their lives a mimicry, their passions a quotation.”[^c6-28]

Karl Jaspers also doubts the reliability of witnessing in the ascertainment and communication of truth. He says that the truth of Galileo’s theories will subsist whether or not he gave evidence as a witness before the Inquisition. And Giordano Bruno was wrong in his view of the world, however much he proclaimed the depth and sincerity of his convictions by dying at the stake.[^c6-29]

The philosopher Gianni Vattimo also considers witnessing as an epistemological category to be avoided.[^c6-30] Nietzsche, he says, criticizes the notion of witnessing from a psychological point of view (as the power-hungry selfdelusion of the individual subject who attempts to monopolize truth and control the lives of others), whereas Heidegger does so from a sociological angle (the individual as dominated or absorbed by society, who represents himself as a simple mouthpiece of the collectivity).[^c6-31] For one and the other, Vattimo would say, truth, such as it is, may not be considered as a universally true proposition, but rather a general order, a historical structure, lifestyle, or culture. Besides, the structure in question is not determined on the basis of decisions made by individuals, for the one capable of making revolutionary decisions could only be born beyond the structure, or at best, within it.[^c6-32]

The sociologist Jacques Ellul, while not denying the value of witnessing as a way to truth, argues that the technologically dominated society we live in makes it particularly difficult to appreciate its value. “Technological society is a rigorous, coherent, logical society, which tends to close in on itself, to be self-sufficient, to exclude whatever is not itself.”[^c6-33] In a technological society, where everything is instrumentalized, he says, “witnessing becomes radically impossible.”[^c6-34] This has brought modern man to renounce being a witness, he says.[^c6-35] All personal commitment (_engagement_) with truth and its communication is but apparent and rhetorical.[^c6-36]

All the above objections may be summed up in the words of Jean Nabert, whose understanding of witnessing is shared by Paul Ricoeur and many others: “Does anyone have the right to give an absolute character to a moment of history?”[^c6-37] The universal and abstract quality of truth, it would seem, is simply incompatible with the concrete and contingent quality of the witnessing process. It may be observed that the above-mentioned authors reflect, in one way or another, an illuminist epistemology in which concrete, contingent being relates to eternal, unchangeable, truth ambivalently, extrinsically. On the other hand, a realist epistemology in which truth, even universal truth, is “located” (as it were)

[^c6-29]: Cf. especially K. Jaspers, _Der philosophische Glaube_ (München: R. Piper, 1948), 11f.; also his work _Von der Wahrheit_. On Jasper’s thought in this regard, cf. J. Hereu i Bohigas, _Trascendencia y revelación de Dios: metafísica de las “cifras” según Karl Jaspers, metafísica del testimonio según Jean Nabert_ (Barcelona: Facultad de Teología de Barcelona; Herder, 1983).

[^c6-30]: Cf. G. Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” _passim_. Cf. D. Paoletti, “Il pensiero debole e la testimonianza cristiana.”

Vattimo, “Tramonto del soggetto e problema della testimonianza,” 135.

[^c6-33]: In a technological society, Ellul says, “il n’y a donc pas, à proprement parler, urgence d’un témoignage,” “Témoignage et société technicienne,” 445. “La société technicienne est une société rigoureuse, cohérente, logique, et qui tend toujours à se clore, à s’achever, à exclure ce qui n’est pas elle,” ibid., 446.

[^c6-34]: Everything is instrumentalized in a technological society, and “le témoignage est radicalement impossible,” ibid., 449.

[^c6-35]: “Il me semble que l’on assiste alors à une tendance de l’homme moderne à un renoncement à être témoin,” ibid., 452.

[^c6-37]: J. Nabert, _Essai sur le mal_, 148, cit. by P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 37.

[p. 79]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p79}

in concrete beings and events should offer no _a priori_ obstacle to the mediation of truth in and through the concrete.

### Personalist Attempts to Harmonize the General and the Particular

Can universal truth be drawn from particular situations or persons or events or actions? The question has been examined in the context of the above objections by many philosophers. The ensuing reflections of the theologian Bernhard Welte on the category of witnessing are worthwhile considering.[^c6-38] Later on we shall consider the explanation of Hans Urs von Balthasar.

In an important article published in the 1950s, Welte pointed out that the difficult relationship between unconditional revelation and conditioned history “may not be eliminated by way of approximation. . . . The infinite trust directed toward something infinitely important (faith in the historical apparition of Jesus) cannot be obtained by a ‘maybe.’”[^c6-39] And he goes on to account for Lessing’s perplexity: “how can one come to believe without inflating the historical fact and without turning faith into an unfounded leap, given that the historical element itself cannot give any absolute support?”[^c6-40] Welte offers a way out of the dilemma by explaining Christian revelation in terms of _personal encounter_. Through an encounter of a personal kind we “are able to see what makes it possible to say: ‘I can abandon myself,’ ‘I can trust.’”[^c6-41] Trust is made possible however not simply when the potential believer links up with specific content elements being revealed, but with the _person_ behind it who gives unity and vitality to the entire process. This takes place principally, Welte says, by _witnessing_. Witnessing is not a kind of separate form, but “what is manifested, appears and is witnessed in its own self-testimony, that is its direct and real presence.”[^c6-42]

Welte bases his reflections on the notion of truth as revelation (_alétheia_) found in Heidegger and Jaspers: in effect, openness to the unconditional truth of being necessarily involves a free openness to a free, historical event.[^c6-43] He is aware of the importance of historical analysis in order to comprehend the abstract _content_ of revelation (what we have referred to with the term _apprehensio_), but explains that _truth_ (referred to above as the _judicium_) as such _only_ presents itself in the personal encounter, specifically, in the context of the importance of the truth in question for the individual. “History serves as a means in historical theology, but is separated from the latter by the seriousness of the historical person, in

On his thought, cf. I. Feige, _Geschichtlichkeit: zu Bernhard Weltes Phänomenologie des Geschichtlichen auf der Grundlage unveröffentlicher Vorlesungen_ (Freiburg i.B.; Basel: Herder, 1989); A.J. Godzieba, _Bernhard Welte’s Fundamental Theological Approach to Christology_ (New York: P. Lang, 1994).

La profezia di Bernhard Welte_ (Roma: Città Nuova, 2009).

[p. 80]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p80}

whom the truth of the event of Jesus takes the place of his appearance.”[^c6-44] The successive process of witnessing to revelation, Welte observes, is what gives rise to the category of tradition. “Tradition takes place as a dialogue, in which the truth of what took place is inserted into the event of the truth in the present moment, in such a way that what once happened speaks with each new present, and the present responds to the founding word that comes from the past.”[^c6-45] Thus tradition may not be reduced to a mere collection of material elements or doctrinal affirmations, _what_ is handed on, but to the conservation of truth, _that_ is handed on as “event and history, in that the truth of what once was, appears as truth only in the ever new event of a historical existence capable of truth.”[^c6-46] This notion is confirmed in the distinction and union in revelation between word and event that Vatican II’s _Dei Verbum_ speaks about.[^c6-47]

Another author who has attempted to combine the universal and the contingent in our access to truth is Hans Urs von Balthasar.[^c6-48] He speaks of the “objective evidence” to the truth present in the life of Christ, an evidence “that emerges and sheds its light from the phenomenon itself, and not the sort of evidence that is recognized in the process of satisfying the subject’s needs.”[^c6-49] In this he goes decisively beyond the immanent method of Blondel and Bultmann,[^c6-50] and explains that subjective presuppositions are simply not in a position to occupy the place of Christ’s life in all of its divine power and presence.[^c6-51] Likewise according to von Balthasar, Pierre Rousselot stays too close to the Kantism he attempts to overcome.[^c6-52] For Rousselot, he says, “the synthetic power remains, one-sidedly, a part of the subjective dynamism, borne by grace.”[^c6-53] As a result, von Balthasar says, human “active-constructive synthetic power ought not to be overestimated to the detriment of God’s own power, which expresses and imposes itself in its historical witness.”[^c6-54] Truth in other words is not primarily determined by the subject, but is, rather, made present in the subject through God’s action.[^c6-55]

Eberhard Kunz suggests, however, that von Balthasar—whose position is illuminist from the epistemological point of view—may be asking too much of the intrinsic power of God’s revelation independently of the subject who receives it. We must ask, he says, “if the form of revelation is independent of the hopes, aspirations, experiences and needs of man in the radical way von Balthasar suggests.”[^c6-56] In other words, revealed truth must be in a position to connect with the knowing subject on its own terms, to connect with finite reality. Thomas Aquinas says as much: “Faith cannot precede intelligence universally, because man could not assent to what is proposed should he not have been able to understand it in some way.”[^c6-57]

[p. 81]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p81}

The above discussion leads us to see that the category of testimony/witnessing, which is perfect in Christ and particularly relevant in the life of Christian believers, in whom Christ lives, is clearly related to the notion of personhood, in a juridical and personalist context.

### The Assumption of Juridical and Personalist Values in Witnessing

As we have seen already, the notion and process of witnessing in Scripture is clearly juridical in language and character. The linguistic description of witnessing refers to the process in a court of law which is meant to prove the truth of a claim, or support an accusation. Indeed, a clear continuity between the theological and the legal is a constant feature of the Bible.[^c6-58] Specifically, the gospel of John is presented in terms of a vast cosmic trial. Paul Ricoeur is of the opinion that the theological category of witnessing is but a perfected form of the juridical one. “The profane side of witnessing is not simply abolished” in Scripture, “but, in a sense, is conserved and even exalted.”[^c6-59] He admits, as we saw above, that “the philosophical problem of witnessing is the problem of giving witness to the Absolute; and not only that, but giving absolute witness to the Absolute.”[^c6-60] This is why the _realist_ understanding of truth we looked at in earlier chapters is so important: concrete realities are in a position to _locate and channel,_ or even contain, truth, also absolute truth.

Along with Ricoeur, several philosophers of the existentialist, personalist, and hermeneutic schools, among them Søren Kierkegaard,[^c6-61] Gabriel Marcel,[^c6-62] Emmanuel Lévinas[^c6-63] and Jean-Luc Marion,[^c6-64] have taken considerable interest in the category of witnessing. According to them, the point of reference for access to truth is not the universal or the general, _but the human person,_ who, though limited and concrete, must be considered an indispensable point of reference for universal truth. Obviously, both the vision of Nietzsche, who criticizes the dynamic of witnessing because he perceives in it the individual seeking power over others and attempting to monopolize truth and localize it in himself, and that of Heidegger, who takes it that the individual is, perhaps without realizing it, dominated and absorbed by society and is therefore incapable of handing on truth to everybody, are based on the principle of _the subservience of the individual to the collectivity_. This is a vision that is clearly open to criticism from the standpoint of personalist philosophies and Christian theology, as we have

[^c6-58]: Cf. Trites, _Te New Testament Concept of Witness._ See Chapter 4, 2 and 3.

[^c6-59]: P. Ricoeur, “L’herméneutique du témoignage,” 44. “Le sens profane n’est pas simplement aboli, mais, d’une certain façon, conservé et même exalté . . . L’irruption du sens nouveau et la conservation de l’ancien dans le nouveau,” ibid. This is a very important issue. In the context of the Suffering Servant of the prophet Isaiah, “l’aspect juridique du témoignage n’est pas moins important. C’est dans la perspective d’une contestation, d’un procès mettant en jeu le droit de Jahvé à être et à être seul véritablement Dieu, que l’homme est convoqué au témoignage: _‘_ Mais qui est semblable à moi? Qu’il se lève et parle, qu’il se révèle et argumente devant moi _’_ (Is 44:7). L’envoi est en même temps l’appel à la décision: _‘_ Vous êtes mes témoins, y a-t-il d’autre Dieu que moi? _’_ (Is 44:8). Le procès engagé par Jahvé avec les peuples et leurs idoles appelle une décision qui tranche,” ibid., 45. “Cette reprise du thème du procès à l’intérieur du thème de la confession-profession est, à mes yeux, la note majeure du concept prophétique du témoignage,” ibid.

[^c6-60]: “Le problème philosophique du témoignage, c’est le problème du témoignage de l’absolu, mieux: du témoignage absolu du l’absolu,” ibid., 35.

[^c6-61]: Cf. Chapter 6, notes 12ff.

[^c6-62]: Cf. G. Marcel, “Le témoignage comme localisation de l’existentiel,” _Nouvelle Revue Téologique_ 78 (1946): 182–91; “Le témoignage et l’épreuve, catégories existentielles privilégiées,” in _Atti del Congresso Internazionale di filosofia_ (Roma: Castellani, 1946), 15–20.

[^c6-63]: Cf. E. Lévinas, “Vérité du dévoilement et vérité du témoignage,” in Castelli, _La testimonianza_, 101–10. Lévinas speaks of witnessing to truth in terms of ethical responsibility.

[^c6-64]: Cf. J.-L. Marion, _Being Given: Toward a Phenomenology of Givenness_ (Stanford (CA): Stanford University Press, 2002). For a summary of Marion’s thought, cf. P. Martinelli, _La testimonianza_, 80–2; 93f.; F. Gaiffi, “La filosofia contemporanea e la testimonianza,” cit. The latter says: “costituito testimone, il soggetto resta al servizio della verità, ma non può pretendere di esserne il produttore.” Witnessing involves an encounter with what Marion calls “saturated phenomena,” experiences that exceed “our capacity for representation or control, such as divine revelation or love. Witnessing, therefore, does not involve a process of explaining or possessing, but of humbly receiving and responding to what is given.”

[p. 82]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p82}

seen above. And of course, the notion of person is deeply rooted in the process of anthropological self-understanding that stemmed from a living, thinking Christian faith, based especially on the doctrine of Christology and Trinity.[^c6-65]

Kierkegaard, a staunch defender of the category of witness, stated on one occasion: “the single individual (in Danish, _den Enkelt_)—with this category the cause of Christianity stands or falls. . . . Without this category pantheism would be unconditionally victorious. . . . One can promise to make a Christian of every individual one can get in under this category—yet insofar as one person cannot do this for another, more correctly, one can assure him that he will become that.”[^c6-66] And elsewhere: “There is a view of life that holds that truth is where the crowd is, that truth itself needs to have the crowd on its side,” says Kierkegaard. The “crowd” truth is just a form of popular, anonymous, consensus. “But,” he says, “‘the crowd’ is untruth.”[^c6-67] Clearly the center of the witnessing process is occupied by the human person in their individuality.

Gabriel Marcel, in his work _Te Mystery of Being,_ discusses the idea of human existence as a search for transcendent meaning. Witnessing, in this sense, involves a personal commitment to seeking and living out the truth, particularly the truth that is revealed in moments of deep personal connection, suffering, and transformation. Likewise, witnessing involves acts of faith and hope. Marcel concludes with the question: “is not the essence of man to be a being who can bear witness? May it not be that man is none other than a being who can give witness?”[^c6-68]

Interestingly, Joseph Ratzinger’s definition of truth moves in the same direction: “to perceive truth is a phenomenon that conforms man to being. It is a ‘coming together in one’ of the self and the world, it is agreement and consonance, it is being given and being purified.”[^c6-69] In this description, we find a widening of the classical definition of truth (_adaequatio rei et intellectus_) in a more personalist direction, which includes intellect, will, heart, and the entire person who is called to respond to and embrace the light of truth. And since the person is deeply involved in this process, witnessing becomes simply essential to the communication of truth.

### The Transcendence of Personhood over Nature

Perhaps the key issue that emerges in the foregoing discussion is that witnessing requires a notion of personhood that is, in some ways, above and beyond nature.

[^c6-65]: Cf. my studies “La persona umana tra filosofia e teologia,” _Annales Teologici_ 13 (1999): 71–105; “L’Europa e la speranza: tra promessa e ricordo. Riflessione intorno all’ _Ecclesia in Europa_,” _Path_ 4 (2005): 241–70; _Children of God in the World_, 552–67; _Faith Challenges Culture. A Reflection on the Dynamics of Modernity_ (Lanham: Rowman & Littlefield, 2021).

[^c6-66]: S. Kierkegaard, “The Point of View, Supplement,” 122f.

[^c6-67]: Ibid., 106.

[^c6-68]: G. Marcel, _Being and Having_ (The University Press: Glasgow 1949), 97.

[^c6-69]: J. Ratzinger, “Interpretation-Kontemplation-Aktion. Überlegungen zum Aufrag einer Katholischen Akademie,” _Communio (ed. tedesca)_ 12 (1983): 167–179, here 175.

[p. 83]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p83}

The individual person takes priority over the species. In fact, to be a “person” is not a simple attribute, among others, of human nature.[^c6-70] Rather we may say the following: an attribute belonging to the nature of the human individual (rationality, spirituality) is what identifies this human individual not only as an exemplar of the species but as an irreplaceable and eternal being, a person. Thomas Aquinas affirms specifically that “the person is that which is most perfect in the whole of nature.”[^c6-71]

By knowing the specific nature of humans (rational, corporeal, etc.) we can affirm that _that_ individual is a person. Still, being a person is not a simple “attribute” of human nature, for, as Robert Spaemann says, “Person and nature are realities that are simply incommensurable with one another.”[^c6-72] “Human beings have certain definite properties that license us to call them ‘persons’, but it is not the properties we call persons, but rather the human beings who possess the properties.”[^c6-73] At the same time, being a person is not strictly speaking dependent on human nature or its rationality, at least if we apply the Aristotelian notion of rationality (humans who share a rationality that is common to all).[^c6-74] Thomas Aquinas on the contrary retains always an individualized and personalized notion of rationality, as when he says, _Hic homo intelligit_, “this concrete man [person] thinks.”[^c6-75]

Using different language, Kierkegaard explains the special character of the person as a single individual in the following terms: “The paradox of faith is this, that the single individual is superior to the universal, and determines its relationship with the Absolute through its relationship with the Absolute; it does not determine its relationship with the Absolute through its relationship with the universal.”[^c6-76] That is to say, humans possess their singular dignity not as members of humanity, not because other humans recognize them as such, not on the basis of their rationality (which for all we know may be shared with everybody else), not because of their nature, but because they are creatures of God, made one by one “in his image and likeness” (Gn 1:27).

It should now be clear that any philosophy that systematically denies the value and centrality of the human person—more precisely, the subservience of person to nature—in philosophical discourse (we have seen this in Spinoza, Nietzsche, Vattimo, and others) will find insurmountable difficulties with the category of witnessing. Of course, the singularity of the human person finds its maximum expression in the divine person of Jesus Christ, who is, as we have seen, the supreme Witness, in and through whom Christians bear witness to Him and His eternal Father in the Spirit.[^c6-77]

[^c6-70]: Thomas Aquinas writes: “Forma significata per hoc nomen persona, non est essentia vel natura, sed personalitas” _S.T. I_, q. 39, a. 3, ad 4. See my study _Children of God in the World_, 561.

[^c6-71]: “Persona est id quod perfectissimum est in tota natura,” Thomas Aquinas, _S.T. I_, q. 29, a. 3, co.

[^c6-72]: R. Spaemann, _Das Natürliche und das Vernünfige: Aufsätze zur Anthropologie_ (München: Piper, 1987), 34. See also his work _Persons: the Difference between “Someone” and “Something”_ (Oxford; New York: Oxford University Press, 2006), especially 236–48.

[^c6-73]: Spaemann, _Persons_, 236.

[^c6-74]: See my study _Children of God in the World_, 43, note 41.

[^c6-75]: Thomas Aquinas, _S.T.I,_ q. 76, a. 1c.

[^c6-76]: S. Kierkegaard, “The Concept of Anxiety. A Simple Psychologically orienting Deliberation on the Dogmatic Issue of Hereditary Sin,” in _Kierkegaard’s Writings_, 8 (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 1980), prolegom. II.

[p. 85]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p85}

## Conclusion: Some Manifestations of Christian Witnessing {#ocallaghan.wtd.conclusion-some-manifestations-of-christian-witnessing}

Perhaps the key elements presented in this book can be summed up in a question and four words: truth, witness, conversion, and trust.

The question: what aspects of Christian evangelization are affected by the dynamics of witnessing? Teaching is teaching; preaching is preaching; beauty is beauty; rationality is rationality. All four are in a position to communicate fundamental elements of Christian revelation and salvation, to present a picture of what Christianity looks like, of what Christian culture and life should be. All four appeal to different human registers. They relate as it were to the _apprehensio_ or content of revealed truth, and respond to the question: what do Christians say and hold and teach and defend? But the intractable issue remains the following one: how do we know, each one of us personally, that the Christian teachings in question _are true?_ And how do we communicate this truth to others _as true_? We may _like_ religious doctrines, we may be moved or consoled by them, but they still may be false. Afer all, witnessing and Christian evangelization are not the same thing as mere publicity or advertisement.[^concl-1] Or we may well _dislike_ Christian doctrines, or feel indifferent before at least some of them, but they may well be true in that they are meant to challenge us. What assures us that these expressions correspond to reality? The thesis developed throughout this book is the following: for the purpose of ensuring veracity or truthfulness, words and doctrines and images must be accompanied side by side with real, living people who give _witness_ to other real living people, because this is ultimately what makes it possible for us to say “this is true,” this provides us with the _judicium_ of veracity, this is what it makes it possible for us to _convert_ to Christian faith, to commit our lives to Christianity, to hand on the faith. Witnessing involves not so much what Christians say or proclaim collectively, but rather what they do as persons, how much they are prepared to put their lives on the line, with a view to demonstrating the truth of what they teach. It is a _personal_ issue, or better, an interpersonal one. Truth is communicated by and to people. Several areas could be mentioned, although each one would require further development and explanation.[^concl-2]

[^concl-1]: Cf. G. Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 103ff., 106f.

[^concl-2]: For a further development of the interpersonal aspect of witnessing, cf. R. Fisichella, “Testimonianza, Parola e Nuova Evangelizzazione,” in Granados and O’Callaghan (eds.), _Parola e testimonianza_, 177–88.

[p. 86]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p86}

_Conclusion_

One key aspect of witnessing is that the word it accompanies _hits home_, leaves a mark, makes an impact. Words are not only informative, but also _performative_, they (should) achieve something, producing a change.[^concl-3] For Angelini, “Christian witness is destined by its own nature to provoke conflicts, because it brings into the light the quality of works done.”[^concl-4] Witnessing takes place in “the words that strike you.”[^concl-5] In that sense, words themselves give witness, for, if true, they encounter resonance and acceptance in the human mind and heart . . . the one who hears them says “this is true,” “I know it in my heart and soul.” We already cited Thomas Aquinas, who said: “Faith cannot precede intelligence universally, because man could not assent to what is proposed should he not have been able to understand it in some way.”[^concl-6] In other words, a certain, minimum, rational knowledge precedes faith. In this way, Aquinas excludes fideism, the leap of faith, the voluntaristic acceptance of what is simply and externally proclaimed by another. But still, the power of the word is meant to be communicated through witnessing. This may be done in many different ways: by the public proclamation of the truth in spite of popular pushback and rejection, by a joyfully lived celibate lifestyle, by a faithful and fruitful marriage, by striving to overcome intergenerational differences in the education process,[^concl-7] by Christian perseverance in the faith, by the Christian sanctification of demanding work, by living serenely through a serious illness, by consolidated conversion experiences,[^concl-8] by taking public vows of poverty, chastity, and obedience, by persevering Christian joy, by consolidated human virtues, by loyal and dedicated friendship, by works of literature, by authentically Christian institutions. And many others besides. These living realities are manifestations of witnessing, and bring us to be convinced that “this is true.”

All of these processes, sometimes perceived as judicial and even violent,[^concl-9] may well produce “pushback,” and even rejection and persecution (Mk 13:9). Yet within this very process not only is truth revealed, but so also is the person who gives witness. Avery Dulles puts it as follows: “To accept the testimony is to accept the witness as a person; to reject it, conversely, is to reject the witness as a person. When we bear witness to our religious faith we make an offer of friendship; we expose what is most intimate and vulnerable in ourselves, most subject to ridicule and rejection. Trustingly we invite others to enter into a personal communion of shared faith, a communion constituted by a network of interpersonal relations.”[^concl-10]

“The responsive listener,” Dulles continues, “out of love and respect for the person of the witness, will seek to enter the latter’s cognitive perspectives, to see

[^concl-4]: G. Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 14.

[^concl-5]: Ibid., 108.

[^concl-6]: “Fides non potest universaliter praecedere intellectum, non enim posset homo assentire credendo aliquibus propositis nisi ea aliqualiter intelligeret,” _S.T. II-II_, q. 8, a. 8 ad 2.

[^concl-7]: On the role of family in witnessing , cf. G. Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 19.

[^concl-8]: Cf. J. Alonso, “Le testimonianze dei convertiti: orizzonte significativo e valore teologico,” in Granados and O’Callaghan (eds.), _Parola e testimonianza_, 255–70.

[^concl-9]: Cf. G. Angelini, _La testimonianza,_ 108.

[^concl-10]: A. Dulles, “Fundamental Theology and the Dynamics of Conversion,” 187.

[p. 87]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p87}

_Conclusion_

the world from the witness’s point of view. Through empathy it will be possible for the hearer to imagine what reality must look like to the speaker, and this vision, once grasped, may seem far more attractive than anything the hearer could have conceived apart from this testimony.”[^concl-11] “The successful proclamation of Christianity does not require, in the first instance, a better theory of apologetics. It does require that Christians be seriously committed to their faith, so as to make the churches living and corporate signs of the presence of Christ in the world.”[^concl-12] That is, successful evangelization primarily requires witnessing.

Elsewhere Dulles says: “Personal testimony calls for an epistemology quite distinct from the scientific, as commonly understood. The scientist treats the datum to be investigated as a passive object to be mastered and brought within the investigator’s intellectual horizons. Interpretations proffered by others are not accepted on authority but are tested by critical probing. But when we proceed by testimony, the situation is very different. The event is an interpersonal encounter, in which the witness plays an active role, making an impact upon us. Without in any way compelling us to believe, the witness calls for a free assent that involves personal respect and trust. To reject the message is to withhold confidence in the witness. To accept it is a trusting submission to the witness’s authority. To the extent that we believe, we renounce our autonomy and willingly depend on the judgment of others.”[^concl-13]

Thus the dynamics of witnessing requires trust, the last of the four words mentioned at the outset. But it also produces trust. “In believing,” John Paul II says, “we entrust ourselves to the knowledge acquired by other people. This suggests an important tension. On the one hand, the knowledge acquired through belief can seem an imperfect form of knowledge, to be perfected gradually through personal accumulation of evidence; on the other hand, belief is ofen humanly richer than mere evidence, because it involves an interpersonal relationship and brings into play not only a person’s capacity to know but also the deeper capacity to entrust oneself to others, to enter into a relationship with them which is intimate and enduring.”[^concl-14]

We can end up with two inspiring texts. One St. Anthony of Padua, patron of lost things: “The man who is filled with the Holy Spirit speaks in different languages. These different languages are different ways of witnessing to Christ, such as humility, poverty, patience and obedience; we speak in those languages when we reveal in ourselves these virtues to others. Actions speak louder than words; let your words teach and your actions speak. We are full of words but

[^concl-11]: Ibid.

[^concl-12]: Ibid., 190.

[^concl-13]: A. Dulles, “The Rebirth of Apologetics,” 21.

[^concl-14]: John Paul II, Enc. _Fides et ratio_, n. 32.

[p. 88]{#ocallaghan.wtd.p88}

_Conclusion_

empty of actions, and therefore are cursed by the Lord, since he himself cursed the fig tree when he found no fruit but only leaves. Gregory the Great says: ‘A law is laid upon the preacher to practice what he preaches.’ It is useless for a man to flaunt his knowledge of the law if he undermines its teaching by his actions” (Sermon 1, 226).

The other is from an anonymous sermon of the second century: “Why is the Lord’s name blasphemed? Because we say one thing and do another. When they hear the words of God on our lips, unbelievers are amazed at their beauty and power, but when they see that those words have no effect in our lives, their admiration turns to scorn, and they dismiss such words as myths and fairy tales” (Sermon 13,3).

## About the Author {#ocallaghan.wtd.about-the-author}

**Paul O’Callaghan,** born in Dublin (1956), studied Electronic Engineering at University College Dublin (1979) and was ordained a Roman Catholic priest by St John Paul II (1982). He earned his doctorate in theology from the University of Navarre, Spain (1987). He has taught theology at the University of Navarre (1985–90), and from 1990 at the School of Theology of the Pontifical University of the Holy Cross (“Santa Croce”), Rome (Italy). He served as Vice-Rector and Spiritual Director at “Sedes Sapientiae” International Ecclesiastical College (1991–2020) in Rome, was Academic Vice-Rector at Santa Croce (1998–2000), and Dean of the Faculty of Theology (2000–2008). He is a member of the Council of the Pontifical Academy of Theology (2004–), Professor of Theological Anthropology (2004–), Director of the Department of Dogmatic Theology (2012–20) and of the “Centro di Formazione sacerdotale” (2016–20). He is on the Scientific Board of the several scientific journals. Prof. O’Callaghan is the author of ten books translated into several languages, and hundreds of articles on theological anthropology, eschatology, creation theology, Lutheran theology, spirituality, etc. His most recent books include: _Faith Challenges Culture. A Reflection on the Dynamics of Modernity_ (2021) and _God’s Gif of the Universe. An Introduction to Creation Teology_ (2022). For further details see http:// docenti.pusc.it/?u=callaghan.
